Why, when I was your age, young man, and after that too, I was glad to get $4 a week.”
“I don’t think my salary is under discussion, Mr. Pierce﻿—”
“*Brother* Pierce!” suggested Winch, in a half-shuckling undertone.
“Brother Pierce, then!” echoed Theron, impatiently. “The Quarterly Conference and the Estimating Committee deal with that. The trustees have no more to do with it than the man in the moon.”

===Chinese===

教会产业上的三笔抵押落在三位理事手里，这在塞隆不是新闻。可他再看一眼，这件事的又一面引起了他的好奇。
「我注意到利率不一样，」他脱口说，说过就想收回，因为那两位有关的理事在座位上不安地挪动起来。
「哦，那没什么，」伊拉斯塔斯·温奇嚷道，摆出大张旗鼓的热闹。「那不过是戈林奇弟兄给俺们的基金捐款的一种讨喜的小法子。您留意到，这几笔抵押立约的年月，州的法定利率都是七厘。后来降成了六厘。您瞧，事情一变，戈林奇弟兄不是表白入会的教友，不受俺们规程的约束，本可以顺顺当当把利息让下一分去。可皮尔斯弟兄同俺商量了，俺们认定俺们叫合同捆了个结实。您知道《规程》把『誓约必守到字』说得多么重。既是这样，俺们把不改亲手立下的字据看作本分。就是这么回事，韦尔弟兄。」
「我明白了，」塞隆说，竭力保持客气的平静。「还有——别的什么事么？」
「还有这个，」皮尔斯弟兄插进来：「俺们受命做守法的人，七厘*本来*是法，如今也还该是法，若不是州议会里那伙破衣烂衫——」
「这一层我们当然不必深究了，」牧师打断他，自觉脊梁里道德的骨头正在硬起来。「我们还有别的议程么？」
皮尔斯弟兄的小眼睛一爆火星，额上的皱纹怒冲冲地加深了。「议程？」他质问。「有，多着呢。俺们得省。这会儿已经短了快300美元。除开您的房钱，您净拿800美元——合一星期15.38美元，就养您同您太太两张嘴。嗐，俺像您这岁数、还往后好些年的岁数，一礼拜挣4块钱就喜得了不得。」
「我想我的薪俸不在讨论之列，皮尔斯先生——」
「*皮尔斯弟兄*！」温奇半吃吃笑着低声提示。
「那就皮尔斯弟兄！」塞隆不耐烦地应声。「薪俸归季议会和核薪委员会管。理事们管得着它，不比月亮上那位先生多。」

===Original===

“Come, come, Brother Ware,” put in Erastus Winch, “we mustn’t have no hard feelin’s. Brotherly love is what we’re all lookin’ after. Brother Pierce’s meanin’ wasn’t agin your drawin’ your full salary, every cent of it, only﻿—only there are certain little things connected with the parsonage here that we feel you ought to bear. F’r instance, there’s the new sidewalk we had to lay in front of the house here only a month ago. Of course, if the treasury was flush we wouldn’t say a word about it. An’ then there’s the gas bill here. Seein’ as you get your rent for nothin’, it don’t seem much to ask that you should see to lightin’ the place yourself.”
“No, I don’t think that either is a proper charge upon me,” interposed Theron. “I decline to pay them.”
“We can have the gas shut off,” remarked Brother Pierce, coldly.
“As soon as you like,” responded the minister, sitting erect and tapping the carpet nervously with his foot. “Only you must understand that I will take the whole matter to the Quarterly Conference in July. I already see a good many other interesting questions about the financial management of this church which might be appropriately discussed there.”
“Oh, come, Brother Ware!” broke in Trustee Winch, with a somewhat agitated assumption of good-feeling. “Surely these are matters we ought to settle amongst ourselves. We never yet asked outsiders to meddle with our business here. It’s our motto, Brother Ware. I say, if you’ve got a motto, stand by it.”
“Well, my motto,” said Theron, “is to be behaved decently to by those with whom I have to deal; and I also propose to stand by it.”
Brother Pierce rose gingerly to his feet, with the hesitation of an old man not sure about his knees. When he had straightened himself, he put on his hat, and eyed the minister sternly from beneath its brim.
“The Lord gives us crosses grievous to our natur’,” he said, “an’ we’re told to bear ’em cheerfully as long as they’re on our backs; but there ain’t nothin’ said agin our unloadin’ ’em in the ditch the minute we git the chance. I guess you won’t last here more ’n a twelvemonth.”
He pulled his soft and discolored old hat down over his brows with a significantly hostile nod, and, turning, stumped toward the hall-door without offering to shake hands.
The other trustees had risen likewise, in tacit recognition that the meeting was over.

===Chinese===

「行了，行了，韦尔弟兄，」伊拉斯塔斯·温奇打圆场，「俺们可不能伤和气。弟兄相爱才是俺们大家求的。皮尔斯弟兄的意思不是不让您全薪照领，一个子儿不少，只是——只是牧师住宅这儿有几样小小的开销，俺们觉得该您担待。比如，一个月前俺们才在房前铺了那条新人行道。当然，库里有富余，俺们一个字也不提。还有这儿的煤气账。您既白住着房子，请您自己照管这里的灯火，也不算多要求吧。」
「不，这两样我都不认为该记在我账上，」塞隆插话说。「我拒付。」
「俺们可以叫停煤气，」皮尔斯弟兄冷冷地说。
「随你们的便，」牧师应道，坐得笔直，一只脚紧张地敲着地毯。「只是你们须知，七月我要把整桩事提到季议会上去。财务管理的这一面，我已经看出好些别的一样有意思的问题，到时候一并议论倒也合适。」
「哦，别介，韦尔弟兄！」温奇理事赶忙插进来，好脾气的架势里透着几分慌乱。「这些事总该俺们自己了断。俺们从来没有请外人插手过俺们这儿的事务。这是俺们的座右铭，韦尔弟兄。俺说，人有了座右铭，就得守着。」
「好，我的座右铭，」塞隆说，「是凡同我打交道的人，待我要有体面；我也打算守住它。」
皮尔斯弟兄小心翼翼地站起身，带着老人对自己膝盖没有把握的迟疑。他站直了，戴上帽子，从帽檐底下严厉地睨着牧师。
「主给了俺们背不动的十字架，」他说，「吩咐俺们背在背上的时候要甘心；可主没说过一句，不许俺们一得机会就把它们卸进沟里。俺料您在这儿撑不过一年。」
他把那顶发软变色的旧帽往眉毛上一磕，意味深长地怀敌意一点头，转身朝门厅蹒去，也没有伸手握别。
另两位理事也站了起来，心照不宣地承认会已开完。

===Original===

Winch clasped the minister’s hand in his own broad, hard palm, and squeezed it in an exuberant grip. “Don’t mind his little ways, Brother Ware,” he urged in a loud, unctuous whisper, with a grinning backward nod: “he’s a trifle skittish sometimes when you don’t give him free rein; but he’s all wool an’ a yard wide when it comes to right-down hardpan religion. My love to Sister Ware;” and he followed the senior trustee into the hall.
Mr. Gorringe had been tying up his books and papers. He came now with the bulky parcel under his arm, and his hat and stick in the other hand. He could give little but his thumb to Theron to shake. His face wore a grave expression, and not a line relaxed as, catching the minister’s look, he slowly covered his left eye in a deliberate wink.
---
“Well?﻿—and how did it go off?” asked Alice, from where she knelt by the oven door, a few minutes later.
For answer, Theron threw himself wearily into the big old farm rocking-chair on the other side of the stove, and shook his head with a lengthened sigh.
“If it wasn’t for that man Gorringe of yours,” he said dejectedly, “I think *I* should feel like going off﻿—and learning a trade.”

===Chinese===

温奇用他自己宽大坚硬的手掌裹住牧师的手，兴冲冲一攥。「别理会他这些小脾气，韦尔弟兄，」他大声而油润地低语，回头咧嘴一点：「有时您不撒开缰绳，他有点儿炮蹶子；可论到板板正正、扎扎实实的信仰，他是整幅的羊毛、足一码宽。替俺问韦尔师母好；」说罢随着首席理事进了门厅。
戈林奇先生早把他的账簿文书捆好，此刻夹着鼓鼓的一包走来，另一只手拿着帽子和手杖。他只剩一根大拇指能给塞隆握。他脸上是一副凝重的表情；接住牧师的目光时，他缓缓地、郑重地捂住左眼眨了一眨，脸上没有一条纹松劲。
---
「怎么样？——开得如何？」几分钟后，艾丽斯（Alice）跪在烤箱门前问。
塞隆的回答，是把自己疲惫地掼进炉子那一头那把农家旧摇椅里，拖着长音叹了一声，摇头。
「要不是你那位戈林奇，」他沮丧地说，「我想*我*真想一走了之——去学一门手艺。」

### IV / 四
===Original===

On the following Sunday, young Mrs. Ware sat alone in the preacher’s pew through the morning service, and everybody noted that the roses had been taken from her bonnet. In the evening she was absent, and after the doxology and benediction several people, under the pretence of solicitude for her health, tried to pump her husband as to the reason. He answered their inquiries civilly enough, but with brevity: she had stayed at home because she did not feel like coming out﻿—this and nothing more.
The congregation dispersed under a gossip-laden cloud of consciousness that there must be something queer about Sister Ware. There was a tolerably general agreement, however, that the two sermons of the day had been excellent. Not even Loren Pierce’s railing commentary on the pastor’s introduction of an outlandish word like “epitome”﻿—clearly forbidden by the Discipline’s injunction to plain language understood of the people﻿—availed to sap the satisfaction of the majority.
Theron himself comprehended that he had pleased the bulk of his auditors; the knowledge left him curiously hot and cold. On the one hand, there was joy in the apparent prospect that the congregation would back him up in a stand against the trustees, if worst came to worst. But, on the other hand, the bonnet episode entered his soul. It had been a source of bitter humiliation to him to see his wife sitting there beneath the pulpit, shorn by despotic order of the adornments natural to her pretty head. But he had even greater pain in contemplating the effect it had produced on Alice herself. She had said not a word on the subject, but her every glance and gesture seemed to him eloquent of deep feeling about it. He made sure that she blamed him for having defended his own gas and sidewalk rights with successful vigor, but permitted the sacrifice of her poor little inoffensive roses without a protest. In this view of the matter, indeed, he blamed himself. Was it too late to make the error good? He ventured a hint on this Sunday evening, when he returned to the parsonage and found her reading an old weekly newspaper by the light of the kitchen lamp, to the effect that he fancied there would be no great danger in putting those roses back into her bonnet. Without lifting her eyes from the paper, she answered that she had no earthly desire to wear roses in her bonnet, and went on with her reading.

===Chinese===

第二个礼拜天，年轻的韦尔太太独自坐在传道人的专席上听完了晨间礼拜，人人都注意到，她帽子上的玫瑰花没有了。晚礼拜她没有来；颂荣和祝福之后，有几个人借着问候她身体的由头，想从她丈夫嘴里探出缘故。他对这些探问答得还算客气，却只有一句：她留在家，是因为不想出门——如此而已，再无别话。
会众散去时，心头罩着一团塞满闲话的疑云：韦尔姊妹身上必有蹊跷。不过，这天的两篇讲道都极精彩，倒是大体公认的。连洛伦·皮尔斯的那通牢骚也无损多数人的满意——他抱怨牧师竟用出“缩影”（epitome）这样文绉绉的怪词，说教规明令要用人人听得懂的平白语言。
塞隆自己也明白，他讨得了听众中大半的欢心；这认知叫他忽冷忽热，滋味古怪。一方面有喜：眼看事态万一逼到绝地，会众是会撑他对抗理事们的。可另一方面，帽子事件钻进了他的灵魂。看见妻子坐在讲坛底下，头上天然的妆饰被一道专制命令剪除，这于他是苦涩的屈辱。可他看着这事在艾丽斯本人身上造成的后果，还更痛心。这件事她一字不提，可在他看来，她的每一瞥、每一个姿势都在诉说深沉的心绪。他认定了：她怪他为自己的煤气权和人行道权争得雷厉风行，却让她那几朵可怜无辜的玫瑰做了牺牲，一声不吭。这样看问题，他其实也在怪自己。这错误还补救得了么？礼拜天晚上回到牧师宅，见她在厨房灯下读一份旧周报，他斗胆暗示了一句：依他想，把那些玫瑰放回帽子上，怕也没什么大风险。她眼睛不离报纸，回答说，她压根没有戴玫瑰花的意思，又接着读她的报。

===Original===

At breakfast the next morning Theron found himself in command of an unusual fund of humorous good spirits, and was at pains to make the most of it, passing whimsical comments on subjects which the opening day suggested, recalling quaint and comical memories of the past, and striving his best to force Alice into a laugh. Formerly her merry temper had always ignited at the merest spark of gayety. Now she gave his jokes only a dutiful half-smile, and uttered scarcely a word in response to his running fire of talk. When the meal was finished, she went silently to work to clear away the dishes.
Theron turned over in his mind the project of offering to help her, as he had done so often in those dear old days when they laughingly began life together. Something decided this project in the negative for him, and after lingering moments he put on his hat and went out for a walk.
Not even the most doleful and trying hour of his bitter experience in Tyre had depressed him like this. Looking back upon these past troubles, he persuaded himself that he had borne them all with a light and cheerful heart, simply because Alice had been one with him in every thought and emotion. How perfect, how ideally complete, their sympathy had always been! With what absolute unity of mind and soul they had trod that difficult path together! And now﻿—henceforth﻿—was it to be different? The mere suggestion of such a thing chilled his veins. He said aloud to himself as he walked that life would be an intolerable curse if Alice were to cease sharing it with him in every conceivable phase.
He had made his way out of town, and tramped along the country hill-road for a considerable distance, before a merciful light began to lessen the shadows in the picture of gloom with which his mind tortured itself. All at once he stopped short, lifted his head, and looked about him. The broad valley lay warm and tranquil in the May sunshine at his feet. In the thicket up the side-hill above him a gray squirrel was chattering shrilly, and the birds sang in a tireless choral confusion. Theron smiled, and drew a long breath. The gay clamor of the woodland songsters, the placid radiance of the landscape, were suddenly taken in and made a part of his new mood. He listened, smiled once more, and then started in a leisurely way back toward Octavius.

===Chinese===

第二天早餐时，塞隆发觉自己存了一注反常的诙谐好兴致，便着意把它用到十足：就这个星期的头一天所提示的题目，说些奇思妙语，翻出旧日古怪滑稽的回忆，竭力要逗艾丽斯笑。从前，只要一星快乐的火花，她那快活的性子总能点燃。如今她对他的笑话只尽本分地半笑一笑，对他连珠的谈锋几乎一字不答。饭一吃完，她便默默动手收拾碗碟。
塞隆心里转着帮她一把的念头——在那笑呵呵共同开场的亲爱的旧日子里，他常这么做的。有什么东西替他把这念头否决了；磨蹭了几分钟，他戴上帽子出门散步去了。
在泰尔那些最凄苦、最难熬的时辰里，他也没有这样消沉过。回想那些旧日的患难，他说服自己：当初他能怀着轻快的心一概担下来，全因为每一念、每一情，艾丽斯都同他一心。他们的默契向来何等完美、何等理想地周全！他们曾以何等浑然一体的心神，同走那条难路！而如今——从此以后——难道要变了么？单是这念头一丝闪过，他的血脉就凉了。他边走边对自己大声说，倘若艾丽斯不再同他分享生活的每一个角落，人生便是无法忍受的诅咒。
他出了镇，沿着乡间的山路走了好一程，一线慈悲的光才开始淡化他心中那幅自苦的阴郁图景。蓦地他收住脚，抬起头，环顾四周。宽阔的谷地卧在他脚下，在五月的阳光里温煦而安宁。上方山坡的树丛里，一只灰松鼠尖声聒噪，鸟雀们唱成一支不知疲倦的混杂合唱。塞隆微笑了，深深吸了一口气。林间歌者的喧闹，风景的安详光辉，霎时间被他一并纳受，化为他新心境的一部分。他听着，又笑了一笑，随后从容起步，往奥克塔维乌斯回去。

===Original===

How could he have been so ridiculous as to fancy that Alice﻿—his Alice﻿—had been changed into someone else? He marvelled now at his own perverse folly. She was overworked﻿—tired out﻿—that was all. The task of moving in, of setting the new household to rights, had been too much for her. She must have a rest. They must get in a hired girl.
Once this decision about a servant fixed itself in the young minister’s mind, it drove out the last vestage of discomfort. He strode along now in great content, revolving idly a dozen different plans for gilding and beautifying this new life of leisure into which his sanguine thoughts projected Alice. One of these particularly pleased him, and waxed in definiteness as he turned it over and over. He would get another piano for her, in place of that which had been sacrificed in Tyre. That beneficient modern invention, the instalment plan, made this quite feasible﻿—so easy, in fact, that it almost seemed as if he should find his wife playing on the new instrument when he got home. He would stop in at the music store and see about it that very day.
Of course, now that these important resolutions had been taken, it would be a good thing if he could do something to bring in some extra money. This was by no means a new notion. He had mused over the possibility in a formless way ever since that memorable discovery of indebtedness in Tyre, and had long ago recognized the hopelessness of endeavor in every channel save that of literature. Latterly his fancy had been stimulated by reading an account of the profits which Canon Farrar had derived from his *Life of Christ*. If such a book could command such a bewildering multitude of readers, Theron felt there ought to be a chance for him. So clear did constant rumination render this assumption that the young pastor in time had come to regard this prospective book of his as a substantial asset, which could be realized without trouble whenever he got around to it.
He had not, it is true, gone to the length of seriously considering what should be the subject of his book. That had not seemed to him to matter much, so long as it was scriptural. Familiarity with the process of extracting a fixed amount of spiritual and intellectual meat from any casual text, week after week, had given him an idea that any one of many subjects would do, when the time came for him to make a choice.

===Chinese===

他怎么会荒唐到以为艾丽斯——他的艾丽斯——变成了另一个人？他此刻惊异于自己的乖僻痴愚。她是劳累过度——累坏了——仅此而已。搬场安家、收拾新居的活计，于她太重了。她必须歇一歇。他们必须雇个姑娘。
关于雇人的决定一经在年轻牧师心里坐实，便把最后一丝不快也驱逐出去了。他此刻大步走着，满心舒泰，闲闲盘算着一打各式各样的计划，要把艾丽斯——他乐观的憧憬正把她投进的那份闲适新生活——镀金添彩。其中有一个格外中他的意，越拣量越分明。他要给她再买一架钢琴，顶替泰尔牺牲掉的那架。分期付款这一造福现代的发明，使这事完全可行——实在太容易了，容易得仿佛他到家时就能看见妻子已经在新琴上弹起来了。今天他就顺路进乐器行去问一问。
当然啰，这些重大的决定既已下定，他若能再想点法子挣些外快，岂不更好。这绝不是新念头。自打在泰尔那场难忘的负债发现以来，他早就在无形无状地盘算着这种可能，并且早就认清：除了文学一道，条条路都是死路。新近他读到一段记述，说法拉尔尊牧凭他的《基督传》得了多少进项，幻想更受了刺激。倘若这样一本书能赢得这样茫茫多端的读者，塞隆觉得，自己也该有机会。反复的咀嚼把这假设越嚼越亮，年轻的牧师到后来竟把这部未来之书看作一笔实在的资产，什么时候腾出手来，什么时候便能毫不费事地变现。
诚然，他还从来没有认真想过，他的书该写什么题目。这一点在他看来无关紧要，只要合乎圣经便是。每星期从随手拈来的一段经文里榨出定量灵粮和智粮的长年功夫，给了他一个想法：真到了选择的时候，许多题目里随便哪一个都行。

===Original===

He realized now that the time for a selection had arrived, and almost simultaneously found himself with a ready-made decision in his mind. The book should be about Abraham!
Theron Ware was extremely interested in the mechanism of his own brain, and followed its workings with a lively curiosity. Nothing could be more remarkable, he thought, than to thus discover that, on the instant of his formulating a desire to know what he should write upon, lo, and behold! there his mind, quite on its own initiative, had the answer waiting for him! When he had gone a little further, and the powerful range of possibilities in the son’s revolt against the idolatry of his father, the image-maker, in the exodus from the unholy city of Ur, and in the influence of the new nomadic life upon the little deistic family group, had begun to unfold itself before him, he felt that the hand of Providence was plainly discernible in the matter. The book was to be blessed from its very inception.
Walking homeward briskly now, with his eyes on the sidewalk and his mind all aglow with crowding suggestions for the new work, and impatience to be at it, he came abruptly upon a group of men and boys who occupied the whole path, and were moving forward so noiselessly that he had not heard them coming. He almost ran into the leader of this little procession, and began a stammering apology, the final words of which were left unspoken, so solemnly heedless of him and his talk were all the faces he saw.
In the centre of the group were four workingmen, bearing between them an extemporized litter of two poles and a blanket hastily secured across them with spikes. Most of what this litter held was covered by another blanket, rounded in coarse folds over a shapeless bulk. From beneath its farther end protruded a big broom-like black beard, thrown upward at such an angle as to hide everything beyond to those in front. The tall young minister, stepping aside and standing tiptoe, could see sloping downward behind this hedge of beard a pinched and chalk-like face, with wide-open, staring eyes. Its lips, of a dull lilac hue, were moving ceaselessly, and made a dry, clicking sound.
Theron instinctively joined himself to those who followed the litter﻿—a motley dozen of street idlers, chiefly boys.

===Chinese===

他此刻意识到，选择的时刻到了，而且几乎同时发现自己心里已有一个现成的决断。这本书就写亚伯拉罕！
塞隆·韦尔对自己头脑的机制极感兴趣，总怀着活泼的好奇追看它的运转。还有什么比这更妙的呢——他刚一立意要知道该写什么，你瞧！他的头脑，全然自作主张，已经把答案备在那里了！再往前走一步，当种种雄浑的可能在他面前铺展开来——儿子对造像的父亲那偶像崇拜的反叛，出离不圣之城吾珥的大迁徙，新的游牧生活对那个小小的一神家族的影响——他感到此事之中，天意历历可辨。这本书自怀胎之日起便蒙福泽。
此刻他快步往家走，眼睛望着人行道，满心为新作涌起的种种设想烧得通亮，急不可待要动手，却冷不丁撞上一群男人和孩子，占满了整条路，走得这样悄无声息，他竟没有听见他们来。他几乎撞上这支小小行列的首领，讷讷地道起歉来；道歉的后半截没说出口，因为他看见的那些面孔，全都庄严地无视着他和他的话。
人群中央是四个工人，抬着一副临时拼成的担架——两根木杆，一条毯子用钉子草草固定在中间。担架上载的东西大半又盖着一条毯子，粗粝的皱褶罩住一堆不成形状的重物。从毯子远端底下翘出一把扫帚似的黑大胡子，翘起的角度恰好把后面的东西全遮住了，叫前面的人看不见。高个子的年轻牧师侧身踮脚，才望见这排胡须的斜后面有一张瘪陷的、粉笔似的脸，眼睛瞪得大大的。那两片暗丁香色的嘴唇动个不停，发出干燥的、嗒嗒的声响。
塞隆不由自主地汇入了跟随担架的人们——街头闲人凑成的杂色一打，多半是孩子。

===Original===

One of these in whispers explained to him that the man was one of Jerry Madden’s workmen in the wagon-shops, who had been deployed to trim an elm-tree in front of his employer’s house, and, being unused to such work, had fallen from the top and broken all his bones. They would have cared for him at Madden’s house, but he had insisted upon being taken home. His name was MacEvoy, and he was Joey MacEvoy’s father, and likewise Jim’s and Hughey’s and Martin’s. After a pause the lad, a bright-eyed, freckled, barefooted wee Irishman, volunteered the further information that his big brother had run to bring “Father Forbess,” on the chance that he might be in time to administer “extry munction.”
The way of the silent little procession led through back streets﻿—where women hanging up clothes in the yards hurried to the gates, their aprons full of clothespins, to stare open-mouthed at the passersby﻿—and came to a halt at last in an irregular and muddy lane, before one of a half dozen shanties reared among the ash-heaps and debris of the town’s most bedraggled outskirts.
A stout, middle-aged, red-armed woman, already warned by some messenger of calamity, stood waiting on the roadside bank. There were whimpering children clinging to her skirts, and a surrounding cluster of women of the neighborhood, some of the more elderly of whom, shrivelled little crones in tidy caps, and with their aprons to their eyes, were beginning in a low-murmured minor the wail which presently should rise into the keen of death. Mrs. MacEvoy herself made no moan, and her broad ruddy face was stern in expression rather than sorrowful. When the litter stopped beside her, she laid a hand for an instant on her husband’s wet brow, and looked﻿—one could have sworn impassively﻿—into his staring eyes. Then, still without a word, she waved the bearers toward the door, and led the way herself.
Theron, somewhat wonderingly, found himself, a minute later, inside a dark and ill-smelling room, the air of which was humid with the steam from a boiler of clothes on the stove, and not in other ways improved by the presence of a jostling score of women, all straining their gaze upon the open door of the only other apartment﻿—the bedchamber.

===Chinese===

其中一个孩子压低嗓门向他解释：这人是杰里·马登车马作坊的工人，被派去修剪雇主家门前的一棵榆树，因为不惯这活计，从树顶上摔下来，浑身骨头都断了。本来马登家要照料他的，可他执意要回家。他姓麦克埃沃伊，是乔伊·麦克埃沃伊的父亲，也是吉姆的、休吉的和马丁的父亲。顿了一顿，那孩子——一个眼睛亮亮、长着雀斑、光脚的小爱尔兰人——又自动补充说，他哥哥跑去请“福布斯神父”了，指望赶得及给他行“临终圣油”。
这支不出声的小小行列取道后街——院子里晾衣裳的女人们围裙兜满衣夹，勿勿赶到门口，张着嘴呆望过路人——末了在一条歪歪斜斜的泥巷里停下，停在半打窝棚中的一座前面；这一带窝棚，扎在全镇最褴褛的边上，灰堆和垃圾中间。
一个健壮的、红胳膊的中年妇人，已由某位凶信的使者预先告知，立在路边的土埂上等着。孩子们哭哭唧唧地扯着她的裙子，四围聚着一群街坊妇人，其中年纪大些的——干瘪的小老太婆，戴着整洁的便帽——已经把围裙掩上眼睛，用低低的小调起了那哀声，不一刻便要升作死别的恸哭。麦克埃沃伊太太自己一声不哼，她那张宽大的红脸膛，与其说悲哀，不如说严峻。担架在她身边停住时，她把一只手在丈夫湿漉漉的额上按了一瞬，朝他瞪着的眼睛望了望——你几乎可以赌咒说，那是无动于衷的一望。随后她仍旧一言不发，朝门口挥了挥抬担架的人，自己头前引路。
塞隆几分恍惚地发觉，一分钟后自己已置身一间昏暗而气味难闻的屋子：炉上煮着衣服，满屋蒸腾着水汽；挤挤挨挨二十来个妇人，也并不使空气改善什么——个个伸长脖子，只盯着唯一那间里屋的房门——卧室的门。

===Original===

Through this they could see the workmen laying MacEvoy on the bed, and standing awkwardly about thereafter, getting in the way of the wife and old Maggie Quirk as they strove to remove the garments from his crushed limbs. As the neighbors watched what could be seen of these proceedings, they whispered among themselves eulogies of the injured man’s industry and good temper, his habit of bringing his money home to his wife, and the way he kept his Father Mathew pledge and attended to his religious duties. They admitted freely that, by the light of his example, their own husbands and sons left much to be desired, and from this wandered easily off into domestic digressions of their own. But all the while their eyes were bent upon the bedroom door; and Theron made out, after he had grown accustomed to the gloom and the smell, that many of them were telling their beads even while they kept the muttered conversation alive. None of them paid any attention to him, or seemed to regard his presence there as unusual.
Presently he saw enter through the sunlit street doorway a person of a different class. The bright light shone for a passing instant upon a fashionable, flowered hat, and upon some remarkably brilliant shade of red hair beneath it. In another moment there had edged along through the throng, to almost within touch of him, a tall young woman, the owner of this hat and wonderful hair. She was clad in light and pleasing spring attire, and carried a parasol with a long oxidized silver handle of a quaint pattern. She looked at him, and he saw that her face was of a lengthened oval, with a luminous rose-tinted skin, full red lips, and big brown, frank eyes with heavy auburn lashes. She made a grave little inclination of her head toward him, and he bowed in response. Since her arrival, he noted, the chattering of the others had entirely ceased.
“I followed the others in, in the hope that I might be of some assistance,” he ventured to explain to her in a low murmur, feeling that at last here was someone to whom an explanation of his presence in this Romish house was due. “I hope they won’t feel that I have intruded.”
She nodded her head as if she quite understood. “They’ll take the will for the deed,” she whispered back. “Father Forbes will be here in a minute. Do you know is it too late?”

===Chinese===

透过房门，他们看见工人们把麦克埃沃伊放上床，随后尴尬地站在旁边，碍手碍脚，挡着妻子和老玛吉·夸克，她们正使劲从他那压碎的肢体上褪衣裳。邻妇们望着这番手续的可见部分，交头接耳地称道伤者的勤勉和好脾气，称道他把工钱如数交给老婆的习惯，称道他守着马修神父的戒酒誓、尽着教门的本分。她们坦然承认，照他的榜样一照，自家的丈夫儿子大有欠缺；由此又轻巧地荡开去，各自数说起自家的家务。可她们的眼睛始终钉在卧室门上；塞隆等眼睛适应了昏暗和气味之后，看出来她们许多人一面维持着咕咕哝哝的谈话，一面还在捡念珠。没有一个人理会他，也没有人觉得他在此地有什么反常。
不多时，他望见一个不同流品的人从亮堂堂的街门进来。亮光一闪，照出一顶时兴的花帽，和帽子底下一头红得惊人的头发。转眼间，一位高挑的年轻女子已侧着身从人堆里蹭过来，蹭到离他几乎伸手可及的地方——正是这顶帽子和这头奇发的主人。她一身轻盈悦目的春装，手里一把阳伞，长长的乌银伞柄，式样古雅。她望了望他；他看见一张长卵形的脸，皮肤透着明亮的玫瑰色，嘴唇丰红，一双棕色的大眼睛坦率有神，衬着浓重的金褐睫毛。她朝他微微郑重地一颜首，他鞠躬作答。他注意到，自她进门，旁人的嘁喳便全然停了。
“我跟着大家进来，指望能帮上点什么忙，”他压低声音向她解释，觉得这罗马教人家里的在场，总算有了一个该向之交代的人。“希望他们不至于觉得我冒昧。”
她点点头，仿佛全然会意。“他们会领你的心意的，”她低声回敬，“福布斯神父一会儿就到。您知道是不是已经晚了？”

===Original===

Even as she spoke, the outer doorway was darkened by the commanding bulk of a newcomer’s figure. The flash of a silk hat, and the deferential way in which the assembled neighbors fell back to clear a passage, made his identity clear. Theron felt his blood tingle in an unaccustomed way as this priest of a strange church advanced across the room﻿—a broad-shouldered, portly man of more than middle height, with a shapely, strong-lined face of almost waxen pallor, and a firm, commanding tread. He carried in his hands, besides his hat, a small leather-bound case. To this and to him the women courtesied and bowed their heads as he passed.
“Come with me,” whispered the tall girl with the parasol to Theron; and he found himself pushing along in her wake until they intercepted the priest just outside the bedroom door. She touched Father Forbes on the arm.
“Just to tell you that I am here,” she said. The priest nodded with a grave face, and passed into the other room. In a minute or two the workmen, Mrs. MacEvoy, and her helper came out, and the door was shut behind them.
“He is making his confession,” explained the young lady. “Stay here for a minute.”
She moved over to where the woman of the house stood, glum-faced and tearless, and whispered something to her. A confused movement among the crowd followed, and out of it presently resulted a small table, covered with a white cloth, and bearing on it two unlighted candles, a basin of water, and a spoon, which was brought forward and placed in readiness before the closed door. Some of those nearest this cleared space were kneeling now, and murmuring a low buzz of prayer to the click of beads on their rosaries.
The door opened, and Theron saw the priest standing in the doorway with an uplifted hand. He wore now a surplice, with a purple band over his shoulders, and on his pale face there shone a tranquil and tender light.
One of the workmen fetched from the stove a brand, lighted the two candles, and bore the table with its contents into the bedroom. The young woman plucked Theron’s sleeve, and he dumbly followed her into the chamber of death, making one of the group of a dozen, headed by Mrs. MacEvoy and her children, which filled the little room, and overflowed now outward to the street door.

===Chinese===

话音未落，外面的门口便被一位新来者的赫赫身躯暗了下去。大礼帽一闪，加上聚着的邻妇们恭敬地退开让路的样子，他的身份便明白了。这位异教会堂的神父穿过屋子向里走，塞隆感到自己的血以一种陌生的样子发烫——一个宽肩膀、敦实的男子，身量过中人，一张轮廓俊秀刚劲、几乎蜡白的脸，脚步坚定而威严。他手里除帽子外，还捧着一只小皮匣。经过时，妇人们向他屈膝、低头。
“跟我来。”拿阳伞的高挑姑娘对塞隆悄声说；他发觉自己随着她挤过去，恰在卧室门外截住了神父。她在福布斯神父臂上碰了碰。
“只告诉您一声，我在这儿。”她说。神父面色庄重地点点头，进了里屋。一两分钟后，工人们、麦克埃沃伊太太和帮手走了出来，门在他们身后关上了。
“他在办告解，”年轻的小姐解释道，“请在这儿稍候。”
她挪到主妇站立的地方——那张着脸、无泪的主妇——对她耳语了几句。人群中随即起了一阵忙乱，不多时便变出一张小桌，铺上白布，摆上两支未点的蜡烛、一盆水、一把匙，抬到关着的门前放好待用。离这片清出的空当最近的几个人已经跪下，念珠嗒嗒地捻着，嗡嗡地低声祷告。
门开了，塞隆看见神父立在门口，举起一只手。他此刻披着白罩袍，肩上搭一条紫带，苍白的脸上放着一种安详而温柔的光。
一个工人从炉边取来一根柴火，点着两支蜡烛，端起桌子和桌上的物件进了卧室。年轻女子拽了拽塞隆的衣袖，他懵懵地随她走进这间临终之室，加入以麦克埃沃伊太太和她的孩子们为首的十来个人的一群；这群人挤满了小屋，又向外漫到街门口。

===Original===

He found himself bowing with the others to receive the sprinkled holy water from the priest’s white fingers; kneeling with the others for the prayers; following in impressed silence with the others the strange ceremonial by which the priest traced crosses of holy oil with his thumb upon the eyes, ears, nostrils, lips, hands, and feet of the dying man, wiping off the oil with a piece of cotton-batting each time after he had repeated the invocation to forgiveness for that particular sense. But most of all he was moved by the rich, novel sound of the Latin as the priest rolled it forth in the “Asperges Me, Domine,” and “Misereatur Vestri Omnipotens Deus,” with its soft Continental vowels and liquid *r*’s. It seemed to him that he had never really heard Latin before. Then the astonishing young woman with the red hair declaimed the Confiteor, vigorously and with a resonant distinctness of enunciation. It was a different Latin, harsher and more sonorous; and while it still dominated the murmured undertone of the other’s prayers, the last moment came.
Theron had stood face to face with death at many other bedsides; no other final scene had stirred him like this. It must have been the girl’s Latin chant, with its clanging reiteration of the great names﻿—*beatum Michaelem Archangelum, beatum Joannem Baptistam, sanctos Apostolos Petrum et Paulum*﻿—invoked with such proud confidence in this squalid little shanty, which so strangely affected him.
He came out with the others at last﻿—the candles and the folded hands over the crucifix left behind﻿—and walked as one in a dream. Even by the time that he had gained the outer doorway, and stood blinking at the bright light and filling his lungs with honest air once more, it had begun to seem incredible to him that he had seen and done all this.

===Chinese===

他发觉自己随众鞠躬，领受神父白皙手指洒下的圣水；随众跪下祈祷；随众在慑服的静默中，看那古怪的仪节——神父用拇指蘸圣油，在垂死者的眼睛、耳朵、鼻孔、嘴唇、双手和双脚上画十字，每对着一种官能念完赦罪祷文，便用一小块药棉把油拭去。而最最打动他的，是神父诵出拉丁文时那丰润新奇的声音——“Asperges Me, Domine”“Misereatur Vestri Omnipotens Deus”——那柔软的欧陆元音和流丽的 r 音。他觉得，自己从前根本没有听见过拉丁文。随后，那位红发的惊人年轻女子高诵起《悔罪经》，字字铿锵，清晰洪亮。那是另一种拉丁文，更硬，更洪亮；它还盖过旁人祷声的低吟，最后一刻便到了。
塞隆在许多别的床前面对过死亡；没有一场临终像这样撼动过他。必定是那姑娘的拉丁诵唱——那些伟大的名字一次又一次铿然复沓：*beatum Michaelem Archangelum, beatum Joannem Baptistam, sanctos Apostolos Petrum et Paulum*——在这间鄙陋的小窝棚里被念得那样骄傲而确信，才这样奇异地打动了他。
末了他随众人出来——蜡烛和交叠在十字架上的双手留在身后——走路像在梦里。及至走到外面的门口，站在亮光里眨眼、重新给肺里灌满正当的空气，他已经觉得难以置信：这一切是他亲眼看见、亲身随行过的。

### IX / 九
===Original===

Jeremiah Madden was supposed to be probably the richest man in Octavius. There was no doubt at all about his being its least pretentious citizen.
The huge and ornate modern mansion which he had built, putting to shame every other house in the place, gave an effect of ostentation to the Maddens as a family; it seemed only to accentuate the air of humility which enveloped Jeremiah as with a garment. Everybody knew some version of the many tales afloat which, in a kindly spirit, illustrated the incongruity between him and his splendid habitation. Some had it that he slept in the shed. Others told whimsical stories of his sitting alone in the kitchen evenings, smoking his old clay pipe, and sorrowing because the second Mrs. Madden would not suffer the pigs and chickens to come in and bear him company. But no matter how comic the exaggeration, these legends were invariably amiable. It lay in no man’s mouth to speak harshly of Jeremiah Madden.
He had been born a Connemara peasant, and he would die one. When he was ten years old he had seen some of his own family, and most of his neighbors, starve to death. He could remember looking at the stiffened figure of a woman stretched on the stones by the roadside, with the green stain of nettles on her white lips. A girl five years or so older than himself, also a Madden and distantly related, had started in despair off across the mountains to the town where it was said the poor-law officers were dealing out food. He could recall her coming back next day, wild-eyed with hunger and the fever; the officers had refused her relief because her bare legs were not wholly shrunken to the bone. “While there’s a calf on the shank, there’s no starvation,” they had explained to her. The girl died without profiting by this official apothegm. The boy found it burned ineffaceably upon his brain. Now, after a lapse of more than forty years, it seemed the thing that he remembered best about Ireland.
He had drifted westward as an unconsidered, unresisting item in that vast flight of the famine years. Others whom he rubbed against in that melancholy exodus, and deemed of much greater promise than himself, had done badly. Somehow he did well. He learned the wheelwright’s trade, and really that seemed all there was to tell.

===Chinese===

杰里迈亚·马登（Jeremiah Madden）被公认大概是奥克塔维乌斯最有钱的人；至于他是全城最不摆架子的人，则毫无疑义。
他盖起的那座宽大华丽的现代宅邸，叫本地一切别的房子都黯然失色，使马登一家在观感上俨然炫耀；可在杰里迈亚身上，它似乎只越发衬出那一身衣裳似的谦卑。流转的许多故事，人人都听过其中一两个版本，它们都好意地渲染着他同他那座华宅之间的不般配。有人说他睡在棚子里。有人讲些古怪的轶闻，说他晚间独自坐在厨房里抽他的老泥烟斗，发愁的是第二任马登太太不许猪和鸡进来给他作伴。可无论夸张得多滑稽，这些传闻无一不带着善意。说杰里迈亚·马登一句重话，谁也张不开这个口。
他生来是康尼马拉（Connemara）的农民，到死也是。他十岁上，亲眼见自己家里的人饿死了几个，邻居大半饿死。他记得望见一个女人僵直地躺在路边石头上，白嘴唇上染着荨麻的绿渍。一个比他大五岁光景的姑娘，也姓马登，沾点远亲，绝望里翻山越岭往镇上去——听说济贫法的官员在那儿放赈。他记得她第二天回来，饿疯了眼，还烧着热：官员们拒绝给她救济，因为她两条光腿还没有瘦得只剩骨头。「小腿肚上还挂着肉，就算不上饿死。」——他们是这样向她解释的。姑娘没有从这个官样的格言里得着好处，死了。这句话却深深烙在了这男孩的脑子里。如今四十多年过去，这仿佛成了他关于爱尔兰记得最牢的一件事。
在饥荒年月那场大流亡里，他作为一个不起眼的、随波逐流的分子，向西漂了过来。在那忧郁的大出走里同他摩肩接踵、他以为远比自己有出息的一些人，后来都不济。不知怎么，他倒发了。他学了造轮车的手艺——说来，事情仿佛也就这么多。

===Original===

The rest had been calm and sequent progression﻿—steady employment as a journeyman first; then marriage and a house and lot; the modest start as a master; the move to Octavius and cheap lumber; the growth of his business, always marked of late years stupendous﻿—all following naturally, easily, one thing out of another. Jeremiah encountered the idea among his fellows, now and again, that he was entitled to feel proud of all this. He smiled to himself at the thought, and then sent a sigh after the smile. What was it all but empty and transient vanity? The score of other Connemara boys he had known﻿—none very fortunate, several broken tragically in prison or the gutter, nearly all now gone the way of flesh﻿—were as good as he. He could not have it in his heart to take credit for his success; it would have been like sneering over their poor graves.
Jeremiah Madden was now fifty-three﻿—a little man of a reddened, weatherworn skin and a meditative, almost saddened, aspect. He had blue eyes, but his scanty iron-gray hair showed raven black in its shadows. The width and prominence of his cheekbones dominated all one’s recollections of his face. The long vertical upper-lip and irregular teeth made, in repose, an unshapely mouth; its smile, though, sweetened the whole countenance. He wore a fringe of stiff, steel-colored beard, passing from ear to ear under his chin. His weekday clothes were as simple as his workaday manners, fitting his short black pipe and his steadfast devotion to his business. On Sundays he dressed with a certain rigor of respectability, all in black, and laid aside tobacco, at least to the public view. He never missed going to the early Low Mass, quite alone. His family always came later, at the ten o’clock High Mass.
There had been, at one time or another, a good many members of this family. Two wives had borne Jeremiah Madden a total of over a dozen children. Of these there survived now only two of the first Mrs. Madden’s offspring﻿—Michael and Celia﻿—and a son of the present wife, who had been baptized Terence, but called himself Theodore. This minority of the family inhabited the great new house on Main Street. Jeremiah went every Sunday afternoon by himself to kneel in the presence of the majority, there where they lay in Saint Agnes’ consecrated ground.

===Chinese===

余下的，只是平静而依次的进展——先做雇工，活计稳定；接着娶妻，置下房子和地基；做师傅的小小起点；迁到奥克塔维乌斯，赶上便宜的木材；生意壮大，近几年更是壮大得惊人——一切都自然而然、轻巧顺遂，一件生出一件。同伴间时不时有人向他暗示：这一切他有资格引以为傲。他想到这里独自一笑，笑罢又叹一声。这一切算得了什么，不过空洞而倏忽的虚荣？他认得的那些康尼马拉的伙伴——运气都不算好，几个惨毁在监狱和沟渠里，如今几乎都已入了土——哪一个不同他一样好？他心里拿不出为自己成功居功的意思；那样想，就像对着他们可怜的坟墓冷笑。
杰里迈亚·马登如今五十三岁——一个小个子，皮肤泛红、饱经风霜，神情沉思，近乎悲戚。眼睛是蓝的，稀疏的铁灰头发在暗处却透出鸦黑。颧骨的宽和高，占据了对那张脸的全部记忆。上唇长而垂直，牙齿不齐，静下来时合成一张不好看的嘴；可它一笑，整张脸都甜了。下巴底下，两耳之间，他围了一圈硬直的钢色短须。平日的衣裳同他平日的举止一样朴素，正配他那支黑色短烟斗和他对营生的专注。星期天他穿得颇有几分体面的严谨，通身黑，烟草至少当众搁下不碰。清早的低弥撒他从不缺席，总是独自一人。家里的人总晚一步，来赴十点钟的大弥撒。
这个家，前前后后人口曾经不少。两位太太一共给杰里迈亚·马登生了十几个孩子。如今在世的，只有前马登太太所出的两个——迈克尔（Michael）同塞莉娅（Celia）——和现任太太的一个儿子：受洗名特伦斯（Terence），自己却管自己叫西奥多（Theodore）。家里的少数派住着正街的大新宅。每逢星期日下午，杰里迈亚独自步行，到多数派安卧的圣阿格尼斯（Saint Agnes）祝圣墓地，跪在他们面前。

===Original===

If the weather was good, he generally extended his walk through the fields to an old deserted Catholic burial-field, which had been used only in the first years after the famine invasion, and now was clean forgotten. The old wagon-maker liked to look over the primitive, neglected stones which marked the graves of these earlier exiles. Fully half of the inscriptions mentioned his County Galway﻿—there were two naming the very parish adjoining his. The latest date on any stone was of the remoter ’fifties. They had all been stricken down, here in this strange land with its bitter winters, while the memory of their own soft, humid, gentle west-coast air was fresh within them. Musing upon the clumsy sculpture, with its “R.I.P.,” or “Pray for the Soul of,” half to be guessed under the stain and moss of a generation, there would seem to him but a step from this present to that heartrending, awful past. What had happened between was a meaningless vision﻿—as impersonal as the passing of the planets overhead. He rarely had an impulse to tears in the new cemetery, where his ten children were. He never left this weed-grown, forsaken old God’s-acre dry-eyed.
One must not construct from all this the image of a melancholy man, as his fellows met and knew him. Mr. Madden kept his griefs, racial and individual, for his own use. To the men about him in the offices and the shops he presented day after day, year after year, an imperturbable cheeriness of demeanor. He had been always fortunate in the selection of lieutenants and chief helpers. Two of these had grown now into partners, and were almost as much a part of the big enterprise as Jeremiah himself. They spoke often of their inability to remember any unjust or petulant word of his﻿—much less any unworthy deed. Once they had seen him in a great rage, all the more impressive because he said next to nothing. A thoughtless fellow told a dirty story in the presence of some apprentices; and Madden, listening to this, drove the offender implacably from his employ. It was years now since anyone who knew him had ventured upon lewd pleasantries in his hearing. Jokes of the sort which women might hear he was very fond of though he had not much humor of his own. Of books he knew nothing whatever, and he made only the most perfunctory pretence now and again of reading the newspapers.

===Chinese===

天气好时，他通常还穿过田野，再走远些，到一片荒废的老天主教坟场去——那片坟地只在饥荒来袭后的头几年用过，如今已被忘得干干净净。老造车匠爱去看那些没人照管的粗陋墓石，底下埋着那些更早的流亡者。碑文里足足一半提到他的戈尔韦郡（County Galway）——有两块，写的正是紧邻他家乡的那个堂区。石上最晚的日期，也在遥远的五十年代。他们都是在这苦寒的异乡倒下的，那时他们自己西海岸湿润温柔的空气，记忆犹新。默想着那些笨拙的刻工——「R.I.P.」，或「为某某之灵祈祷」——在一辈子的苔痕污渍下只能猜个大概，他会觉得，从眼前这光景到那令人心碎的可怕的往昔，中间只隔着一步。两者之间发生的一切，反倒是一场没有意义的幻景——同头顶行星的运行一样非关个人。在新墓园里，他那十个孩子长眠之处，他难得有落泪的冲动；离开这片荒草没径、被人遗弃的老基督墓地，他却从不曾干着眼睛。
可千万别据此构想出一个愁眉苦脸的人来——那是他同伴们会面、相识的那个他。马登先生把他的悲苦，种族的也罢、个人的也罢，都留给自己受用。对办公室和工场里的人们，他日复一日、年复一年呈现着一种不动声色的爽朗。他挑选副手和主要帮手，向来运气好。其中两位如今已升为合伙人，同杰里迈亚本人一样，几乎成了这项大事业不可分的一部分。他们常说，怎么也想不起他有一句不公道或使气的话——更不必说一件不端的事了。他们只见过他大怒一回，正因为那回他几乎一言不发，愈发摂人。一个没轻没重的家伙当着几个学徒讲了个下流故事；马登听着，把那肇事者毫不容情地逐出了工号。知情的人当面再不敢讲秽语玩笑，这已是多年前的事了。女人也听得的那类笑话他很喜欢，虽然他自己谈不上有多少幽默。书他一概不知，报纸也只是隔三差五装一装样子翻一翻。

===Original===

The elder son Michael was very like his father﻿—diligent, unassuming, kindly, and simple﻿—a plain, tall, thin red man of nearly thirty, who toiled in paper cap and rolled-up shirtsleeves as the superintendent in the sawmill, and put on no airs whatever as the son of the master. If there was surprise felt at his not being taken into the firm as a partner, he gave no hint of sharing it. He attended to his religious duties with great zeal, and was President of the Sodality as a matter of course. This was regarded as his blind side; and young employees who cultivated it, and made broad their phylacteries under his notice, certainly had an added chance of getting on well in the works. To some few whom he knew specially well, Michael would confess that if he had had the brains for it, he should have wished to be a priest. He displayed no inclination to marry.
The other son, Terence, was some eight years younger, and seemed the product of a wholly different race. The contrast between Michael’s sandy skin and long gaunt visage and this dark boy’s handsome, rounded face, with its prettily curling black hair, large, heavily fringed brown eyes, and delicately modelled features, was not more obvious than their temperamental separation. This second lad had been away for years at school﻿—indeed, at a good many schools, for no one seemed to manage to keep him long. He had been with the Jesuits at Georgetown, with the Christian Brothers at Manhattan; the sectarian Mt. St. Mary’s and the severely secular Annapolis had both been tried, and proved misfits. The young man was home again now, and save that his name had become Theodore, he appeared in no wise changed from the beautiful, wilful, bold, and showy boy who had gone away in his teens. He was still rather small for his years, but so gracefully moulded in form, and so perfectly tailored, that the fact seemed rather an advantage than otherwise. He never dreamed of going near the wagon-works, but he did go a good deal﻿—in fact, most of the time﻿—to the Nedahma Club. His mother spoke often to her friends about her fears for his health. He never spoke to his friends about his mother at all.
The second Mrs. Madden did not, indeed, appeal strongly to the family pride. She had been a Miss Foley, a dressmaker, and an old maid.

===Chinese===

长子迈克尔极像他父亲——勤勉，谦和，厚道，简单——一个快三十的瘦高红皮肤汉子，戴纸帽、挽着袖子在锯木厂当监工，做东家的儿子却半点不摆谱。倘若有人纳罕他没被吸收入伙做合伙人，他自己没有露过一丝同感。他尽宗教本分极为热心，理所当然地做着圣体会的会长。这被看作他的软肋；年轻雇工们有意经营它，在他的注意下把经匣做得大大的，在厂里得到的好处确实多一分。对个别交情深的，迈克尔会承认：倘若自己有那个脑子，他本想做一名神父。他没有显出一点成家的意思。
另一个儿子特伦斯小八岁上下，仿佛是另一个人种的产品。迈克尔沙色的皮肤、瘦长清癯的脸，同这个黑发少年的俊秀圆脸——漂亮的黑色鬈发，大而浓睫的棕色眼睛，精致的五官——之间的对照，还比不上两人脾性的悬殊。这第二个孩子离家上学上了多年——说实在的，上了好些所学校，因为没有一家似乎留得住他长久。他在乔治敦随耶稣会读过，在曼哈顿随基督徒兄弟会读过；教门色彩浓的圣玛丽山和一丝不苟世俗的安纳波利斯都试过，都证明不合身。如今这年轻人又回了家；除了名字变成了西奥多，他同当年十几岁离家时那个漂亮、任性、大胆、爱出风头的男孩相比，仿佛没有半点改变。就年纪说他仍嫌矮小，可身量塑得那样秀挺，衣裳裁得那样熨帖，这短处倒似乎成了长处。他做梦也不走近造车工场；倒确确实常去——其实是常驻——内达马俱乐部。他母亲常对朋友们念叨他的健康。他对朋友们从来不提他母亲。
第二位马登太太，实在说，撑不起这个家的门第。她原是弗利小姐（Miss Foley），做女装的，一个老姑娘。

===Original===

Jeremiah had married her after a brief widowerhood, principally because she was the sister of his parish priest, and had a considerable reputation for piety. It was at a time when the expansion of his business was promising certain wealth, and suggesting the removal to Octavius. He was conscious of a notion that his obligations to social respectability were increasing; it was certain that the embarrassments of a motherless family were. Miss Foley had shown a good deal of attention to his little children. She was not ill-looking; she bore herself with modesty; she was the priest’s sister﻿—the niece once removed of a vicar-general. And so it came about.
Although those most concerned did not say so, everybody could see from the outset the pity of its ever having come about at all. The pious and stiffly respectable priest’s sister had been harmless enough as a spinster. It made the heart ache to contemplate her as a wife. Incredibly narrow-minded, ignorant, suspicious, vain, and sour-tempered, she must have driven a less equable and well-rooted man than Jeremiah Madden to drink or flight. He may have had his temptations, but they made no mark on the even record of his life. He only worked the harder, concentrating upon his business those extra hours which another sort of home-life would have claimed instead. The end of twenty years found him a rich man, but still toiling pertinaciously day by day, as if he had his wage to earn. In the great house which had been built to please, or rather placate, his wife, he kept to himself as much as possible. The popular story of his smoking alone in the kitchen was more or less true; only Michael as a rule sat with him, too weak-lunged for tobacco himself, but reading stray scraps from the papers to the lonely old man, and talking with him about the works, the while Jeremiah meditatively sucked his clay pipe. One or two evenings in the week the twain spent up in Celia’s part of the house, listening with the awe of simple, honest mechanics to the music she played for them.
Celia was to them something indefinably less, indescribably more, than a daughter and sister. They could not think there had ever been anything like her before in the world; the notion of criticising any deed or word of hers would have appeared to them monstrous and unnatural.

===Chinese===

杰里迈亚鳏居不久便娶了她，主要因为她是本堂神父的妹妹，又素有虔敬之名。那正是他生意扩张、眼看要发大财、酛酿迁居奥克塔维乌斯的时候。他自觉对社会体面的义务在加重；缺娘的孩子们的家累在加重，则是一定的。弗利小姐对他的小孩们表现过许多关切。她不难看；举止端谨；她是神父的妹妹——还是一位副主教的隔房侄女。于是事情就成了。
虽然当事的人们没有说破，人人从头就看得出：这事根本不该成，可惜了。这位虔敬而古板的神父之妹做姑娘时倒也无害；把她想成一个妻子，就叫人心酸。心地狭隘得出奇，无知，多疑，虚荣，脾气又酸，换了任何一个不如杰里迈亚·马登沉稳有根的人，早叫她逼得酗酒或出走了。他未必没有受过试探，但他一生平顺的记录上不留一点痕迹。他只是愈加勤勉，把另一种家庭生活本会占去的额外钟点，统统灌注到生意上。二十年下来，他成了富人，却仍旧一天一天坚韧地劳作，仿佛工钱还待挣。在那座为讨太太欢心——不如说为安抚太太——盖起的大宅里，他尽量守着自己。他独自在厨房抽烟那个流行的传说，大体属实；只是照例还有迈克尔坐着陪他——自己肺弱抽不得烟，就读报上的零星片段给寂寞的老人听，同他谈厂里的事，杰里迈亚则沉思地咂着他的泥烟斗。每星期总有一两个晚上，爷儿俩在塞莉娅那半边屋里度过，以老实手艺人那种敬畏，听她为他们弹奏的音乐。
塞莉娅于他们，比女儿和妹妹说不清地少了些什么，又道不明地多了些什么。他们想不出世上曾有过她这样的人；批评她一言一行的念头，在他们看来简直是怪诞和逆天。

===Original===

She seemed to have come up to this radiant and wise and marvellously talented womanhood of hers, to their minds, quite spontaneously. There had been a little Celia﻿—a redheaded, sulky, mutinous slip of a girl, always at war with her stepmother, and affording no special comfort or hope to the rest of the family. Then there was a long gap, during which the father, four times a year, handed Michael a letter he had received from the superioress of a distant convent, referring with cold formality to the studies and discipline by which Miss Madden might profit more if she had been better brought up, and enclosing a large bill. Then all at once they beheld a big Celia, whom they spoke of as being home again, but who really seemed never to have been there before﻿—a tall, handsome, confident young woman, swift of tongue and apprehension, appearing to know everything there was to be known by the most learned, able to paint pictures, carve wood, speak in diverse languages, and make music for the gods, yet with it all a very proud lady, one might say a queen.
The miracle of such a Celia as this impressed itself even upon the stepmother. Mrs. Madden had looked forward with a certain grim tightening of her combative jaws to the homecoming of the “redhead.” She felt herself much more the fine lady now than she had been when the girl went away. She had her carriage now, and the magnificent new house was nearly finished, and she had a greater number of ailments, and spent far more money on doctor’s bills, than any other lady in the whole section. The flush of pride in her greatest achievement up to date﻿—having the most celebrated of New York physicians brought up to Octavius by special train﻿—still prickled in her blood. It was in all the papers, and the admiration of the flatterers and “soft-sawdherers”﻿—wives of Irish merchants and smaller professional men who formed her social circle﻿—was raising visions in her poor head of going next year with Theodore to Saratoga, and fastening the attention of the whole fashionable republic upon the variety and resources of her invalidism. Mrs. Madden’s fancy did not run to the length of seeing her stepdaughter also at Saratoga; it pictured her still as the sullen and hated “redhead,” moping defiantly in corners, or courting by her insolence the punishments which leaped against their leash in the stepmother’s mind to get at her.

===Chinese===

在他们心里，她仿佛是全然自发地升到这一步——这样光彩、这样智慧、这样天赋异禀的女性。从前有过一个小塞莉娅——红头发、乖戾、倔强的一条小丫头，永远在同继母交战，也给家里别人带不来多少安慰或指望。随后是一长段空白：其间做父亲的每季度一次，把一封远处修院院长来信交给迈克尔——信冷冷地按格式提到马登小姐的课业与管教，说她倘使教养更好些本可获益更多，随信附一张大账单。随后，忽然间，他们看见了一个大塞莉娅——他们说她「回家了」，其实她仿佛从来没有在家过——一个高挑、俊美、自信的少妇，口齿和悟性一样敏捷，最渊博的人该懂的她仿佛都懂，会画画，会木雕，会说好几种语言，能为神明奏乐；而与此同时，又是一位十分骄傲的贵女，不妨说，一位女王。
这样一个塞莉娅的奇迹，连继母也被镇住了。马登太太原本咬紧了她那好斗的牙关，专等「红毛」回家。如今她自觉比姑娘离家那时阔气多了：有了自家的马车；富丽的新宅快落成了；论毛病之多、医生账单之巨，全地段没有一位太太比得上她。她至今最得意的一桩功业——专列火车把纽约最有名的名医接来奥克塔维乌斯——那一阵得意还刺刺地留在血里。各报都登了；奉承者和「软奉承」们——爱尔兰商人家的太太和较小的自由职业者眷属，她们构成她的社交圈——的艳羡，正在她可怜的脑袋里升起明年的幻景：携西奥多去萨拉托加，让整个时髦的共和国都来瞩目她病况的花样与家底。马登太太的幻想还伸展不到把继女也放进萨拉托加；她脑海里那还是那个愠怒可恨的「红毛」，赌气闷坐角落里，或者用她的无礼去招惹惩罚——那些惩罚在继母心里挣着链子，随时要扑到她身上去。

===Original===

The real Celia, when she came, fairly took Mrs. Madden’s breath away. The peevish little plans for annoyance and tyranny, the resolutions born of ignorant and jealous egotism, found themselves swept out of sight by the very first swirl of Celia’s dress-train, when she came down from her room robed in peacock blue. The stepmother could only stare.
Now, after two years of it, Mrs. Madden still viewed her stepdaughter with round-eyed uncertainty, not unmixed with wrathful fear. She still drove about behind two magnificent horses; the new house had become almost tiresome by familiarity; her preeminence in the interested minds of the Dearborn County Medical Society was as towering as ever, but somehow it was all different. There was a note of unreality nowadays in Mrs. Donnelly’s professions of wonder at her bearing up under her multiplied maladies; there was almost a leer of mockery in the sympathetic smirk with which the Misses Mangan listened to her symptoms. Even the doctors, though they kept their faces turned toward her, obviously did not pay much attention; the people in the street seemed no longer to look at her and her equipage at all. Worst of all, something of the meaning of this managed to penetrate her own mind. She caught now and again a dim glimpse of herself as others must have been seeing her for years﻿—as a stupid, ugly, boastful, and bad-tempered old nuisance. And it was always as if she saw this in a mirror held up by Celia.
Of open discord there had been next to none. Celia would not permit it, and showed this so clearly from the start that there was scarcely need for her saying it. It seemed hardly necessary for her to put into words any of her desires, for that matter. All existing arrangements in the Madden household seemed to shrink automatically and make room for her, whichever way she walked. A whole quarter of the unfinished house set itself apart for her. Partitions altered themselves; doorways moved across to opposite sides; a recess opened itself, tall and deep, for it knew not what statue﻿—simply because, it seemed, the Lady Celia willed it so.
When the family moved into this mansion, it was with a consciousness that the only one who really belonged there was Celia. She alone could behave like one perfectly at home.

===Chinese===

真的塞莉娅一回来，登时叫马登太太倒抽一口冷气。那些找岔与逞威的小盘算、那些出于无知而妒忌的自我的决意，在塞莉娅一身孔雀蓝从楼上下来、裙裾旋起的第一卷里，就被扫得无影无踪。继母只会瞪着眼看。
如今，两年过去，马登太太望着继女，仍是一双圆眼睛的不定，还掺着怒冲冲的惧怕。她依旧坐着两匹骏马的车；新房子住惯了，几乎住腻了；她在迪尔伯恩县医学会诸位有心人心目中的独尊，仍同从前一样巍峨——可不知怎么，全变了样。如今唐纳利太太惊佩她百病缠身还撑得住的那些表白里，有一丝虚浮；曼根家小姐们听着她数症状时那副同情的假笑里，几乎带着嘲弄的斜睨。连医生们，脸虽还朝着她，显然也不大上心；街上的人仿佛根本不再看她同她的车马了。最糟的是，这局面的某种含义，居然也渗进了她自己的头脑。她时不时朦朦胧胱地瞥见一眼自己——正如旁人这些年来必已看见的那样：一个愚蠢、丑陋、好吹、坏脾气的老厌物。而她看见这一眼，永远像是照在塞莉娅举起的一面镜子里。
公开的反目，几乎没有过。塞莉娅不容许；她从头就把这一点表得太清楚，连说破都几乎用不着。其实，她的任何愿望都几乎无须出口。马登家一切既有的安排，仿佛都会自动收缩，给她让路，无论她往哪边走。未完工的大宅整整四分之一自行划归了她。隔墙自行改位；门洞自行挪到对面；一处壁龛自行敞开，又高又深，专为安放天知道什么雕像——只因，仿佛，塞莉娅娘娘这样定了。
全家搬进这座巨宅时，人人心里都明白：真正配住在这里的只有塞莉娅。只有她能举止如鱼得水，仿佛在自己家里。

===Original===

It seemed entirely natural to the others that she should do just what she liked, shut them off from her portion of the house, take her meals there if she felt disposed, and keep such hours as pleased her instant whim. If she awakened them at midnight by her piano, or deferred her breakfast to the late afternoon, they felt that it must be all right, since Celia did it. She had one room furnished with only divans and huge, soft cushions, its walls covered with large copies of statuary not too strictly clothed, which she would suffer no one, not even the servants, to enter. Michael fancied sometimes, when he passed the draped entrance to this sacred chamber, that the portiere smelt of tobacco, but he would not have spoken of it, even had he been sure. Old Jeremiah, whose established habit it was to audit minutely the expenses of his household, covered over round sums to Celia’s separate banking account, upon the mere playful hint of her holding her checkbook up, without a dream of questioning her.
That the stepmother had joy, or indeed anything but gall and wormwood, out of all this is not to be pretended. There lingered along in the recollection of the family some vague memories of her having tried to assert an authority over Celia’s comings and goings at the outset, but they grouped themselves as only parts of the general disorder of moving and settling, which a fortnight or so quite righted. Mrs. Madden still permitted herself a certain license of hostile comment when her stepdaughter was not present, and listened with gratification to what the women of her acquaintance ventured upon saying in the same spirit; but actual interference or remonstrance she never offered nowadays. The two rarely met, for that matter, and exchanged only the baldest and curtest forms of speech.
Celia Madden interested all Octavius deeply. This she must have done in any case, if only because she was the only daughter of its richest citizen. But the bold, luxuriant quality of her beauty, the original and piquant freedom of her manners, the stories told in gossip about her lawlessness at home, her intellectual attainments, and artistic vagaries﻿—these were even more exciting. The unlikelihood of her marrying anyone﻿—at least any Octavian﻿—was felt to add a certain romantic zest to the image she made on the local perceptions.

===Chinese===

她爱做什么就做什么——把旁人关在她那半边屋子外，兴致来了就在那儿独自用饭，起居随一时的兴致——这在旁人看来全然自然。她若半夜里用钢琴把他们惊醒，或把早饭推到下半晌，他们都觉得这必是对的，因为是塞莉娅做的。她有一间屋子，只陈卧榻和大软垫，四壁挂着放大复制的雕像，衣饰未必严实，任何人她都不许进，仆人也不例外。迈克尔有时从这间圣室垂着门帘的门口经过，恍惚觉得门帘上有烟味，可他纵然拿准了，也不会说。老杰里迈亚向来有逐项稽核家用开支的老规矩，可只要塞莉娅把支票簿扬一扬，作个玩笑的暗示，他就整笔整笔地拨进她独立的银行户头，做梦也不问一句。
要说继母从这一切里得了欢趣——或者除了苦胆和茵陈还有什么——那可装不得。家里人模糊记得，起初她似乎试过对塞莉娅的出入行使一点权威；可这些记忆如今都归并进搬家安顿的一般性忙乱里去了，个把星期便一切就绪。如今马登太太仍许给自己一点自由：继女不在跟前时说几句敌意的评语，也乐于听相熟的女眷们壮着胆子说同一口风的话；可实际的干涉或规劝，她如今再也不给。说实话，这样两个人难得碰面，见了面也只交换最干瘪最简慢的套语。
塞莉娅·马登叫整个奥克塔维乌斯深感兴趣。这原是免不了的：单凭她是全城首富唯一的女儿这一条也就够了。可她那大胆、繁茂的美，她那新颖而辛辣的举止的自由，闲话里流传的她在家的任性，她的学问和艺术的奇行——这些更叫人兴奋。她不会嫁给任何人——至少不会嫁任何奥克塔维乌斯人——这一层不可能，又给她在当地知觉里的形象添了某种浪漫的刺激。

===Original===

There was no visible young Irishman at all approaching the social and financial standard of the Maddens; it was taken for granted that a mixed marriage was quite out of the question in this case. She seemed to have more business about the church than even the priest. She was always playing the organ, or drilling the choir, or decorating the altars with flowers, or looking over the robes of the acolytes for rents and stains, or going in or out of the pastorate. Clearly this was not the sort of girl to take a Protestant husband.
The gossip of the town concerning her was, however, exclusively Protestant. The Irish spoke of her, even among themselves, but seldom. There was no occasion for them to pretend to like her: they did not know her, except in the most distant and formal fashion. Even the members of the choir, of both sexes, had the sense of being held away from her at haughty arm’s length. No single parishioner dreamed of calling her friend. But when they referred to her, it was always with a cautious and respectful reticence. For one thing, she was the daughter of their chief man, the man they most esteemed and loved. For another, reservations they may have had in their souls about her touched close upon a delicately sore spot. It could not escape their notice that their Protestant neighbors were watching her with vigilant curiosity, and with a certain tendency to wink when her name came into conversation along with that of Father Forbes. It had never yet got beyond a tendency﻿—the barest fluttering suggestion of a tempted eyelid﻿—but the whole Irish population of the place felt themselves to be waiting, with clenched fists but sinking hearts, for the wink itself.
The Rev. Theron Ware had not caught even the faintest hint of these overtures to suspicion.
When he had entered the huge, dark, cool vault of the church, he could see nothing at first but a faint light up over the gallery, far at the other end. Then, little by little, his surroundings shaped themselves out of the gloom. To his right was a rail and some broad steps rising toward a softly confused mass of little gray vertical bars and the pale twinkle of tiny spots of gilded reflection, which he made out in the dusk to be the candles and trappings of the altar. Overhead the great arches faded away from foundations of dimly discernible capitals into utter blackness.

===Chinese===

像马登家这样社会与钱财的标准，全城没有一个配得上的年轻爱尔兰人；人们理所当然地认定，这回异教通婚绝无可能。她在教堂里的事务，仿佛比神父还多。她不是在弹管风琴，就是在操练唱诗班，或用鲜花装饰祭坛，或检查辅祭的祭衣有无破绽污渍，或在牧馆进进出出。这显然不是会娶新教徒丈夫的那种姑娘。
可是城里关于她的闲话，纯属新教一面。爱尔兰人谈起她，连在自己人中间，也不多。他们无须装作喜欢她：他们不认识她，至多保持着最疏远最客套的关系。连唱诗班里的男女成员，都觉出被她傲然隔在一臂之外。没有一个堂区居民敢想同她做朋友。可他们提到她，总是带着谨慎而恭敬的缄默。一来，她是他们头号人物的女儿——他们最敬最爱的人物。二来，他们灵魂里对她可能存有的保留，正触着一个微妙而疼痛的旧疮。他们的新教邻居正睁着警惕的好奇看她，而每当她的名字同福布斯神父的名字一起进到谈话里，便有某种眨眼的倾向——这逃不过他们的眼睛。这至今还只是倾向——眼皮被撩拨得最轻微的一颤——可全城的爱尔兰人都觉得自己在攢紧拳头、沉下心，等着那一眨真的落下。
塞隆·韦尔牧师呢，连这些可疑的先声最淡的一丝也不曾听到。
他走进教堂那巨大、幽暗、清凉的穹窟时，起初什么也看不见，只在远远另一头的楼廊上方有一点微光。随后，四周的轮廓才一点点从昏暗中显形。右手是一道栏杆和几级宽台阶，往上一团柔和而纷乱的东西——灰色的小竖条和镀金反光的小小闪烁，他在暮色里认出那是祭坛的蜡烛和饰物。头顶上，巨大的拱从依稀可辨的柱头一线，隐进彻底的黑。

===Original===

There was a strange medicinal odor﻿—as of cubeb cigarettes﻿—in the air.
After a little pause, he tiptoed noiselessly up the side aisle toward the end of the church﻿—toward the light above the gallery. This radiance from a single gas-jet expanded as he advanced, and spread itself upward over a burnished row of monster metal pipes, which went towering into the darkness like giants. They were roaring at him now﻿—a sonorous, deafening, angry bellow, which made everything about him vibrate. The gallery balustrade hid the keyboard and the organist from view. There were only these jostling brazen tubes, as big round as trees and as tall, trembling with their own furious thunder. It was for all the world as if he had wandered into some vast tragical, enchanted cave, and was being drawn against his will﻿—like fascinated bird and python﻿—toward fate at the savage hands of these swollen and enraged genii.
He stumbled in the obscure light over a kneeling-bench, making a considerable racket. On the instant the noise from the organ ceased, and he saw the black figure of a woman rise above the gallery-rail and look down.
“Who is it?” the indubitable voice of Miss Madden demanded sharply.
Theron had a sudden sheepish notion of turning and running. With the best grace he could summon, he called out an explanation instead.
“Wait a minute. I’m through now. I’m coming down,” she returned. He thought there was a note of amusement in her tone.
She came to him a moment later, accompanied by a thin, tall man, whom Theron could barely see in the dark, now that the organ-light too was gone. This man lighted a match or two to enable them to make their way out.
When they were on the sidewalk, Celia spoke: “Walk on ahead, Michael!” she said. “I have some matters to speak of with Mr. Ware.”

===Chinese===

空气里有一种古怪的药味——像荜澄茄香烟。
略停了停，他踮着脚、悄没声儿地沿侧廊往教堂那头去——往楼廊上方的那点光去。这一支煤气灯的光焰随他前进而扩大，向上铺展开去，照出一排锃亮的巨大金属音管，像巨人般耸入黑暗。它们此刻正朝他吼——洪亮、震耳、怒气冲天的咆哮，震得他周身一切都颤。楼廊的栏杆挡住了琴键和琴师。只有这一排挤挤挨挨的黄铜大管，树一样粗、树一样高，在自家狂怒的雷鸣里发抖。这光景活脱是他误入了某个巨大、悲惨、着魔的洞窟，身不由己地——像被催眠的鸟和蟒——被这些鼓胀、暴怒的巨灵一只只野蛮的手，拖向命运。
他在昏暗里绊上一张跪凳，弄出好大一声响。管风琴的声响当即停了；他看见一个女人的黑影从楼廊栏杆上直起来，朝下望。
「是谁？」这不容置疑的马登小姐的嗓音厉声问。
塞隆猛生出一个怯场的念头：转身就跑。可他竭力拿出体面来，喊出了解释。
「等一下。我这儿完了。这就下来。」她回话。他觉得她的声口里有一点被逗乐的意味。
片刻后她来到他跟前，还带着一个瘦高个男人；管风琴的灯也熄了，塞隆在黑暗里几乎看不清他。这人划了一两根火柴，照着他们出去。
到了人行道上，塞莉娅开口：「迈克尔，你头里走！」她说。「我有些事要同韦尔先生谈谈。」
