第四部

### V / 五
===Original===

While Mr. Ware stood thus on the doorstep, through a minute of formless musing, the priest and the girl came out, and, somewhat to his confusion, made him one of their party. He felt himself flushing under the idea that they would think he had waited for them﻿—was thrusting himself upon them. The notion prompted him to bow frigidly in response to Father Forbes’ pleasant “I am glad to meet you, sir,” and his outstretched hand.
“I dropped in by the﻿—the merest accident,” Theron said. “I met them bringing the poor man home, and﻿—and quite without thinking, I obeyed the impulse to follow them in, and didn’t realize﻿—”
He stopped short, annoyed by the reflection that this was his second apology. The girl smiled placidly at him, the while she put up her parasol.
“It did me good to see you there,” she said, quite as if she had known him all her life. “And so it did the rest of us.”
Father Forbes permitted himself a soft little chuckle, approving rather than mirthful, and patted her on the shoulder with the air of being fifty years her senior instead of fifteen. To the minister’s relief, he changed the subject as the three started together toward the road.
“Then, again, no doctor was sent for!” he exclaimed, as if resuming a familiar subject with the girl. Then he turned to Theron. “I daresay you have no such trouble; but with our poorer people it is very vexing. They will *not* call in a physician, but hurry off first for the clergyman. I don’t know that it is altogether to avoid doctor’s bills, but it amounts to that in effect. Of course in this case it made no difference; but I have had to make it a rule not to go out at night unless they bring me a physician’s card with his assurance that it is a genuine affair. Why, only last winter, I was routed up after midnight, and brought off in the mud and pelting rain up one of the new streets on the hillside there, simply because a factory girl who was laced too tight had fainted at a dance. I slipped and fell into a puddle in the darkness, ruined a new overcoat, and got drenched to the skin; and when I arrived the girl had recovered and was dancing away again, thirteen to the dozen. It was then that I made the rule. I hope, Mr. Ware, that Octavius is producing a pleasant impression upon you so far?”
“I scarcely know yet,” answered Theron.

===Chinese===

韦尔先生正在台阶上这样立着，胡思乱想了一分钟，神父和姑娘走了出来，而且——叫他有点慌——把他收作了他们一路的人。他想到他们也许当他是在等候他们——是硬凑上前来——脸便红了。这念头促使他对福布斯神父那句愉快的“幸会，先生”和伸出的手，只冷冷鞠了一躬。
“我是顺路——纯粹偶然进来的，”塞隆说，“我碰上他们送那可怜的人回家，就——就全没想到什么，跟着一时的冲动进来了，并没有意识到——”
他蓦地打住，想起这已是第二回道歉，好生懊恼。姑娘一面收阳伞，一面冲他安然一笑。
“看见您在那儿，我心里很受用，”她说，口气简直像同他认识了一辈子，“我们其余的人也都受用。”
福布斯神父纵容自己轻轻一笑，嘉许的意思多过好笑，拍了拍她的肩，一副比她年长五十岁而不是十五岁的气派。叫牧师松快的是，三人一起朝大路走去时，他换了话头。
“再说了，也没请大夫！”他像同姑娘续上熟话题似的嚷了一声。随后转向塞隆。“您那边想必没这种烦恼；可在我们穷教民，这就烦人得很。他们死活不肯请大夫，倒先火急火燎去请神父。我不敢说全是为了躲大夫的账，可实际上就是这么回事。当然今天这一位，请不请都一样；可我不得不立下一条规矩：夜里不出去，除非他们带来大夫的名片，担保这是一桩正经事。嗐，就在去年冬天，我半夜里被叫起来，拖进泥泞瓢泼的大雨，爬上山坡那边一条新街，就因为一个厂里的姑娘束腰束得太紧，跳舞时晕倒了。黑地里我一滑跌进水坑，毁了一件新大衣，浑身湿透；赶到那里，姑娘早缓过来了，正跳得撒欢呢。就是从那时起我立了那规矩。我希望，韦尔先生，奥克塔维乌斯到目前为止给您的印象还好吧？”
“我还说不好。”塞隆答道。

===Original===

The genial talk of the priest, with its whimsical anecdote, had in truth passed over his head. His mind still had room for nothing but that novel deathbed scene, with the winged captain of the angelic host, the Baptist, the glorified Fisherman and the Preacher, all being summoned down in the pomp of liturgical Latin to help MacEvoy to die. “If you don’t mind my saying so,” he added hesitatingly, “what I have just seen in there *did* make a very powerful impression upon me.”
“It is a very ancient ceremony,” said the priest; “probably Persian, like the baptismal form, although, for that matter, we can never dig deep enough for the roots of these things. They all turn up Turanian if we probe far enough. Our ways separate here, I’m afraid. I am delighted to have made your acquaintance, Mr. Ware. Pray look in upon me, if you can as well as not. We are near neighbors, you know.”
Father Forbes had shaken hands, and moved off up another street some distance, before the voice of the girl recalled Theron to himself.
“Of course you knew *him* by name,” she was saying, “and he knew *you* by sight, and had talked of you; but *my* poor inferior sex has to be introduced. I am Celia Madden. My father has the wagon-shops, and I﻿—I play the organ at the church.”
“I﻿—I am delighted to make your acquaintance,” said Theron, conscious as he spoke that he had slavishly echoed the formula of the priest. He could think of nothing better to add than, “Unfortunately, we have no organ in our church.”
The girl laughed, as they resumed their walk down the street. “I’m afraid I couldn’t undertake two,” she said, and laughed again. Then she spoke more seriously. “That ceremony must have interested you a good deal, never having seen it before. I saw that it was all new to you, and so I made bold to take you under my wing, so to speak.”
“You were very kind,” said the young minister. “It was really a great experience for me. May﻿—may I ask, is it a part of your functions, in the church, I mean, to attend these last rites?”
“Mercy, no!” replied the girl, spinning the parasol on her shoulder and smiling at the thought. “No; it was only because MacEvoy was one of our workmen, and really came by his death through father sending him up to trim a tree. Ann MacEvoy will never forgive us that, the longest day she lives. Did you notice her? She wouldn’t speak to me.

===Chinese===

神父这段兴致勃勃的闲谈，连同那段滑稽的轶事，其实从他头顶上过去了。他心里装得下的，还是那幕新奇的临终场景——天军的有翼统帅、施洗者、得了荣耀的渔人和宣教者，一齐在礼仪拉丁文的盛装里被召下来，帮麦克埃沃伊死。“您要是不嫌我多嘴，”他迟疑地补充道，“我方才在里头看见的那一幕，*确实*给了我极深的印象。”
“那是一场非常古老的仪式，”神父说，“多半源出波斯，同洗礼的样式一样；不过说到这个，这类东西的根，我们永远掘不够深。再往深探，它们全都露出图兰的底。咱们在这儿分路吧，恐怕。很高兴认识了您，韦尔先生。得便请过来坐坐，您知道，咱们是近邻。”
福布斯神父握过手，沿另一条街走出老远，姑娘的声音才把塞隆唤回自己。
“您当然早知道*他*的大名，”她正说着，“他也认得*您*的相貌，还谈起过您；可*我*这个可怜的低一等性别，得靠人引见。我叫塞莉娅·马登。我父亲开那家车马作坊，我呢——我在教堂里弹琴。”
“我——我很高兴认识您。”塞隆说，说出口才意识到自己照抄了神父的套话。他想不出更好的添语，只有一句：“可惜，我们教堂里没有琴。”
姑娘笑起来，两人接着沿街往下走。“两架琴我怕弹不过来。”她说，又笑了。随后她说得郑重了些。“那场仪式，您从前没见过，想必很让您感兴趣。我瞧出这一切对您全是新的，所以就冒昧——这么说吧——把您收编到我的翅膀底下了。”
“您真好心，”年轻的牧师说，“这于我实在是一次大开眼界。可不可以——可不可以问一句，在教里，我是说，出席这类临终仪式，算是您的职分么？”
“天哪，不是！”姑娘答道，肩上转着阳伞，想到这念头便笑了。“不；只因为麦克埃沃伊是我们作坊的工人，而且说实在的，他这条命就是父亲派他上树修剪才丢的。安·麦克埃沃伊一辈子也不会饶我们这一遭，活到多老也不饶。您注意到她了么？她都不肯理我。

===Original===

After you came out, I tried to tell her that we would look out for her and the children; but all she would say to me was: ‘An’ fwat would a wheelwright, an’ him the father of a family, be doin’ up a tree?’ ”
They had come now upon the main street of the village, with its flagstone sidewalk overhung by a lofty canopy of elm-boughs. Here, for the space of a block, was concentrated such fashionable elegance of mansions and ornamental lawns as Octavius had to offer; and it was presented with the irregularity so characteristic of our restless civilization. Two or three of the houses survived untouched from the earlier days﻿—prim, decorous structures, each with its gabled centre and lower wings, each with its row of fluted columns supporting the classical roof of a piazza across its whole front, each vying with the others in the whiteness of those wooden walls enveloping its bright green blinds. One had to look over picket fences to see these houses, and in doing so caught the notion that they thus railed themselves off in pride at being able to remember before the railroad came to the village, or the wagon-works were thought of.
Before the neighboring properties the fences had been swept away, so that one might stroll from the sidewalk straight across the well-trimmed sward to any one of a dozen elaborately modern doorways. Some of the residences, thus frankly proffering friendship to the passerby, were of wood painted in drabs and dusky reds, with bulging windows which marked the native yearning for the medieval, and shingles that strove to be accounted tiles. Others﻿—a prouder, less pretentious sort﻿—were of brick or stone, with terra-cotta mouldings set into the walls, and with real slates covering the riot of turrets and peaks and dormer peepholes overhead.
Celia Madden stopped in front of the largest and most important-looking of these new edifices, and said, holding out her hand: “Here I am, once more. Good morning, Mr. Ware.”
Theron hoped that his manner did not betray the flash of surprise he felt in discovering that his new acquaintance lived in the biggest house in Octavius. He remembered now that someone had pointed it out as the abode of the owner of the wagon factories; but it had not occurred to him before to associate this girl with that village magnate. It was stupid of him, of course, because she had herself mentioned her father.

===Chinese===

“您出来之后，我想告诉她，我们会照应她和孩子们；可她对我只说一句：‘一个做车轮的，还是拖家带口的人，爬到树顶上去做什么呀？’”
这时他们已走到村里的大街，石板的人行道上覆着榆树枝叶高高搭成的凉棚。就在这一个街区的地面上，集中了奥克塔维乌斯拿得出手的全部时新宅第和观赏草坪；而且排布得错错落落，正是我们这个躁动文明的一贯作风。两三所房子还是早年的旧物，原封未动——齐整规矩的宅子，各带一个人字形的中央和两翼低厢，各带一排凹槽柱子，撑起横贯整个正面的古典游廊屋顶，木墙的白净和亮绿窗板相映，彼此争胜。看这些房子得越过尖桩栅栏；看的时候你会生出这样一个印象：它们用栅栏把自己圈起来，是出于骄傲——骄傲自己还记得铁路通到村里、车马厂尚无人听说的那些年月。
邻家地界的栅栏则已扫荡一空，人行道上散步的人可以径直跨过修剪齐整的草坪，走向十几座讲究的新式门廊中的随便哪一座。有几所这样向路人坦然示好的宅子是木造的，漆成灰褐和暗红，鼓出的窗户标志着本土对中世纪的渴慕，木瓦极力想冒充屋瓦。另一些——更傲、也较少铺张的一类——是砖石造的，墙上嵌着赤陶的线脚，头顶上塔尖、山尖和老虎窗小瞭望孔的一片喧闹，覆着真正的石板。
塞莉娅·马登在这些新宅里最大、最气派的一座前站住，伸出手来说：“我又到家了。再见，韦尔先生。”
塞隆发现这位新相识住在奥克塔维乌斯最大的宅子里，心头掠过一阵惊讶；他指望自己的神色没有泄露它。他此刻想起，曾有人指过这宅子，说它是车马厂东家的居所；可他先前不曾想到把这位姑娘同那位村庄巨富联系起来。这自然是他迟钝，因为她自己明明提过她的父亲。

===Original===

He looked at her again with an awkward smile, as he formally shook the gloved hand she gave him, and lifted his soft hat. The strong noon sunlight, forcing its way down between the elms, and beating upon her parasol of lace-edged, creamy silk, made a halo about her hair and face at once brilliant and tender. He had not seen before how beautiful she was. She nodded in recognition of his salute, and moved up the lawn walk, spinning the sunshade on her shoulder.
Though the parsonage was only three blocks away, the young minister had time to think about a good many things before he reached home.
First of all, he had to revise in part the arrangement of his notions about the Irish. Save for an occasional isolated and taciturn figure among the nomadic portion of the hired help in the farm country, Theron had scarcely ever spoken to a person of this curiously alien race before. He remembered now that there had been some dozen or more Irish families in Tyre, quartered in the outskirts among the brickyards, but he had never come in contact with any of them, or given to their existence even a passing thought. So far as personal acquaintance went, the Irish had been to him only a name.
But what a sinister and repellent name! His views on this general subject were merely those common to his communion and his environment. He took it for granted, for example, that in the large cities most of the poverty and all the drunkenness, crime, and political corruption were due to the perverse qualities of this foreign people﻿—qualities accentuated and emphasized in every evil direction by the baleful influence of a false and idolatrous religion. It is hardly too much to say that he had never encountered a dissenting opinion on this point. His boyhood had been spent in those bitter days when social, political, and blood prejudices were fused at white heat in the public crucible together. When he went to the Church Seminary, it was a matter of course that every member of the faculty was a Republican, and that every one of his classmates had come from a Republican household. When, later on, he entered the ministry, the rule was still incredulous of exceptions. One might as well have looked in the Nedahma Conference for a divergence of opinion on the Trinity as for a difference in political conviction.

===Chinese===

他再次望着她，带一点局促的微笑，正式握了握她递来的戴手套的手，举了举软帽。正午强烈的日光从榆树间挤下来，打在她那把乳白镶花边的绸阳伞上，在她头发和脸的四周造出一圈光晕，又明艳，又温柔。他先前竟没看出她这样美。她点头还礼，转身走上草坪小径，肩上转着那把阳伞。
牧师宅虽只隔着三个街区，年轻的牧师到家之前，倒有工夫想了许多事。
首先，他得把自己关于爱尔兰人的观念，部分地重排一遍。除了农乡雇工里流动人口中偶尔一个孤僻寡言的身影，塞隆从前几乎同一个这种奇异的异族说过话。他此刻想起，泰尔也有十来户爱尔兰人家，窝在城郊砖窑中间；可他从未同其中任何一家打过交道，连他们存在这件事，也不曾给过一念。就个人相识而论，爱尔兰人于他只是一个名目。
可这又是何等阴森、何等可憎的名目！在这件大题目上，他的见解无非是他教会和他环境里人人共有的那套。他想当然地认定，譬如说，大城市里大多数的贫穷和全部的酗酒、犯罪和政治败坏，都出在这族外国人的乖劣品性上——而这品性又被一种虚假而拜偶像的宗教的恶毒影响，朝每一个恶的方向催化、加重。说他从未遇到过对此的异议，也不为过分。他的少年时代，正值那些苦涩的年月：社会的、政治的、血统的偏见，在公众的坩埚里烧到白热，熔作一炉。他进教会神学院时，教员清一色的共和党、同学清一色出自共和党人家，是理所当然的事。后来他入了教职，这规矩依然不容例外。要想在内达马议会里找出政治信念的分歧，正如想找出对三位一体的异见一样。

===Original===

Indeed, even among the laity, Theron could not feel sure that he had ever known a Democrat; that is, at all closely. He understood very little about politics, it is true. If he had been driven into a corner, and forced to attempt an explanation of this tremendous partisan unity in which he had a share, he would probably have first mentioned the War﻿—the last shots of which were fired while he was still in petticoats. Certainly his second reason, however, would have been that the Irish were on the other side.
He had never before had occasion to formulate, even in his own thoughts, this tacit race and religious aversion in which he had been bred. It rose now suddenly in front of him, as he sauntered from patch to patch of sunlight under the elms, like some huge, shadowy, and symbolic monument. He looked at it with wondering curiosity, as at something he had heard of all his life, but never seen before﻿—an abhorrent spectacle, truly! The foundations upon which its dark bulk reared itself were ignorance, squalor, brutality and vice. Pigs wallowed in the mire before its base, and burrowing into this base were a myriad of narrow doors, each bearing the hateful sign of a saloon, and giving forth from its recesses of night the sounds of screams and curses. Above were sculptured rows of lowering, apelike faces from Nast’s and Keppler’s cartoons, and out of these sprang into the vague upper gloom﻿—on the one side, lampposts from which negroes hung by the neck, and on the other gibbets for dynamiters and Molly Maguires, and between the two glowed a spectral picture of some black-robed tonsured men, with leering satanic masks, making a bonfire of the Bible in the public schools.
Theron stared this phantasm hard in the face, and recognized it for a very tolerable embodiment of what he had heretofore supposed he thought about the Irish. For an instant, the sight of it made him shiver, as if the sunny May had of a sudden lapsed back into bleak December. Then he smiled, and the bad vision went off into space. He saw instead Father Forbes, in the white and purple vestments, standing by poor MacEvoy’s bedside, with his pale, chiselled, luminous, uplifted face, and he heard only the proud, confident clanging of the girl’s recital﻿—*beatum Michaelem Archangelum, beatum Joannem Baptistam, Petrum et Paulum﻿—em!﻿—am!﻿—um!

===Chinese===

实在说，连在平信徒中间，塞隆也说不准自己认识过一个民主党人——至少没有亲熟的。诚然，他对政治懂得极少。倘若把他逼到墙角，要他解释这他又身与其中的惊人的一党一色，他多半头一条要提那场战争——末几响枪声放过时，他还穿着裙子呢。不过第二条理由必然是：爱尔兰人在对面那一党。
他一向没有机会把这自幼浸在其中的种族与宗教上的默默反感，哪怕在自己心里，理成个条贯。此刻他在榆树下一斑一斑的日影间信步，它突然立到他面前，像一座巨大、幽暗而寓意的碑。他怀着讶异的好奇打量它，像打量一件耳闻了一辈子、却从未见过的事物——真真一幅可憎的景象！它那黑沉沉的躯干所踞的基座，是愚昧、臅臅、横暴和罪恶。基座前的泥淖里猪在打滚；凿进基座的是无数扇窄门，门门挂着酒馆的可憎招牌，从各自的黑夜里放出尖叫和咒骂。再往上，浮雕塑着一列列凶横的、类人猿的脸，取材于纳斯特和凯普勒的漫画；从这些脸中间，向上方朦胧的幽暗里窜出——一边是从脖子上吊黑人的灯柱，另一边是绞死爆炸犯和莫莉·马奎尔分子的绞架，两者之间幽幽燃着一幅画：黑袍削发的人们戴着狞笑的撒旦面具，在公立学校里焚烧圣经。
塞隆狠狠盯住这幻影的脸，认出它正是他此前自以为对爱尔兰人所持之见的可观化身。有一瞬，这景象叫他打了个寒噪，仿佛晴明的五月蓦地退回了萧索的十二月。随后他微笑了，那坏景象便散入太空。他看见的是身披白紫祭衣的福布斯神父，立在可怜的麦克埃沃伊床前，苍白、轮廓分明、容光焕发地仰着一张脸；他听见的只有姑娘诵念的铿然自信——*beatum Michaelem Archangelum, beatum Joannem Baptistam, Petrum et Paulum——em！——am！——um！*

===Original===

*﻿—like strokes on a great resonant alarm-bell, attuned for the hearing of heaven. He caught himself on the very verge of feeling that heaven must have heard.
Then he smiled again, and laid the matter aside, with a parting admission that it had been undoubtedly picturesque and impressive, and that it had been a valuable experience to him to see it. At least the Irish, with all their faults, must have a poetic strain, or they would not have clung so tenaciously to those curious and ancient forms. He recalled having heard somewhere, or read, it might be, that they were a people much given to songs and music. And the young lady, that very handsome and friendly Miss Madden, had told him that she was a musician! He had a new pleasure in turning this over in his mind. Of all the closed doors which his choice of a career had left along his pathway, no other had for him such a magical fascination as that on which was graven the lute of Orpheus. He knew not even the alphabet of music, and his conceptions of its possibilities ran but little beyond the best of the hymn-singing he had heard at Conferences, yet none the less the longing for it raised on occasion such mutiny in his soul that more than once he had specifically prayed against it as a temptation.
Dangerous though some of its tendencies might be, there was no gainsaying the fact that a love for music was in the main an uplifting influence﻿—an attribute of cultivation. The world was the sweeter and more gentle for it. And this brought him to musing upon the odd chance that the two people of Octavius who had given him the first notion of polish and intellectual culture in the town should be Irish. The Romish priest must have been vastly surprised at his intrusion, yet had been at the greatest pains to act as if it were quite the usual thing to have Methodist ministers assist at Extreme Unction. And the young woman﻿—how gracefully, with what delicacy, had she comprehended his position and robbed it of all its possible embarrassments! It occurred to him that they must have passed, there in front of her home, the very tree from which the luckless wheelwright had fallen some hours before; and the fact that she had forborne to point it out to him took form in his mind as an added proof of her refinement of nature.

===Chinese===

*——像敲在一口为天庭的听觉而调准的洪亮警钟上。他发觉自己已到了这一步的边缘：觉得天庭必定已经听见了。
于是他又笑了笑，把这件事放下，临别承认一句：这无疑是有画意、有威仪的，见到它于他是一次宝贵的阅历。至少，爱尔兰人纵有千般毛病，必有一脉诗性，否则不会把那些古怪的旧形式抱得这样死。他记起在哪儿听人说过——也许是读到过——他们是个耽于歌乐的民族。而那位年轻的小姐，那位十分美丽、十分和气的马登小姐，方才亲口告诉他，她是搞音乐的！他把这个念头在心里翻来覆去，得到一种新的愉快。他择业之路在沿途关上的那一扇扇门里，再没有哪一扇像刻着俄耳甫斯琉特的那扇，对他有这样的魔力。他连音乐的字母都不识，对它可能性的想象，也超不出他在历届议会上听过的最好的赞美诗歌唱；然而对它的渴望，偶尔会在他灵魂里掀起那样的哗变，以致他不只一次专门祷告，求上帝把这渴望当作试探抵拒掉。
它的某些倾向尽管危险，有一条却是无可否认的：对音乐的爱好大体是一种向上的力量——一种教养的表征。世界因它而更甜美、更温厚。由此他又想到那桩巧事：奥克塔维乌斯城里最先给了他“文雅”和“智识教养”之感的两个人，竟都是爱尔兰人。那位罗马教神父对他的闯入必定大为诧异，却煞费苦心地做出一副样子，仿佛美以美会牧师出席终傅圣事本是家常便饭。而那位年轻女子——她体察他的处境，又把这处境可能有的种种难堪摘得干干净净，何等风致，何等细腻！他想到，方才在她家门前经过的那棵树，必就是几个钟头前那个倒霉车轮匠坠落的那棵；而她竟克制着没有指给他看——这一事实在他心里成形，又添了一条她天性高洁的证明。

===Original===

The midday dinner was a little more than ready when Theron reached home, and let himself in by the front door. On Mondays, owing to the moisture and clutter of the weekly washing in the kitchen, the table was laid in the sitting-room, and as he entered from the hall the partner of his joys bustled in by the other door, bearing the steaming platter of corned beef, dumplings, cabbages, and carrots, with arms bared to the elbows, and a red face. It gave him great comfort, however, to note that there were no signs of the morning’s displeasure remaining on this face; and he immediately remembered again those interrupted projects of his about the piano and the hired girl.
“Well! I’d just about begun to reckon that I was a widow,” said Alice, putting down her fragrant burden. There was such an obvious suggestion of propitiation in her tone that Theron went around and kissed her. He thought of saying something about keeping out of the way because it was “Blue Monday,” but held it back lest it should sound like a reproach.
“Well, what kind of a washerwoman does *this* one turn out to be?” he asked, after they were seated, and he had invoked a blessing and was cutting vigorously into the meat.
“Oh, so-so,” replied Alice; “she seems to be particular, but she’s mortal slow. If I hadn’t stood right over her, we shouldn’t have had the clothes out till goodness knows when. And of course she’s Irish!”
“Well, what of *that*?” asked the minister, with a fine unconcern.
Alice looked up from her plate, with knife and fork suspended in air. “Why, you know we were talking only the other day of what a pity it was that none of our own people went out washing,” she said. “That Welsh woman we heard of couldn’t come, after all; and they say, too, that she presumes dreadfully upon the acquaintance, being a church member, you know. So we simply *had* to fall back on the Irish. And even if they do go and tell their priest everything they see and hear, why, there’s one comfort, they can tell about *us* and welcome. Of course I see to it she doesn’t snoop around in here.”
Theron smiled. “That’s all nonsense about their telling such things to their priests,” he said with easy confidence.
“Why, you told me so yourself,” replied Alice, briskly. “And I’ve always understood so, too; they’re bound to tell *everything* in confession. That’s what gives the Catholic Church such a tremendous hold.

===Chinese===

塞隆到家、自己开前门进去时，午饭已经备得过了时候。星期一是每周洗衣的日子，厨房里又湿又乱，饭桌便摆在了起居室；他从门厅进去，他那同甘共苦的伴侣正从另一道门里忙碌着进来，端着一大盘冒热气的腕牛肉、面疙疸、卷心菜和胡萝卜，胳膊赤到肘弯，脸红红的。叫他很宽慰的是，这张脸上已寻不出早晨愠色的痕迹；他立刻又想起了那两桩被岔断的计划——钢琴和女仆。
“哎呀！我都开始盘算自己做了寡妇啦。”艾丽斯放下她那盘喷香的担子说。她话音里那股显而易见的求和的意思，使塞隆绕过去吻了她。他想说一句什么“蓝色星期一”躲在外面的缘故，又咽了回去，唯恐听来像责备。
“那么，*这位*洗衣妇怎么样？”两人落座、他作了谢饭祷、正起劲切肉时问。
“哦，马马虎虎，”艾丽斯答道，“瞧着还细心，就是慢得要命。我要不是站在她背后盯着，衣服还不知什么时候能晾出去呢。当然啦，她是爱尔兰人！”
“那又*怎么*呢？”牧师问，装出一副漂亮的漫不经心。
艾丽斯从盘子上抬起头，刀叉悬在半空。“嗐，咱们前几天不还说着，可惜咱们自己的人没有出去洗衣的么，”她说，“听说的那位威尔士女人到底还是来不成；再说人家还仗着是教会会友，跟你摆谱摆得厉害呢。所以咱们*只好*退而求其次用爱尔兰人。就算她们真会把看见听见的一切都告诉神父——喏，好歹有一点安慰：讲到*咱们*，尽他们讲去。当然我看着，不叫她在这些屋里乱翻。”
塞隆笑了。“说她们把这些事告诉神父，全是瞎扯。”他以轻松的自信说。
“嗐，这话是你自己对我说的呀，”艾丽斯利落地回敬，“我一向也是这么理解的；告解里他们什么都要*统统*说出来。这正是天主教会攥得这样死的一个大把柄。

===Original===

You’ve spoken of it often.”
“It must have been by way of a figure of speech,” remarked Theron, not with entire directness. “Women are great hands to separate one’s observations from their context, and so give them meanings quite unintended. They are also great hands,” he added genially, “or at least one of them is, at making the most delicious dumplings in the world. I believe these are the best even you ever made.”
Alice was not unmindful of the compliment, but her thoughts were on other things. “I shouldn’t like that woman’s priest, for example,” she said, “to know that we had no piano.”
“But if he comes and stands outside our house every night and listens﻿—as of course he will,” said Theron, with mock gravity, “it is only a question of time when he must reach that conclusion for himself. Our only chance, however, is that there are some sixteen hundred other houses for him to watch, so that he may not get around to us for quite a spell. Why, seriously, Alice, what on earth do you suppose Father Forbes knows or cares about our poor little affairs, or those of any other Protestant household in this whole village? He has his work to do, just as I have mine﻿—only his is ten times as exacting in everything except sermons﻿—and you may be sure he is only too glad when it is over each day, without bothering about things that are none of his business.”
“All the same I’m afraid of them,” said Alice, as if argument were exhausted.

===Chinese===

“这话你常说起的。”
“那多半是打个比方，”塞隆说，话说得不太直截。“女人个个是能手，专会把人的观察从它的上下文里剥出来，于是赋予它全非本意的意思。她们也个个是能手——”他和气地补上一句，“至少其中有一位是——做天下最好吃的面疙疸。我相信这是连你也从没做出过的最好的一次。”
艾丽斯没有辜负这句恭维，心思却在别处。“拿那女人的神父来说吧，”她说，“我就不愿意他知道咱们家没有钢琴。”
“可他要是夜夜来站在咱们房外听——他当然会来，”塞隆佯作庄重地说，“那他迟早自己得出这个结论，只是时间问题。咱们唯一的指望，是他还有一千六百来所别的房子要看，一时半会儿轮不到咱们这儿。说正经的，艾丽斯，你以为福布斯神父会知道、会在乎咱们这点可怜的私事，或者全村哪一户新教人家的私事么？他有他的工，正如我有我的——只是他的工除了讲道一项，样样比我的重十倍——你可以放心，他每天巴不得工完就歇，才不来操心那些不干他事的事。”
“反正我怕他们。”艾丽斯说，仿佛道理已经说尽。

### VI / 六
===Original===

On the following morning young Mr. Ware anticipated events by inscribing in his diary for the day, immediately after breakfast, these remarks: “Arranged about piano. Began work upon book.”
The date indeed deserved to be distinguished from its fellows. Theron was so conscious of its importance that he not only prophesied in the little morocco-bound diary which Alice had given him for Christmas, but returned after he had got out upon the front steps of the parsonage to have his hat brushed afresh by her.
“Wonders will never cease,” she said jocosely. “With you getting particular about your clothes, there isn’t anything in this wide world that can’t happen now!”
“One doesn’t go out to bring home a piano every day,” he made answer. “Besides, I want to make such an impression upon the man that he will deal gently with that first cash payment down. Do you know,” he added, watching her turn the felt brim under the wisp-broom’s strokes, “I’m thinking some of getting me a regular silk stovepipe hat.”
“Why don’t you, then?” she rejoined, but without any ring of glad acquiescence in her tone. He fancied that her face lengthened a little, and he instantly ascribed it to recollections of the way in which the roses had been bullied out of her own headgear.
“You are quite sure, now, pet,” he made haste to change the subject, “that the hired girl can wait just as well as not until fall?”
“Oh, *my*, yes!” Alice replied, putting the hat on his head, and smoothing back his hair behind his ears. “She’d only be in the way now. You see, with hot weather coming on, there won’t be much cooking. We’ll take all our meals out here, and that saves so much work that really what remains is hardly more than taking care of a birdcage. And, besides, not having her will almost half pay for the piano.”
“But when cold weather comes, you’re sure you’ll consent?” he urged.
“Like a shot!” she assured him, and, after a happy little caress, he started out again on his momentous mission.
Thurston’s was a place concerning which opinions differed in Octavius. That it typified progress, and helped more than any other feature of the village to bring it up to date, no one indeed disputed. One might move about a great deal, in truth, and hear no other view expressed.

===Chinese===

第二天早晨，年轻的韦尔先生抢在事实前头，一吃过早饭就在当天的日记里写下两行字：“钢琴事办妥。着手著书。”
这一天委实配得同它的弟兄们区别对待。塞隆自觉其重大，不光在艾丽斯圣诞节送他的那本摩洛哥皮小日记里作了预言，还出到牧师宅前门台阶上之后又折回来，让她把帽子再刷一遍。
“怪事真是没有个完，”她打趣道，“连你都对衣裳讲究起来了，这大千世界里还有什么事情不会发生呢！”
“一个人可不是天天出门去搬一架钢琴的，”他答道，“再说，我想给那家伙留个好印象，好叫他头一笔现付的手松些。你知道么，”他望着她在小笤帚下翻转着毡帽檐，补了一句，“我在想，要不要给自己置一顶正经的丝绸大礼帽。”
“那你怎么不置呢？”她回敬，话音里却没有欣然乐从的调子。他觉出她的脸拉长了一点，立刻把它归因于她记起了自己帽子上那些玫瑰被强令剪除的遭遇。
“你拿得准么，宝贝，”他赶忙换话头，“女仆的事，拖到秋天再雇也一点都不碍事？”
“哦，*我的*天，拿得准！”艾丽斯答道，把帽子给他戴上，把他耳后的头发抿平。“这会儿她来只会碍手碍脚。你瞧，天要热了，做饭的事不多。咱们把饭都摆在外头吃，这一省就省下多少活，剩下的那点，实在比照看一只鸟笼多不了什么。再说，不雇她，差不多能抵钢琴的一半价钱呢。”
“可等天冷了，你拿得准你会答应？”
“立马就答应！”她向他担保；他快活地亲了她一下，再度出发，去办他的大事。
瑟斯顿商行在奥克塔维乌斯是个见仁见智的地方。说它体现进步、比村里任何别的东西都更能叫它跟上时代，自然没人异议。确实，你尽可以四处走个遍，听不到第二种说法。

===Original===

But then again one might stumble into conversation with one small storekeeper after another, and learn that they united in resenting the existence of Thurston’s, as rival farmers might join to curse a protracted drought. Each had his special flaming grievance. The little dry-goods dealers asked mournfully how they could be expected to compete with an establishment which could buy bankrupt stocks at a hundred different points, and make a profit if only one-third of the articles were sold for more than they would cost from the jobber? The little boot and shoe dealers, clothiers, hatters, and furriers, the small merchants in carpets, crockery, and furniture, the venders of hardware and household utensils, of leathern goods and picture-frames, of wallpaper, musical instruments, and even toys﻿—all had the same pathetically unanswerable question to propound. But mostly they put it to themselves, because the others were at Thurston’s.
The Rev. Theron Ware had entertained rather strong views on this subject, and that only a week or two ago. One of his first acquaintances in Octavius had been the owner of the principal bookstore in the place﻿—a gentle and bald old man who produced the complete impression of a bibliophile upon what the slightest investigation showed to be only a meagre acquaintance with publishers’ circulars. But at least he had the air of loving his business, and the young minister had enjoyed a long talk with, or rather, at him. Out of this talk had come the information that the store was losing money. Not even the stationery department now showed a profit worth mentioning. When Octavius had contained only five thousand inhabitants, it boasted four bookstores, two of them good ones. Now, with a population more than doubled, only these latter two survived, and they must soon go to the wall. The reason? It was in a nutshell. A book which sold at retail for one dollar and a half cost the bookseller ninety cents. If it was at all a popular book, Thurston’s advertised it at eighty-nine cents﻿—and in any case at a profit of only two or three cents. Of course it was done to widen the establishment’s patronage﻿—to bring people into the store. Equally of course, it was destroying the book business and debauching the reading tastes of the community.

===Chinese===

可你也可能一个接一个撞进小铺店主们的谈话里，从而得知他们同心同德地怨恨着瑟斯顿商行的存在，一如相争的农人们会合力咒骂一场久旱。人人都有自己一桩火辣辣的血泪账。小布匹商们哀哀地问道：一家能在上百个地方吃进破产存货、哪怕只有三分之一的货卖价高出批发行进价也能赚钱的商号，叫他们怎么同它竞争？小靴鞋商、成衣商、帽商、皮货商，小小的地毯商、陶器商、家具商，五金和家用器皿的贩子，皮件和画框的贩子，墙纸、乐器乃至玩具的贩子——人人都有同一个可怜而无从回答的质问要提。可他们多半只提给自己听，因为旁人都在瑟斯顿商行里。
塞隆·韦尔牧师对这件事本来持着相当激烈的见解——而且就在一两个星期之前。他在奥克塔维乌斯最早结识的人里，有一位是本地头号书店的店主——一位和善的秃顶老人，浑身上下一副藏书家的气派，稍稍一查便知，那不过是同出版商行情单的一点可怜的交情。可他至少有一副热爱本行的样子，年轻的牧师同他——毋宁说对着他——畅谈过一场。从这场谈话里得到的信息是：书店在赔钱。如今连文具部也拿不出值得一提的利润了。当年奥克塔维乌斯只有五千居民时，撑着四家书店，其中两家像样的。如今人口翻了一倍还多，只剩这两家还开着，而且眼看也要倒。缘故？一句话就说尽。一本零售价一块半的书，书店进的价是九毛。这书只要稍微行时一点，瑟斯顿商行登广告只卖八毛九——无论如何，只赚两三分钱的利。这自然是为了拓宽商号的客路——把人引进店来。同样自然地，它正在毁掉书业，也在败坏全城的读书趣味。

===Original===

Without the profits from the light and ephemeral popular literature of the season, the bookstore proper could not keep up its stock of more solid works, and indeed could not long keep open at all. On the other hand, Thurston’s dealt with nothing save the demand of the moment, and offered only the books which were the talk of the week. Thus, in plain words, the book trade was going to the dogs, and it was the same with pretty nearly every other trade.
Theron was indignant at this, and on his return home told Alice that he desired her to make no purchases whatever at Thurston’s. He even resolved to preach a sermon on the subject of the modern idea of admiring the great for crushing the small, and sketched out some notes for it which he thought solved the problem of flaying the local abuse without mentioning it by name. They had lain on his desk now for ten days or more, and on only the previous Friday he had speculated upon using them that coming Sunday.
On this bright and cheerful Tuesday morning he walked with a blithe step unhesitatingly down the main street to Thurston’s, and entered without any show of repugnance the door next to the window wherein, flanked by dangling banjos and key-bugles built in pyramids, was displayed the sign, “Pianos on the Instalment Plan.”
He was recognized by some responsible persons, and treated with distinguished deference. They were charmed with the intelligence that he desired a piano, and fascinated by his wish to pay for it only a little at a time. They had special terms for clergymen, and made him feel as if these were being extended to him on a silver charger by kneeling admirers.
It was so easy to buy things here that he was a trifle disturbed to find his flowing course interrupted by his own entire ignorance as to what kind of piano he wanted. He looked at all they had in stock, and heard them played upon. They differed greatly in price, and, so he fancied, almost as much in tone. It discouraged him to note, however, that several of those he thought the finest in tone were among the very cheapest in the lot. Pondering this, and staring in hopeless puzzlement from one to another of the big black shiny monsters, he suddenly thought of something.
“I would rather not decide for myself,” he said, “I know so little about it. If you don’t mind, I will have a friend of mine, a skilled musician, step in and make a selection.

===Chinese===

没有应季轻俏流行读物的利润撑着，正经书店便养不住较为厚重的存货，实在说，连门也开不长。另一面，瑟斯顿商行只做眼下需求的生意，只摆正被街谈巷议的书。所以，说白了，书业正在完蛋，而且几乎行行都是如此。
塞隆对此义愤填膺，回家便告诉艾丽斯，他希望她无论如何不要在瑟斯顿商行买东西。他甚至决意就“推崇以大压小的现代观念”讲一篇道，拟了几条提纲，自以为既痛斥了本地时弊，又不必指名道姓。这提纲在他书桌上躺了十来天；就在上礼拜五，他还盘算着那个礼拜天就用来着。
而这个晴朗愉快的星期二早晨，他步履轻快、毫不犹豫地沿大街径直走进瑟斯顿商行，毫无嫌憎之色地进了那扇门——门旁橱窗里，两边是成金字塔堆着的班卓琴和有键小号，正中立着一块招牌：“钢琴分期付款”。
几个管事的认出了他，待他格外恭敬。听说他要买一架钢琴，他们喜出望外；听说他只想一次付一点点钱，他们更是心醉神迷。他们对教职人员另有优待，而且招待得他仿佛这些优待正由下跪的信众用银盘托着奉上来。
在这里买东西是这样容易，以致他为一件事微微犯了难：他全然不知道自己要哪种钢琴，滔滔的流程竟被打断了。他把店里的存货看了一个遍，又听人一一弹过。它们价钱悬殊，在他听来，音色的悬殊也几乎相当。可叫他泄气的是，几架他以为音色最美的，偏偏是这批里最便宜的。他琢磨着这个，在那些黑亮的大怪物之间左看右看、一筹莫展地干瞪眼，忽然想起一件事。
“我不愿意自己来定，”他说，“我懂得太少。你们要是不介意，我想请我的一位朋友——一位高明的音乐家——过来代选。我对——对*她*的判断力，信得很。”他慌忙补上一句，“这只耽搁一两天。”

===Original===

I have so much confidence in﻿—in *her* judgment.” He added hurriedly, “It will involve only a day or two’s delay.”
The next moment he was sorry he had spoken. What would they think when they saw the organist of the Catholic church come to pick out a piano for the Methodist parsonage? And how could he decorously prefer the request to her to undertake this task? He might not meet her again for ages, and to his provincial notions writing would have seemed out of the question. And would it not be disagreeable to have her know that he was buying a piano by part payments? Poor Alice’s dread of the washerwoman’s gossip occurred to him, at this, and he smiled in spite of himself. Then all at once the difficulty vanished. Of course it would come all right somehow. Everything did.
He was on firmer ground, buying the materials for the new book, over on the stationery side. His original intention had been to bestow this patronage upon the old bookseller, but these suavely smart people in Thurston’s had had the effect of putting him on his honor when they asked, “Would there be anything else?” and he had followed them unresistingly.
He indulged to the full his whim that everything entering into the construction of “Abraham” should be spick-and-span. He watched with his own eyes a whole ream of broad glazed white paper being sliced down by the cutter into single sheets, and thrilled with a novel ecstasy as he laid his hand upon the spotless bulk, so wooingly did it invite him to begin. He tried a score of pens before the right one came to hand. When a box of these had been laid aside, with ink and pen-holders and a little bronze inkstand, he made a sign that the outfit was complete. Or no﻿—there must be some blotting-paper. He had always used those blotting-pads given away by insurance companies﻿—his congregations never failed to contain one or more agents, who had these to bestow by the armful﻿—but the book deserved a virgin blotter.
Theron stood by while all these things were being tied up together in a parcel. The suggestion that they should be sent almost hurt him. Oh, no, he would carry them home himself.

===Chinese===

话一出口他就后悔了。等他们看见天主教教堂的琴师来给美以美会牧师宅挑钢琴，会作何想？而这桩请求，他又怎样体面地向她提出来呢？他也许多少年再见不到她；照他那乡气的观念，写信更是想也不用想。再说，让她知道他分期付款买琴，岂不尴尬？想到这里，可怜的艾丽斯对洗衣妇闲话的畏惧浮上心头，他不由自主地笑了。随即困难偸然消散。当然，车到山前必有路。事情向来如此。
到文具那边置办新书的行头，他脚下就踏实多了。他原打算把这份光顾留给那老书商的；可瑟斯顿商行这些殷勤伶俐的人问一声“还要点别的么”，便把他架在了体面上，他身不由己地跟了过去。
他纵着自己的痴念，叫凡是要进“亚伯拉罕”这项工程的东西，一律簇新雪亮。他亲眼看着一整令阔幅上光的白纸被铡刀裁成单页，把手按上那洁净无瑕的一摞时，涌起一阵新奇的狂喜——它那样诱人动笔。笔他试了二十来支，才试到合手的。等一盒笔连同墨水、笔杆和一只小铜墨水台搁在一边，他示意行头齐备了。哦不——还得有吸墨纸。他一向用保险公司白送的吸墨垫——他的会众里从来没有缺过一两个保险经纪人，这种垫子成抱地送——可这本书配得上一方未曾沾墨的新吸墨纸。
塞隆站在旁边，看这一切捆成一个包裹。伙计提议送货上门，几乎伤了他的心。哦，不必，他要亲自抱回家去。

===Original===

So strongly did they appeal to his sanguine imagination that he could not forbear hinting to the man who had shown him the pianos and was now accompanying him to the door that this package under his arm represented potentially the price of the piano he was going to have. He did it in a roundabout way, with one of his droll, hesitating smiles. The man did not understand at all, and Theron had not the temerity to repeat the remark. He strode home with the precious bundle as fast as he could.
“I thought it best, after all, not to commit myself to a selection,” he explained about the piano at dinnertime. “In such a matter as this, the opinion of an expert is everything. I am going to have one of the principal musicians of the town go and try them all, and tell me which we ought to have.”
“And while he’s about it,” said Alice, “you might ask him to make a little list of some of the new music. I’ve got way behind the times, being without a piano so long. Tell him not any *very* difficult pieces, you know.”
“Yes, I know,” put in Theron, almost hastily, and began talking of other things. His conversation was of the most rambling and desultory sort, because all the while the two lobes of his brain, as it were, kept up a dispute as to whether Alice ought to have been told that this “principal musician” was of her own sex. It would certainly have been better, at the outset, he decided; but to mention it now would be to invest the fact with undue importance. Yes, that was quite clear; only the clearer it became, from one point of view, the shadier it waxed from the other. The problem really disturbed the young minister’s mind throughout the meal, and his abstraction became so marked at last that his wife commented upon it.
“A penny for your thoughts!” she said, with cheerful briskness. This ancient formula of the farmland had always rather jarred on Theron. It presented itself now to his mind as a peculiarly aggravating banality.
“I am going to begin my book this afternoon,” he remarked impressively. “There is a great deal to think about.”
It turned out that there was even more to think about than he had imagined. After hours of solitary musing at his desk, or of pacing up and down before his open bookshelves, Theron found the first shadows of a May-day twilight beginning to fall upon that beautiful pile of white paper, still unstained by ink.

===Chinese===

这一抱在他乐观的想象里那样有分量，以致领他看过钢琴、此刻正送他到门口的那人面前，他忍不住拐弯抹角地暗示了一句：臂下这包东西，潜藏着那架他将要买的钢琴的价钱。他说这话时带着他那种迟疑的滑稽微笑。那人全然没有听懂，塞隆也没胆量再说一遍。他抱着这宝贵的包裹，脚下生风地尽快回了家。
“我想了想，到底还是先不定哪一架好，”吃午饭谈到钢琴时他解释道，“这样的事，行家的意见就是一切。我要请城里一位主要的音乐家去把琴都试一遍，告诉咱们该要哪一架。”
“他试琴的时候，”艾丽斯说，“你不妨请他顺手开一张单子，列几样新出的琴谱。我好久没有琴，落后时代太远了。告诉他别开*太*难的曲子，你知道。”
“是，我知道。”塞隆几乎慌忙地接口，谈起别的事来。他的谈话东拉西扯，因为整个这一顿饭，他那两半脑叶仿佛一直在争执：该不该告诉艾丽斯，这位“主要的音乐家”同她同性。一开头就说清，无疑更好，他裁定；可如今再提，反倒把这事实抬得过分要紧了。是的，这很清楚；只是从一面看越清楚，从另一面看越可疑。这个问题真真切切地搅了年轻牧师整顿饭的心神，末了他那心不在焉太扎眼，太太都议论起来了。
“一个铜板买你的心思！”她欢快麻利地说。这句农田里的老话一向叫塞隆听着硌耳；此刻在他心里，它更成了一句格外气人的陈词滥调。
“今天下午我就要动笔写书了，”他郑重其事地宣告，“要想的事多着呢。”
结果，要想的事比他想的还多。几个钟头独坐冥想，或是对着敞开的书架来回踱步之后，塞隆眼见五月日暮的第一批影子，开始落在那一大摞尚未沾墨的雪白纸上。

===Original===

He saw the book he wanted to write before him, in his mental vision, much more distinctly than ever, but the idea of beginning it impetuously, and hurling it off hot and glowing week by week, had faded away like a dream.
This long afternoon, spent face to face with a project born of his own brain but yesterday, yet already so much bigger than himself, was really a most fruitful time for the young clergyman. The lessons which cut most deeply into our consciousness are those we learn from our children. Theron, in this first day’s contact with the offspring of his fancy, found revealed to him an unsuspected and staggering truth. It was that he was an extremely ignorant and rudely untrained young man, whose pretensions to intellectual authority among any educated people would be laughed at with deserved contempt.
Strangely enough, after he had weathered the first shock, this discovery did not dismay Theron Ware. The very completeness of the conviction it carried with it, saturated his mind with a feeling as if the fact had really been known to him all along. And there came, too, after a little, an almost pleasurable sense of the importance of the revelation. He had been merely drifting in fatuous and conceited blindness. Now all at once his eyes were open; he knew what he had to do. Ignorance was a thing to be remedied, and he would forthwith bend all his energies to cultivating his mind till it should blossom like a garden. In this mood, Theron mentally measured himself against the more conspicuous of his colleagues in the Conference. They also were ignorant, clownishly ignorant: the difference was that they were doomed by native incapacity to go on all their lives without ever finding it out. It was obvious to him that his case was better. There was bright promise in the very fact that he had discovered his shortcomings.
He had begun the afternoon by taking down from their places the various works in his meagre library which bore more or less relation to the task in hand. The threescore books which constituted his printed possessions were almost wholly from the press of the Book Concern; the few exceptions were volumes which, though published elsewhere, had come to him through that giant circulating agency of the General Conference, and wore the stamp of its approval.

===Chinese===

他要写的那本书，在心里眼前，比先前任何时候都分明；可那种一鼓作气开笔、一周一周趁热抛出的念头，已经像梦一样散了。
这个漫长的下午，同他自己头脑昨天才产下的、却已比他本人大出许多的计划面对面度过，对这位年轻教士实在是一段极其多产的时光。刻进我们意识最深的功课，是从我们儿女身上学的功课。塞隆在同他幻想的产儿头一天的接触里，得知了一个不曾疑心、令人晕眩的真相：他是一个极其无知、教养粗陋的青年，他那一副要在受过教育的人们中间充任智识权威的做派，人家报之以应得的嗤笑，毫不过分。
说来奇怪，捱过头一记震动之后，这个发现并没有使塞隆·韦尔气馁。它带来的信念那样完整，反而浸透他的头脑，叫人觉得这事实他原就一贯知道似的。过了一会儿，又来了一种近乎愉快的、事关重大的觉悟感。他先前不过是在自欺欺人的盲目里随波逐流。如今眼睛一下子睁开了；他知道自己该干什么了。无知是一件可以补救的事，他将即刻倾其全力培育自己的头脑，直到它花开满园。乘着这股心气，塞隆在心里同比他显赫的几位议会同僚一一较量。他们也无知，村气地无知：分别只在他们受天生驽钝所判，一辈子也发现不了这一点。他的情形显然更好——单是他发现了自身的短缺这一事实，便含着光明的许诺。
这天下午他是这样开的头：从他那寒酸书架上，把同手头这件工作多少相干的几种书一一取下。构成他全部藏书的六十来册书，几乎尽出教会书局之门；寥寥几本例外，虽在别处出版，也是经总议会那个庞大的发行机构流到他手里的，带着它的许可印。

===Original===

Perhaps it was the sight of these half-filled shelves which started this day’s great revolution in Theron’s opinions of himself. He had never thought much before about owning books. He had been too poor to buy many, and the conditions of canvassing about among one’s parishioners which the thrifty Book Concern imposes upon those who would have without buying, had always repelled him. Now, suddenly, as he moved along the two shelves, he felt ashamed at their beggarly showing.
*The Land and the Book*, in three portly volumes, was the most pretentious of the aids which he finally culled from his collection. Beside it he laid out *Bible Lands*, *Rivers and Lakes of Scripture*, *Bible Manners and Customs*, the “Genesis and Exodus” volume of Whedon’s *Commentary*, some old numbers of the *Methodist Quarterly Review*, and a copy of *Josephus* which had belonged to his grandmother, and had seen him through many a weary Sunday afternoon in boyhood. He glanced casually through these, one by one, as he took them down, and began to fear that they were not going to be of so much use as he had thought. Then, seating himself, he read carefully through the thirteen chapters of Genesis which chronicle the story of the founder of Israel.
Of course he had known this story from his earliest years. In almost every chapter he came now upon a phrase or an incident which had served him as the basis for a sermon. He had preached about Hagar in the wilderness, about Lot’s wife, about the visit of the angels, about the intended sacrifice of Isaac, about a dozen other things suggested by the ancient narrative. Somehow this time it all seemed different to him. The people he read about were altered to his vision. Heretofore a poetic light had shone about them, where indeed they had not glowed in a halo of sanctification. Now, by some chance, this light was gone, and he saw them instead as untutored and unwashed barbarians, filled with animal lusts and ferocities, struggling by violence and foul chicanery to secure a foothold in a country which did not belong to them﻿—all rude tramps and robbers of the uncivilized plain.
The apparent fact that Abram was a Chaldean struck him with peculiar force. How was it, he wondered, that this had never occurred to him before?

===Chinese===

也许正是这几架半空的书架，发动了这一天塞隆自我评估的大革命。他从前不大想藏书的事。他穷，买不起多少；而节俭的教会书局强加给那些“不买而欲有”之人的规矩——得在教民中间挨家兜售——又素来叫他退避。此刻，沿这两架书走着走着他忽然羞愧起来，为它们的寒碜。
《圣地与圣经》三厚册，是他终于从藏书中拣出的帮手里最气派的一部。同它摆在一起的，有《圣经地域》《圣经的江河湖泊》《圣经时代的风俗》，惠登《圣经注释》的“创世记与出埃及记”卷，几期旧的《美以美会季刊》，还有一部他祖母传下的约瑟夫斯——少年时代多少个难熬的礼拜天下昼，都是它陪他打发的。他取书时随手一页页翻过，开始担心它们不会像他想的那样顶用了。随后他坐下来，把载录以色列始祖故事的《创世记》十三章，从头至尾细读了一遍。
这故事他自然从幼年就熟知。几乎每一章里，他此刻都撞见一句话或一件事，曾被他用作一篇讲道的基础。他讲过旷野里的夏甲，讲过罗得的妻子，讲过天使的来访，讲过献以撒的本意，还有古史叙事提示出的十来样别的事。不知怎么，这一回，这一切在他眼里全变了样。他读到的那些人物，在他眼中改了形。此前，一层诗意的光罩着他们，说不得还各有一圈圣化的光轮。如今不知怎的，这光没有了，他看见的是一群未开化、不洗澡的蛮子，满身兽欲和凶悍，靠暴力和卑污的诈术，在一块不属于他们的土地上抢立足之地——全是那片未开化平原上的粗野流寇和劫盗。
亚伯兰原来是个迦勒底人——这个表面的事实给他特异的一击。怎么回事呢，他纳闷，这一点他从前怎么从来没有想到过？

===Original===

Examining himself, he found that he had supposed vaguely that there had been Jews from the beginning, or at least, say, from the flood. But, no, Abram was introduced simply as a citizen of the Chaldean town of Ur, and there was no hint of any difference in race between him and his neighbors. It was specially mentioned that his brother, Lot’s father, died in Ur, the city of his nativity. Evidently the family belonged there, and were Chaldeans like the rest.
I do not cite this as at all a striking discovery, but it did have a curious effect upon Theron Ware. Up to that very afternoon, his notion of the kind of book he wanted to write had been founded upon a popular book called *Ruth the Moabitess*, written by a clergyman he knew very well, the Rev. E. Ray Mifflin. This model performance troubled itself not at all with difficult points, but went swimmingly along through scented summer seas of pretty rhetoric, teaching nothing, it is true, but pleasing a good deal and selling like hot cakes. Now, all at once Theron felt that he hated that sort of book. *His* work should be of a vastly different order. He might fairly assume, he thought, that if the fact that Abram was a Chaldean was new to him, it would fall upon the world in general as a novelty. Very well, then, there was his chance. He would write a learned book, showing who the Chaldeans were, and how their manners and beliefs differed from, and influenced﻿—
It was at this psychological instant that the wave of self-condemnation suddenly burst upon and submerged the young clergyman. It passed again, leaving him staring fixedly at the pile of books he had taken down from the shelves, and gasping a little, as if for breath. Then the humorous side of the thing, perversely enough, appealed to him, and he grinned feebly to himself at the joke of his having imagined that he could write learnedly about the Chaldeans, or anything else. But, no, it shouldn’t remain a joke! His long mobile face grew serious under the new resolve. He would learn what there was to be learned about the Chaldeans. He rose and walked up and down the room, gathering fresh strength of purpose as this inviting field of research spread out its vistas before him. Perhaps﻿—yes, he would incidentally explore the mysteries of the Moabitic past as well, and thus put the Rev. E. Ray Mifflin to confusion on his own subject.

===Chinese===

他反省自己，发觉自己一直含糊地以为，犹太人是从开天辟地就有的，或者至少，比如说，从大洪水起就有的。可是不对，亚伯兰出场时，简简单单就是迦勒底城吾珥的一个市民，同他的邻居之间，没有一点种族不同的暗示。书上还特意提到，他弟弟、罗得的父亲，死在本地本城吾珥。这一家显然是土著，同旁人一样是迦勒底人。
我举这个，并不是说它是什么惊人的发现；可它对塞隆·韦尔确有一种奇效。直到那天下午为止，他心目中想写的那一类书，是以一本风行的《摩押女子路得》为蓝本的——作者是一位他相熟的教士，E. 雷·米夫林牧师。这件范本之作全不为难点操心，一路顺水扬帆，驶过辞藻芬芳的夏海；固然教不了人什么，却讨人欢喜，卖得飞快。此刻，塞隆蓦地觉得自己憎恶那一类书了。*他的*著作必须是另一个等次的东西。他未尝不可以假定：既然“亚伯兰是迦勒底人”这事实于他是新闻，那么放到普天下，也该是一桩新奇闻。好，机会就在这里。他要写一本渊博的书，讲明迦勒底人是谁，他们的风俗和信仰怎样不同于——又怎样影响了——
正是这个心理的当口，自我谴责的浪头蓦地砸下来，把年轻的教士整个淹没。浪头又过去了，剩下他直瞪瞪望着他从架上取下的那摸书，微微喘息，像换不上气来。随后，这件事滑稽的一面——偏是滑稽的一面——又占了上风，他想到自己竟想象能就迦勒底人、就随便什么写出渊博文字的这桩笑话，自己明嘴无力地笑了笑。可是不，这事不能只当笑话！他那张长长的、富于变化的脸在新决意之下严肃起来。他要学会关于迦勒底人一切可学的东西。他站起身，在屋里来回踱步，这片诱人的研究园地在他眼前次第铺展，他的意志也随之汲取新的力量。说不定——对，他还顺带要探一探摩押往事的种种秘辛，这样好在米夫林牧师本人的题目上叫他丢丑。

===Original===

That would in itself be a useful thing, because Mifflin wore kid gloves at the Conference, and affected an intolerable superiority of dress and demeanor, and there would be general satisfaction among the plainer and worthier brethren at seeing him taken down a peg.
Now for the first time there rose distinctly in Theron’s mind that casual allusion which Father Forbes had made to the Turanians. He recalled, too, his momentary feeling of mortification at not knowing who the Turanians were, at the time. Possibly, if he had probed this matter more deeply, now as he walked and pondered in the little living-room, he might have traced the whole of the afternoon’s mental experiences to that chance remark of the Romish priest. But this speculation did not detain him. He mused instead upon the splendid library Father Forbes must have.
“Well, how does the book come on? Have you got to ‘my Lady Keturah’ yet?’ ”
It was Alice who spoke, opening the door from the kitchen, and putting in her head with a pretence of great and solemn caution, but with a correcting twinkle in her eyes.
“I haven’t got to anybody yet,” answered Theron, absently. “These big things must be approached slowly.”
“Come out to supper, then, while the beans are hot,” said Alice.
The young minister sat through this other meal, again in deep abstraction. His wife pursued her little pleasantry about Keturah, the second wife, urging him with mock gravity to scold her roundly for daring to usurp Sarah’s place, but Theron scarcely heard her, and said next to nothing. He ate sparingly, and fidgeted in his seat, waiting with obvious impatience for the finish of the meal. At last he rose abruptly.
“I’ve got a call to make﻿—something with reference to the book,” he said. “I’ll run out now, I think, before it gets dark.”
He put on his hat, and strode out of the house as if his errand was of the utmost urgency. Once upon the street, however, his pace slackened. There was still a good deal of daylight outside, and he loitered aimlessly about, walking with bowed head and hands clasped behind him, until dusk fell. Then he squared his shoulders, and started straight as the crow flies toward the residence of Father Forbes.

===Chinese===

这本身就是一件有益的事，因为米夫林在议会上戴小山羊皮手套，摆出一副衣着一举止都高人一等的可憎架子；见他被削一削威风，朴素可敬的弟兄们必定人人心快。
此时，福布斯神父随口提到的图兰人，头一回清晰地浮上塞隆的心头。他也记起当时那一闪而过的羞惭——因为不知图兰人是谁。倘若此刻他在小起居室里边踱边想时把这件事探得更深些，他也许会把整个下午的心路，都追溯到那位罗马教神父那句偶然的话上去。可这层思辨没有绊住他。他转而遐想起福布斯神父必有的那座堂皇的书斋来。
“嗐，书写得怎么样了？写到‘基土拉夫人’了没有？”
说话的是艾丽斯，她从厨房推开门，探进头来，装出千郑万重的谨慎模样，眼里却亮着补上一刀的促狭。
“我谁也还没写到呢，”塞隆心不在焉地答，“这样的大事，得慢慢接近。”
“那就出来吃晚饭吧，豆子还热着。”艾丽斯说。
年轻的牧师又一顿饭从头坐到尾，照旧深深出神。太太接着拿基土拉——那第二位妻子——寻她的开心，装出十二分的庄重，怂恿他去把那位胆敢篡夺撒拉位置的夫人痛骂一顿；塞隆几乎没有听见，几乎一言不发。他吃得很少，在座位上坐立不安，明明白白等得不耐烦，直等饭毕。末了他霍地站起来。
“我还有一次拜访要作——同书有关的事，”他说，“我想趁天没黑，这就出去一趟。”
他戴上帽子，大步冲出屋去，仿佛身负十万火急的差事。可一上街，他的脚步就慢了下来。外面天光还亮得很，他无所事事地闲荡，低着头，两手扣在背后，直到暮色四合。这时他一挺肩膀，取直线如乌鸦般直奔福布斯神父的寓所而去。

### VII / 七
===Original===

The new Catholic church was the largest and most imposing public building in Octavius. Even in its unfinished condition, with a bald roofing of weather-beaten boards marking on the stunted tower the place where a spire was to begin later on, it dwarfed every other edifice of the sort in the town, just as it put them all to shame in the matter of the throngs it drew, rain or shine, to its services.
These facts had not heretofore been a source of satisfaction to the Rev. Theron Ware. He had even alluded to the subject in terms which gave his wife the impression that he actively deplored the strength and size of the Catholic denomination in this new home of theirs, and was troubled in his mind about Rome generally. But this evening he walked along the extended side of the big structure, which occupied nearly half the block, and then, turning the corner, passed in review its wide-doored, looming front, without any hostile emotions whatever. In the gathering dusk it seemed more massive than ever before, but he found himself only passively considering the odd statement he had heard that all Catholic Church property was deeded absolutely in the name of the Bishop of the diocese.
Only a narrow passageway separated the church from the pastorate﻿—a fine new brick residence standing flush upon the street. Theron mounted the steps, and looked about for a bellpull. Search revealed instead a little ivory button set in a ring of metal work. He picked at this for a time with his fingernail, before he made out the injunction, printed across it, to push. Of course! how stupid of him! This was one of those electric bells he had heard so much of, but which had not as yet made their way to the class of homes he knew. For custodians of a medieval superstition and fanaticism, the Catholic clergy seemed very much up to date. This bell made him feel rather more a countryman than ever.
The door was opened by a tall gaunt woman, who stood in black relief against the radiance of the hallway while Theron, choosing his words with some diffidence, asked if the Rev. Mr. Forbes was in.
“He is,” came the hush-voiced answer. “He’s at dinner, though.”
It took the young minister a second or two to bring into association in his mind this evening hour and this midday meal.

===Chinese===

新的天主教堂是奥克塔维乌斯最大、最气派的公共建筑。纵然还未完工——矮塔上一秃风雨剥蚀的木板顶，标出日后尖塔起建的位置——它已叫全城同类建筑一概成了侏儒；正如无论晴雨，它招到礼拜来的人潮，也令它们一概蒙羞。
这些事实，此前在塞隆·韦尔牧师心里可从来不曾叫他快意过。他甚至提起这话题时的口气，给他太太的印象是：他着实痛心于天主教势力在新家乡的这般壮大，心里为罗马教廷整个地犯愁。可今晚，他沿着这座大建筑绵长的一侧走去——它差不多占了半个街区——转过街角，又把那正面宽大的门廊检阅了一遍，心里竟无一丝敌意。渐浓的暮色里，它比先前愈发显得厚重；可他发现自己只在被动地琢磨方才听到的一句怪话：天主教会的一切产业，都绝对地立契在教区主教一人名下。
教堂同牧馆之间只隔一条窄窄的过道——牧馆是一座紧贴街面的漂亮新砖宅。塞隆登上台阶，四下找门铃拉手。找了半天，却只寻着一个小小的象牙色按钮，嵌在一圈金属细工里。他用指甲抠了它半天，才认出那上面印着的吩咐：请按。可不是么！他多蠢！这就是他常听人说起的电铃，只是还没传进他所熟识的那一等人家。作为中世纪迷信与狂热的看守人，天主教教士倒似乎很赶时髦。这枚铃叫他比先前更觉得自己是个乡巴佬。
开门的是个瘦高个女人，立在门厅亮光的衬托里，一身黑；塞隆字斟句酌、带着几分怯意地问，福布斯牧师先生在不在。
「在，」回答是压低嗓子的。「不过他在吃晚饭。」
年轻的牧师花了一两秒钟，才把这傍晚的时辰同这顿正午的饭联系到一起。

===Original===

Then he began to say that he would call again﻿—it was nothing special﻿—but the woman suddenly cut him short by throwing the door wide open.
“It’s Mr. Ware, is it not?” she asked, in a greatly altered tone. “Sure, he’d not have you go away. Come inside﻿—do, sir!﻿—I’ll tell him.”
Theron, with a dumb show of reluctance, crossed the threshold. He noted now that the woman, who had bustled down the hall on her errand, was gray-haired and incredibly ugly, with a dark sour face, glowering black eyes, and a twisted mouth. Then he saw that he was not alone in the hallway. Three men and two women, all poorly clad and obviously working people, were seated in meek silence on a bench beyond the hat-rack. They glanced up at him for an instant, then resumed their patient study of the linoleum pattern on the floor at their feet.
“And will you kindly step in, sir?” the elderly Gorgon had returned to ask. She led Mr. Ware along the hallway to a door near the end, and opened it for him to pass before her.
He entered a room in which for the moment he could see nothing but a central glare of dazzling light beating down from a great shaded lamp upon a circular patch of white table linen. Inside this ring of illumination points of fire sparkled from silver and porcelain, and two bars of burning crimson tracked across the cloth in reflection from tall glasses filled with wine. The rest of the room was vague darkness; but the gloom seemed saturated with novel aromatic odors, the appetizing scent of which bore clear relation to what Theron’s blinking eyes rested upon.
He was able now to discern two figures at the table, outside the glowing circle of the lamp. They had both risen, and one came toward him with cordial celerity, holding out a white plump hand in greeting. He took this proffered hand rather limply, not wholly sure in the half-light that this really was Father Forbes, and began once more that everlasting apology to which he seemed doomed in the presence of the priest. It was broken abruptly off by the other’s protesting laughter.
“My dear Mr. Ware, I beg of you,” the priest urged, chuckling with hospitable mirth, “don’t, don’t apologize! I give you my word, nothing in the world could have pleased us better than your joining us here tonight. It was quite dramatic, your coming in as you did. We were speaking of you at that very moment.

===Chinese===

随后他开口说改天再来——也没有什么要紧事——那女人却猛地把门敞开，打断了他。
「是韦尔先生，对吧？」她问，口音全然变了。「真的，他不会让您走的。进来——请进，先生！——我去告诉他。」
塞隆半推半就，做了一阵推辞的哑剧，跨过了门槛。这时他才看清，那已顺着门厅匆匆去了的女人，头发灰白，丑得出奇，一张黑黄的酸脸，两只乌黑的怒眼，一张歪嘴。随后他发现门厅里并非只有他。三个男人两个女人，衣着寒素，一望即知是做工的，正温顺无声地坐在衣帽架那头的长凳上。他们抬眼看了他一瞬，又低头去耐心研究脚前地板的油毡花样。
「请您这边来，先生？」那位上了年纪的戈耳工回来请他了。她领着韦尔先生沿门厅走到将近尽头的一扇门前，开门请他先行。
他进了一间屋子；一时之间，他什么也看不见，只看见正中一泓炫目的光，从一盏大灯罩的灯里泼下来，打在白桌布的一轮圆斑上。光环之内，银器瓷器上迸着点点火花；两只斟满酒的高脚杯，把两道烧红的紫红映上桌布。屋子其余处是模糊的黑；可这昏暗里浸透了新异的香气，那开胃的香味，同塞隆眨着眼所见到的东西，分明大有关连。
现在他辨出灯光圈外的桌旁有两个人影。两人都已起身；一位带着热诚的疾步迎上来，伸出一只白胖的手来握。他软塌塌地接了这只手——半明半暗里他还拿不准这是不是真是福布斯神父——又开始那套没完没了的道歉：在这位神父面前，他仿佛命定要道歉。对方笑着抗议，把它拦腰截断了。
「亲爱的韦尔先生，我求您，」神父恳切地说，带着好客的笑，「别道歉，千万别！我向您担保，今晚您来同我们做伴，世上再没有比这更叫我们高兴的了。您这样走进来，简直富有戏剧性。我们那一刻恰恰正谈着您。

===Original===

Oh, I forgot﻿—let me make you acquainted with my friend﻿—my very particular friend, Dr. Ledsmar. Let me take your hat; pray draw up a chair. Maggie will have a place laid for you in a minute.”
“Oh, I assure you﻿—I couldn’t think of it﻿—I’ve just eaten my﻿—my﻿—dinner,” expostulated Theron. He murmured more inarticulate remonstrances a moment later, when the grim old domestic appeared with plates, serviette, and tableware for his use, but she went on spreading them before him as if she heard nothing. Thus committed against a decent show of resistance, the young minister did eat a little here and there of what was set before him, and was human enough to regret frankly that he could not eat more. It seemed to him very remarkable cookery, transfiguring so simple a thing as a steak, for example, quite out of recognition, and investing the humble potato with a charm he had never dreamed of. He wondered from time to time if it would be polite to ask how the potatoes were cooked, so that he might tell Alice.
The conversation at the table was not continuous, or even enlivened. After the lapses into silence became marked, Theron began to suspect that his refusal to drink wine had annoyed them﻿—the more so as he had drenched a large section of tablecloth in his efforts to manipulate a siphon instead. He was greatly relieved, therefore, when Father Forbes explained in an incidental way that Dr. Ledsmar and he customarily ate their meals almost without a word.
“It’s a philosophic fad of his,” the priest went on smilingly, “and I have fallen in with it for the sake of a quiet life; so that when we do have company﻿—that is to say, once in a blue moon﻿—we display no manners to speak of.”
“I had always supposed﻿—that is, I’ve always heard﻿—that it was more healthful to talk at meals,” said Theron. “Of course﻿—what I mean﻿—I took it for granted all physicians thought so.”
Dr. Ledsmar laughed. “That depends so much upon the quality of the meals!” he remarked, holding his glass up to the light.
He seemed a man of middle age and an equable disposition. Theron, stealing stray glances at him around the lampshade, saw most distinctly of all a broad, impressive dome of skull, which, though obviously the result of baldness, gave the effect of quite belonging to the face.

===Chinese===

哦，我忘了——让我引您认识我的朋友——我特别要好的朋友，莱兹马医生。帽子给我；请搬把椅子过来。玛吉一转眼就给您摆好位子。」
「哦，真的——这怎么好——我刚吃过我的——我的——晚饭，」塞隆分辩道。片刻后，那面目冷峻的老女仆端着盘子、餐巾和刀叉来给他摆开，他又含混地推让了几句；她只管一样样摆下去，仿佛什么也没听见。这样推却得差不多了，年轻的牧师也就对着摆上来的东西东吃一点西吃一点；到底还是凡人，吃不下更多，他痛痛快快地引为懳事。这手艺在他看来出奇：一块牛排这样简单的东西，竟烧得改了模样；家常的土豆，竟带上他做梦也想不到的一种魅力。他不时盘算：问一问这土豆是怎样烧的，算不算失礼——好回去告诉艾丽斯。
席间的谈话并不连贯，甚至谈不上活跃。冷场多了，塞隆开始疑心是自己谢绝喝酒惹恼了他们——更何况他摆弄汽水枪，还泼湿了好大一块桌布。所以，当福布斯神父顺口解释说，莱兹马医生和他惯常吃饭几乎不交一语时，他大大松了口气。
「这是他的一个哲学怪癖，」神父含笑接着说，「为了清静，我也就将就了；所以偶尔有客——也就是说，千载难逢——我们也就谈不上什么礼数了。」
「我一向以为——就是说，常听人说——吃饭时说话更合卫生，」塞隆说。「当然——我的意思是——我原以为医生们都是这个看法。」
莱兹马医生笑了。「那得看饭菜是什么成色！」他说，举杯对着灯照。
他像是中年人，性情平稳。塞隆隔着灯罩偷觑他几眼，看得最分明的是那又宽又气派的穹顶般的头颅——虽分明是秃出来的，效果上却仿佛天生该属于这张脸。

===Original===

There were gold-rimmed spectacles, through which shone now and again the vivid sparkle of sharp, alert eyes, and there was a nose of some sort not easy to classify, at once long and thick. The rest was thin hair and short round beard, mouse-colored where the light caught them, but losing their outlines in the shadows of the background. Theron had not heard of him among the physicians of Octavius. He wondered if he might not be a doctor of something else than medicine, and decided upon venturing the question.
“Oh, yes, it is medicine,” replied Ledsmar. “I am a doctor three or four times over, so far as parchments can make one. In some other respects, though, I should think I am probably less of a doctor than anybody else now living. I haven’t practised﻿—that is, regularly﻿—for many years, and I take no interest whatever in keeping abreast of what the profession regards as its progress. I know nothing beyond what was being taught in the sixties, and that I am glad to say I have mostly forgotten.”
“Dear me!” said Theron. “I had always supposed that Science was the most engrossing of pursuits﻿—that once a man took it up he never left it.”
“But that would imply a connection between Science and Medicine!” commented the doctor. “My dear sir, they are not even on speaking terms.”
“Shall we go upstairs?” put in the priest, rising from his chair. “It will be more comfortable to have our coffee there﻿—unless indeed, Mr. Ware, tobacco is unpleasant to you?”
“Oh, my, no!” the young minister exclaimed, eager to free himself from the suggestion of being a killjoy. “I don’t smoke myself; but I am very fond of the odor, I assure you.”
Father Forbes led the way out. It could be seen now that he wore a long house-gown of black silk, skilfully moulded to his erect, shapely, and rounded form. Though he carried this with the natural grace of a proud and beautiful belle, there was no hint of the feminine in his bearing, or in the contour of his pale, firm-set, handsome face. As he moved through the hallway, the five people whom Theron had seen waiting rose from their bench, and two of the women began in humble murmurs, “If you please, Father,” and “Good evening to your Riverence;” but the priest merely nodded and passed on up the staircase, followed by his guests. The people sat down on their bench again.

===Chinese===

一副金丝边眼镜，镜片后不时闪出锐利警觉的眼里的亮光；还有一只难以归类的鼻子，既长且厚。其余便是稀疏的头发和短短的圆须，灯光照着的地方是鼠灰色，其余处轮廓都消失在背景的暗影里。奥克塔维乌斯的医生里，塞隆没听说过他。他捉摸这人兴许不是医学的博士，而是别的什么博士，决意斗胆一问。
「哦，是，是医学，」莱兹马答。「凭羊皮纸文书算，我是三四个来回的博士。不过换几个方面说，我想我大概是当今活着的人里最不博士的一个。我不行医——正经行医——已多年了；同行们所谓的进步，我也毫无兴趣紧跟。六十年代教的那些以外我一概不知；那一点，谢天谢地，也忘得差不多了。」
「哎呀！」塞隆说。「我一向以为科学是最迷人的事业——人一旦沾上，就再也放不下。」
「那岂不是说科学与医学之间有瓜葛！」医生议论道。「亲爱的先生，它们连说话的交情都没有。」
「上楼去坐好不好？」神父插进来，离了座。「咖啡在楼上喝更舒服——韦尔先生当然——除非烟草味儿叫您不快？」
「哦，哪里的话！」年轻的牧师叫起来，急于洗脱败兴的嫌疑。「我自己不抽；可我极爱闻那个味儿，真的。」
福布斯神父领头出去。此刻看得出，他身上穿一件黑绸长袍，精巧地贴着他挺拔、匀称、丰润的身形。他穿着它，带着骄傲的美人儿那份天然的绰约，可无论举止、无论那张苍白、沉稳、俊美的脸的轮廓，都没有一丝女人的气。他穿过门厅时，塞隆见过的那五个等候的人从长凳上站起，两个女人低声下气地开始说「劳驾您了，神父」「神父大人晚上好」；神父只点了点头，径自上楼，客人们随后。那些人又坐回长凳上去。

===Original===

A few minutes later, reclining at his ease in a huge low chair, and feeling himself unaccountably at home in the most luxuriously appointed and delightful little room he had ever seen, the Rev. Theron Ware sipped his unaccustomed coffee and embarked upon an explanation of his errand. Somehow the very profusion of scholarly symbols about him﻿—the great dark rows of encased and crowded bookshelves rising to the ceiling, the classical engravings upon the wall, the revolving bookcase, the reading-stand, the mass of littered magazines, reviews, and papers at either end of the costly and elaborate writing-desk﻿—seemed to make it the easier for him to explain without reproach that he needed information about Abram. He told them quite in detail the story of his book.
The two others sat watching him through a faint haze of scented smoke, with polite encouragement on their faces. Father Forbes took the added trouble to nod understandingly at the various points of the narrative, and when it was finished gave one of his little approving chuckles.
“This skirts very closely upon sorcery,” he said smilingly. “Do you know, there is perhaps not another man in the country who knows Assyriology so thoroughly as our friend here, Dr. Ledsmar.”
“That’s putting it too strong,” remarked the Doctor. “I only follow at a distance﻿—a year or two behind. But I daresay I can help you. You are quite welcome to anything I have: my books cover the ground pretty well up to last year. Delitzsch is very interesting; but Baudissin’s *Studien zur Semitischen Religionsgeschichte* would come closer to what you need. There are several other important Germans﻿—Schrader, Bunsen, Duncker, Hommel, and so on.”
“Unluckily I﻿—I don’t read German readily,” Theron explained with diffidence.
“That’s a pity,” said the doctor, “because they do the best work﻿—not only in this field, but in most others. And they do so much that the mass defies translation. Well, the best thing outside of German of course is Sayce. I daresay you know him, though.”
The Rev. Mr. Ware shook his head mournfully. “I don’t seem to know anyone,” he murmured.
The others exchanged glances.
“But if I may ask, Mr. Ware,” pursued the doctor, regarding their guest with interest through his spectacles, “why do you specially hit upon Abraham? He is full of difficulties﻿—enough, just now, at any rate, to warn off the bravest scholar.

===Chinese===

几分钟后，塞隆·韦尔牧师舒舒坦坦地半躺进一把低矮的大椅，在他生平所见陈设最奢华、最惬意的小间里，莫名地觉得宾至如归；他啜着不惯喝的咖啡，开始交代来意。不知怎么，四壁那一派学问的象喻——一排排顶到天花板的深色玻璃书柜，墙上的古典版画，旋转书架，读经架，贵重而精工的书桌两端堆乱的杂志、评论和报纸——倒叫他不带一点愧色地说明自己需要关于亚伯兰的资料，来得格外容易。他把那部书的事原原本本讲给他们听。
那两位坐在一带香喷喷的淡烟后面望着他，脸上挂着客气的鼓励。福布斯神父还格外费神，在叙述的各处点头会意；听完了，发出他那种小小的赞许的笑。
「这就快挨着法术的边了，」他含笑说。「您可知道，论亚述学之精熟，全国恐怕再没有第二个人赶得上我们这位朋友莱兹马医生。」
「言重了，」医生说。「我不过在后面远远跟着——落后一两年。不过我想我帮得上您。我有的您尽管用：我的书，到去年为止，把这地界铺得相当全了。德利奇很有意思；但鲍德辛的《闪族宗教史研究》更贴您的需要。还有几位要紧的德国人——施拉德、本森、邓克、霍梅尔等等。」
「不巧我——我不大读得来德文，」塞隆怯怯地解释。
「可惜了，」医生说，「最好的活儿都是他们做的——不止这一门，多半的门都如此。而且他们做得太多，量太大，翻译不胜翻。那么，德文以外最好的自然要数塞斯了。您想必认得他。」
韦尔牧师苦苦摇头。「我好像谁也不认得，」他喃喃说。
两位交换了一个眼色。
「不过恕我问一句，韦尔先生，」医生隔着眼镜饶有兴味地端详客人，接着说，「您为什么单单挑中亚伯拉罕？他一身都是难处——至少眼下，足以吓退最大胆的学者。

===Original===

Why not take something easier?”
Theron had recovered something of his confidence. “Oh, no,” he said, “that is just what attracts me to Abraham. I like the complexities and contradictions in his character. Take for instance all that strange and picturesque episode of Hagar: see the splendid contrast between the craft and commercial guile of his dealings in Egypt and with Abimelech, and the simple, straightforward godliness of his later years. No, all those difficulties only attract me. Do you happen to know﻿—of course you would know﻿—do those German books, or the others, give anywhere any additional details of the man himself and his sayings and doings﻿—little things which help, you know, to round out one’s conception of the individual?”
Again the priest and the doctor stole a furtive glance across the young minister’s head. It was Father Forbes who replied.
“I fear that you are taking our friend Abraham too literally, Mr. Ware,” he said, in that gentle semblance of paternal tones which seemed to go so well with his gown. “Modern research, you know, quite wipes him out of existence as an individual. The word ‘Abram’ is merely an eponym﻿—it means ‘exalted father.’ Practically all the names in the Genesis chronologies are what we call eponymous. Abram is not a person at all: he is a tribe, a sept, a clan. In the same way, Shem is not intended for a man; it is the name of a great division of the human race. Heber is simply the throwing back into allegorical substance, so to speak, of the Hebrews; Heth of the Hittites; Asshur of Assyria.”
“But this is something very new, this theory, isn’t it?” queried Theron.
The priest smiled and shook his head. “Bless you, no! My dear sir, there is nothing new. Epicurus and Lucretius outlined the whole Darwinian theory more than two thousand years ago. As for this eponym thing, why Saint Augustine called attention to it fifteen hundred years ago. In his *De Civitate Dei*, he expressly says of these genealogical names, ‘*gentes non homines*;’ that is, ‘peoples, not persons.’ It was as obvious to him﻿—as much a commonplace of knowledge﻿—as it was to Ezekiel eight hundred years before him.”
“It seems passing strange that we should not know it now, then,” commented Theron; “I mean, that everybody shouldn’t know it.”
Father Forbes gave a little purring chuckle. “Ah, there we get upon contentious ground,” he remarked.

何不拣容易些的？」
塞隆的自信恢复了几分。「哦，不，」他说，「吸住我的正是这一点。我喜欢他性格里那些纠结和矛盾。比如夏甲那一整段离奇而多彩的公案：看他早年同埃及人、同亚比米勒打交道时的机诈和生意心，同他晚年那种单纯、直截的敬神，对照何等鲜明。不，这些难处只会吸住我。你们可知道——你们当然知道——那些德文书，或者别的书，有没有哪里多给出一点这个人本身的细节、他的言行——那些能帮着把一个人的形象圆起来的小事，您知道的？」
神父同医生又隔着年轻牧师的头偷觑了一眼。回答的是福布斯神父。
「我怕您把我们这位朋友亚伯拉罕看得太实了，韦尔先生，」他说，用的还是那同他的袍子最相称的、慈父口吻的温和腔调。「现代的研究，您知道，早把他作为个人从存在里抹掉了。『亚伯兰』这个词不过是一个引申祖名——意思是『尊贵的父』。《创世记》谱系里的人名，几乎全是我们所谓的引申祖名。亚伯兰根本不是一个人：他是一支部族、一支宗族、一个氏族。同样，闪也并不是指一个人；它是人的一大支系的名字。希伯呢，简直可以说，是把希伯来人抛回寓言的实体里；赫之于赫梯人，亚设之于亚述，也是如此。」
「可这套学说很新，不是么？」塞隆问。
神父笑着摇头。「哪里的话！亲爱的先生，天下没有新东西。伊壁鸠鲁和卢克莱修在两千多年前，就把整套达尔文学说勾出了轮廓。至于这个引申祖名，圣奥古斯丁一千五百年前就点破过。他在《上帝之城》（*De Civitate Dei*）里论到这些谱系名字，明明白白地说：*gentes non homines*——『是族类，非个人。』这在他眼里一目了然——是学问里的老生常谈——正如八百年前在以西结眼里一样。」
「那我们如今竟不知道它，倒真是奇了，」塞隆议论道；「我是说，竟不是人人皆知。」
福布斯神父发出一小声打呼噜似的轻笑。「啊，这就踩到是非之地了，」他说。

===Original===

“Why should ‘everybody’ be supposed to know anything at all? What business is it of ‘everybody’s’ to know things? The earth was just as round in the days when people supposed it to be flat, as it is now. So the truth remains always the truth, even though you give a charter to ten hundred thousand separate numskulls to examine it by the light of their private judgment, and report that it is as many different varieties of something else. But of course that whole question of private judgment versus authority is No-Man’s-Land for us. We were speaking of eponyms.”
“Yes,” said Theron; “it is very interesting.”
“There is a curious phase of the subject which hasn’t been worked out much,” continued the priest. “Probably the Germans will get at that too, sometime. They are doing the best Irish work in other fields, as it is. I spoke of Heber and Heth, in Genesis, as meaning the Hebrews and the Hittites. Now my own people, the Irish, have far more ancient legends and traditions than any other nation west of Athens; and you find in their myth of the Milesian invasion and conquest two principal leaders called Heber and Ith, or Heth. That is supposed to be comparatively modern﻿—about the time of Solomon’s Temple. But these independent Irish myths go back to the fall of the Tower of Babel, and they have there an ancestor, grandson of Japhet, named Fenius Farsa, and they ascribe to him the invention of the alphabet. They took their ancient name of Feine, the modern Fenian, from him. Oddly enough, that is the name which the Romans knew the Phoenicians by, and to them also is ascribed the invention of the alphabet. The Irish have a holy salmon of knowledge, just like the Chaldean man-fish. The Druids’ tree-worship is identical with that of the Chaldeans﻿—those pagan groves, you know, which the Jews were always being punished for building. You see, there is nothing new. Everything is built on the ruins of something else. Just as the material earth is made up of countless billions of dead men’s bones, so the mental world is all alive with the ghosts of dead men’s thoughts and beliefs, the wraiths of dead races’ faiths and imaginings.”
Father Forbes paused, then added with a twinkle in his eye: “That peroration is from an old sermon of mine, in the days when I used to preach. I remember rather liking it, at the time.”
“But you still preach?” asked the Rev. Mr.

「这题目还有一面奇怪的地方，一直没有人怎么开发，」神父接着说。「早晚德国人也会做这个的。他们在别的行当里，做的正是顶好的爱尔兰人的活儿。我说到《创世记》里的希伯和赫是希伯来人和赫梯人。我自己的民族爱尔兰人，其古老的传说和旧闻，比雅典以西任何民族都要古远；在他们的米利西安人入侵和征服的神话里，你会找到两位主要首领，一个叫希伯，一个叫伊特，或作赫特。那一段算比较晚——大约在所罗门圣殿的时代。可这些独立的爱尔兰神话，一直上溯到巴别塔的倒塌；他们在那里安了一位祖先，雅弗之孙，名叫菲纽斯·法尔萨，把字母的发明归在他名下。爱尔兰人古老的自称『费内』，今天的『芬尼』，就得自他。说来也怪，罗马人正是用这个名字称呼腓尼基人，而字母的发明也同样归在腓尼基人名下。爱尔兰人有一尾知识的圣鲑，同迦勒底的鱼人一模一样。德鲁伊德的拜树，同迦勒底人的拜树如出一辙——异教的树丛，您知道，犹太人为它没少受罚。您看，天下没有新东西。一切都盖在别的东西的废墟上。正如物质的地球由无数亿死人的骨头叠成，精神的世界里也全是死人思想和信仰的鬼魂、死去的种族的信仰与幻想的幽影，熙熙攘攘，生气勃勃。」

===Original===

Ware, with lifted brows.
“No! no more! I only talk now and again,” answered the priest, with what seemed a suggestion of curtness. He made haste to take the conversation back again. “The names of these dead-and-gone things are singularly pertinacious, though. They survive indefinitely. Take the modern name Marmaduke, for example. It strikes one as peculiarly modern, up-to-date, doesn’t it? Well, it is the oldest name on earth﻿—thousands of years older than Adam. It is the ancient Chaldean Meridug, or Merodach. He was the young god who interceded continually between the angry, omnipotent Ea, his father, and the humble and unhappy Damkina, or Earth, who was his mother. This is interesting from another point of view, because this Merodach or Marmaduke is, so far as we can see now, the original prototype of our ‘divine intermediary’ idea. I daresay, though, that if we could go back still other scores of centuries, we should find whole receding series of types of this Christ-myth of ours.”
Theron Ware sat upright at the fall of these words, and flung a swift, startled look about the room﻿—the instinctive glance of a man unexpectedly confronted with peril, and casting desperately about for means of defence and escape. For the instant his mind was aflame with this vivid impression﻿—that he was among sinister enemies, at the mercy of criminals. He half rose under the impelling stress of this feeling, with the sweat standing on his brow, and his jaw dropped in a scared and bewildered stare.
Then, quite as suddenly, the sense of shock was gone; and it was as if nothing at all had happened. He drew a long breath, took another sip of his coffee, and found himself all at once reflecting almost pleasurably upon the charm of contact with really educated people. He leaned back in the big chair again, and smiled to show these men of the world how much at his ease he was. It required an effort, he discovered, but he made it bravely, and hoped he was succeeding.
“It hasn’t been in my power to at all lay hold of what the world keeps on learning nowadays about its babyhood,” he said. “All I have done is to try to preserve an open mind, and to maintain my faith that the more we know, the nearer we shall approach the Throne.”
Dr. Ledsmar abruptly scuffled his feet on the floor, and took out his watch. “I’m afraid﻿—” he began.
“No, no!

===Original===

There’s plenty of time,” remarked the priest, with his soft half-smile and purring tones. “You finish your cigar here with Mr. Ware, and excuse me while I run down and get rid of the people in the hall.”
Father Forbes tossed his cigar-end into the fender. Then he took from the mantel a strange three-cornered black-velvet cap, with a dangling silk tassel at the side, put it on his head, and went out.
Theron, being left alone with the doctor, hardly knew what to do or say. He took up a paper from the floor beside him, but realized that it would be impolite to go farther, and laid it on his knee. Some trace of that earlier momentary feeling that he was in hostile hands came back, and worried him. He lifted himself upright in the chair, and then became conscious that what really disturbed him was the fact that Dr. Ledsmar had turned in his seat, crossed his legs, and was contemplating him with a gravely concentrated scrutiny through his spectacles.
This uncomfortable gaze kept itself up a long way beyond the point of good manners; but the doctor seemed not to mind that at all.

### VIII / 八
===Original===

When Dr. Ledsmar finally spoke, it was in a kindlier tone than the young minister had looked for. “I had half a notion of going to hear you preach the other evening,” he said; “but at the last minute I backed out. I daresay I shall pluck up the courage, sooner or later, and really go. It must be fully twenty years since I last heard a sermon, and I had supposed that that would suffice for the rest of my life. But they tell me that you are worth while; and, for some reason or other, I find myself curious on the subject.”
Involved and dubious though the compliment might be, Theron felt himself flushing with satisfaction. He nodded his acknowledgment, and changed the topic.
“I was surprised to hear Father Forbes say that he did not preach,” he remarked.
“Why should he?” asked the doctor, indifferently. “I suppose he hasn’t more than fifteen parishioners in a thousand who would understand him if he did, and of these probably twelve would join in a complaint to his Bishop about the heterodox tone of his sermon. There is no point in his going to all that pains, merely to incur that risk. Nobody wants him to preach, and he has reached an age where personal vanity no longer tempts him to do so. What *is* wanted of him is that he should be the paternal, ceremonial, authoritative head and centre of his flock, adviser, monitor, overseer, elder brother, friend, patron, seigneur﻿—whatever you like﻿—everything except a bore. They draw the line at that. You see how diametrically opposed this Catholic point of view is to the Protestant.”
“The difference does seem extremely curious to me,” said Theron. “Now, those people in the hall﻿—”
“Go on,” put in the doctor, as the other faltered hesitatingly. “I know what you were going to say. It struck you as odd that he should let them wait on the bench there, while he came up here to smoke.”
Theron smiled faintly. “I *was* thinking that my﻿—my parishioners wouldn’t have taken it so quietly. But of course﻿—it is all so different!”
“As chalk from cheese!” said Dr. Ledsmar, lighting a fresh cigar. “I daresay everyone you saw there had come either to take the pledge, or see to it that one of the others took it. That is the chief industry in the hall, so far as I have observed. Now discipline is an important element in the machinery here. Coming to take the pledge implies that you have been drunk and are now ashamed.

莱兹马医生终于开口时，用的口气比年轻牧师预期的和悦。“前两天晚上，我本有半个心思去听你讲道，”他说，“可临了还是缩回去了。想必迟早我会鼓起勇气，当真去一回。我上一回听讲道，少说也有二十年了；我原以为那一回尽够下半辈子用的了。可他们告诉我，你值得一听；不知怎么，我发觉自己对这事好奇起来了。”
这恭维纵然缠夹而可疑，塞隆还是自觉满意得红了脸。他点头致谢，换了个话头。
“听福布斯神父说他不讲道，我吃了一惊。”他说。
“他为什么要讲？”医生淡然地问，“我想，就算他讲，他一千个教民里懂得的也超不过十五个；而这些人里，多半还有十二个要联名向主教告他讲道的调门不正派。他何苦下那番功夫，单为招这个风险。没有人要他讲道；他也到了这个年纪，个人的虚荣心再也诱惑不动他了。人们*要*他做的，是做他羊群里父亲般的、司礼的、有权威的头领和中心——顾问、训诫者、监督、长兄、朋友、恩主、领主——随你怎么叫——什么都行，只别做讨人嫌的。到这一条他们划清界限。你瞧，这天主教的观点同新教是怎样的针锋相对。”
“这分别在我看来实在古怪得很，”塞隆说，“比如说，门厅里的那些人——”
“说下去，”医生见对方迟疑，插话道，“我知道你要说什么。他让他们在那长凳上干等，自己上楼来抽烟，你觉得奇。”
塞隆淡淡一笑。“我*正是*想，我——我的教民可不会这样安之若素。可当然——这一切差得太远了！”
