他把两条腿伸到对面的椅子上，懒懒地四下张望，觉得总算争得了一点闲暇，可以不受打扰、痛痛快快地想了。面前是将近两个钟头不折不扣的安静；单是为了它而把自己从例行的职分里抽出来这一事实，就在他心里把这时刻标成了一场特殊场合，照情理，理应产出多于寻常的精神收成。
塞隆的冥想，不时被隔壁教堂一阵阵轰隆的赞美诗唱诵打断。千真万确，他对自己说，整个议会、整个美以美会，再没有哪一个会众唱得像这些奥克塔维乌斯人这样糟。那声响一阵阵传来，自来地分作一支主调——又硬又高又尖又薄的女声——加上三四支并行的、打架的男声支调，唱的人还都不会唱。这些嗓子他已经多么熟悉！隔着两层木墙，他辨得出洛夫乔伊弟兄那自命不凡、拼命往前挤的调门——他回回想把别人压下去；也辨得出巴纳姆姊妹往每一节副歌里砸去的那没有生气、不按节拍的一团尖利喧嚷——她唱时总是半阖着眼，下巴一伸。这些人唱赞美诗还拖腔，拖得塞隆自以为懂得了教规里“不可唱得太慢”那条禁令。从前它叫他纳闷；如今他觉得，那必是为奥克塔维乌斯设的预言。
不可能不想起对照中的那另一种教堂音乐——一个月前他听过的那种——以及他聆听它的那另一间牧师客厅的整个气氛。那个怪异的夜晚那一片受惊的、拥挤的印象，原来一直埋在他心里，被更寻常的日常事务挤在角落里。此刻它们出来了，一一掠过他的脑际——不再混乱扭曲，而是秩序井然、条贯分明。它们早先的效果是又怕又迷；此刻它们逐一浮起，他平静地一一检阅，发觉自己毫不畏缩、毫不闪避，反而把每一件都当作他一生最重要阅历的一个自然而受欢迎的部分，依次流连。

===Original===

The young minister had arrived, all at once, at this conclusion. He did not question at all the means by which he had reached it. Nothing was clearer to his mind than the conclusion itself﻿—that his meeting, with the priest and the doctor was the turning-point in his career. They had lifted him bodily out of the slough of ignorance, of contact with low minds and sordid, narrow things, and put him on solid ground. This book he had been reading﻿—this gentle, tender, lovable book, which had as much true piety in it as any devotional book he had ever read, and yet, unlike all devotional books, put its foot firmly upon everything which could not be proved in human reason to be true﻿—must be merely one of a thousand which men like Father Forbes and Dr. Ledsmar knew by heart. The very thought that he was on the way now to know them, too, made Theron tremble. The prospect wooed him, and he thrilled in response, with the wistful and delicate eagerness of a young lover.
Somehow, the fact that the priest and the doctor were not religious men, and that this book which had so impressed and stirred him was nothing more than Renan’s recital of how he, too, ceased to be a religious man, did not take a form which Theron could look square in the face. It wore the shape, instead, of a vague premise that there were a great many different kinds of religions﻿—the past and dead races had multiplied these in their time literally into thousands﻿—and that each no doubt had its central support of truth somewhere for the good men who were in it, and that to call one of these divine and condemn all the others was a part fit only for untutored bigots. Renan had formally repudiated Catholicism, yet could write in his old age with the deepest filial affection of the Mother Church he had quitted. Father Forbes could talk coolly about the “Christ-myth” without even ceasing to be a priest, and apparently a very active and devoted priest. Evidently there was an intellectual world, a world of culture and grace, of lofty thoughts and the inspiring communion of real knowledge, where creeds were not of importance, and where men asked one another, not “Is your soul saved?” but “Is your mind well furnished?” Theron had the sensation of having been invited to become a citizen of this world.

===Chinese===

年轻的牧师就这样一下子得出了这个结论。至于他是循着什么路径到达的，他全不追问。结论本身在他心里比什么都清楚——同神父和医生的相见，是他生涯的转折点。他们把他整个人从愚昧的泥沼里、从同低卑头脑和龌龊窄狭事物的接触里提了出来，放在了坚实的地上。他读着的这本书——这本温柔、体贴、可爱的书，其中含有的真敬虔不亚于他读过的任何灵修书，却又与一切灵修书不同，把脚踏牢在一切不能凭人类理性证明为真的事物上——想必不过是福布斯神父和莱兹马医生这样的人烂熟于胸的千本中的一本。单是想到自己正在走上认识它们的路，塞隆就颤栗。这前景向他求爱，他也以一个年轻恋人那种怅惘而羞怯的热切，颤栗着应和。
至于神父和医生都不是教门中人，至于这本如此打动他、搅动他的书，不过是勒南自述他自己如何也不再是教门中人——这一事实，不知怎么，始终没能凝成一个塞隆可以正面逼视的形状。它化装的，倒是一个含糊的前提：宗教有许许多多不同的种类——往昔死去的种族当年把它们繁衍到实实在在的数千种——每一种想必在其中的好人心里，都有它某处的真理支柱；把其中一个奉为神启、把其余统统定罪，那是只有未受教养的顽固派才配扮的角色。勒南正式弃绝了天主教，晚年却还能怀着最深切的孺慕之情，写他脱离的那位母亲教会。福布斯神父能冷静地谈论“基督神话”，同时照旧做神父，而且显然还是一位非常勤勉、非常尽职的神父。可见分明存在一个智识的世界，一个文化与优雅的世界，一个高洁思想和真学问之令人奋发的契合的世界，在那里信条无关紧要，人们互相问的不是“你的灵魂得救了没有”，而是“你的头脑装备得好不好”。塞隆有一种感觉：自己已被邀请去做这个世界的公民。

===Original===

The thought so dazzled him that his impulses were dragging him forward to take the new oath of allegiance before he had had time to reflect upon what it was he was abandoning.
The droning of the Doxology from the church outside stirred Theron suddenly out of his revery. It had grown quite dark, and he rose and lit the gas. “Blest Be the Tie That Binds,” they were singing. He paused, with hand still in air, to listen. That well-worn phrase arrested his attention, and gave itself a new meaning. He was bound to those people, it was true, but he could never again harbor the delusion that the tie between them was blessed. There was vaguely present in his mind the consciousness that other ties were loosening as well. Be that as it might, one thing was certain. He had passed definitely beyond pretending to himself that there was anything spiritually in common between him and the Methodist Church of Octavius. The necessity of his keeping up the pretence with others rose on the instant like a looming shadow before his mental vision. He turned away from it, and bent his brain to think of something else.
The noise of Alice opening the front door came as a pleasant digression. A second later it became clear from the sound of voices that she had brought someone back with her, and Theron hastily stretched himself out again in the armchair, with his head back in the pillow, and his feet on the other chair. He had come mighty near forgetting that he was an invalid, and he protected himself the further now by assuming an air of lassitude verging upon prostration.
“Yes; there’s a light burning. It’s all right,” he heard Alice say. She entered the room, and Theron’s head was too bad to permit him to turn it, and see who her companion was.
“Theron dear,” Alice began, “I knew you’d be glad to see *her*, even if you were out of sorts; and I persuaded her just to run in for a minute. Let me introduce you to Sister Soulsby. Sister Soulsby﻿—my husband.”
The Rev. Mr. Ware sat upright with an energetic start, and fastened upon the stranger a look which conveyed anything but the satisfaction his wife had been so sure about. It was at the first blush an undisguised scowl, and only some fleeting memory of that reflection about needing now to dissemble, prevented him from still frowning as he rose to his feet, and perfunctorily held out his hand.
“Delighted, I’m sure,” he mumbled.

===Chinese===

这念头叫他眼花，他的冲动拖着他往前，要他来不及细想放弃的是什么，就先去宣一番新的效忠。
外面教堂里颂荣的嗡嗡声，蓦地把塞隆从出神里惊起。天已全黑，他起身点了煤气灯。他们正唱着《福哉，把我们连在一起的爱绳》。他手还悬在半空，停住细听。这句用旧了的短语攫住了他的注意，自己生出了新的意思。他同这些人绑在一起，不假；可他再不能怀抱那根纽带蒙福的错觉了。他心里还隐约意识到，别的一些纽带也在松开。随它去吧，有一件事是确定的：他同奥克塔维乌斯美以美会之间有任何灵性上的共同点——这层自欺，他已经断然走过去了。至于对旁人还必须把这出戏演下去的必要，此刻像一片逼来的阴影，升起在他的精神视界前。他扭开脸，强迫脑子去想别的。
艾丽斯开前门的声响，来得正像一段宜人的岔曲。紧接着，从人语声里听得分明，她带了个人回来；塞隆慌忙重新在安乐椅里躺开，头枕回枕头，脚搁上另一把椅子。他差点忘了自己是个病人；此刻再给自己加一层保护，摆出一副近乎虚脱的委顿。
“嗯；灯还亮着。没事。”他听见艾丽斯说。她进了屋；塞隆的头疼得连转过去看看来客是谁都不行。
“亲爱的塞隆，”艾丽斯开口道，“我知道，哪怕你不舒服，见到*她*也会高兴的；我劝她就进来坐一分钟。让我给你介绍，这位是索尔斯比姊妹。索尔斯比姊妹——这是我丈夫。”
韦尔牧师精神一振，霍地坐直，钉在来客脸上的那一望，传达的怎么也不是他太太满有把握的那种满意。乍一看，那分明是不加掩饰的一皱眉；只因心里闪过一句“如今需要作伪”的提醒，才没有在起身敷衍地伸出手时，继续皱着。
“幸会。”他含含糊糊地说。

===Original===

Then, looking up, he discovered that Sister Soulsby knew he was not delighted, and that she seemed not to mind in the least.
“As your good lady said, I just ran in for a moment,” she remarked, shaking his limp hand with a brisk, businesslike grasp, and dropping it. “I hate bothering sick people, but as we’re to be thrown together a good deal this next week or so, I thought I’d like to lose no time in saying ‘howdy.’ I won’t keep you up now. Your wife has been sweet enough to ask me to move my trunk over here in the morning, so that you’ll see enough of me and to spare.”
Theron looked falteringly into her face, as he strove for words which should sufficiently mask the disgust this intelligence stirred within him. A debt-raiser in the town was bad enough! A debt-raiser quartered in the very parsonage!﻿—he ground his teeth to think of it.
Alice read his hesitation aright. “Sister Soulsby went to the hotel,” she hastily put in; “and Loren Pierce was after her to come and stay at his house, and *I* ventured to tell her that I thought we could make her more comfortable here.” She accompanied this by so daring a grimace and nod that her husband woke up to the fact that a point in Conference politics was involved.
He squeezed a doubtful smile upon his features. “We shall both do our best,” he said. It was not easy, but he forced increasing amiability into his glance and tone. “Is Brother Soulsby here, too?” he asked.
The debt-raiser shook her head﻿—again the prompt, decisive movement, so like a busy man of affairs. “No,” she answered. “He’s doing supply down on the Hudson this week, but he’ll be here in time for the Sunday morning love-feast. I always like to come on ahead, and see how the land lies. Well, good night! Your head will be all right in the morning.”
Precisely what she meant by this assurance, Theron did not attempt to guess. He received her adieu, noted the masterful manner in which she kissed his wife, and watched her pass out into the hall, with the feeling uppermost that this was a person who decidedly knew her way about. Much as he was prepared to dislike her, and much as he detested the vulgar methods her profession typified, he could not deny that she seemed a very capable sort of woman.
This mental concession did not prevent his fixing upon Alice, when she returned to the room, a glance of obvious disapproval.

===Chinese===

随后他抬头一看，发觉索尔斯比姊妹明知他不觉得幸会，而且似乎毫不在意。
“正如您太太说的，我就进来一小会儿，”她说着，用干脆的、办公事的握手握了握他绵软的手，随即放下。“我不爱麻烦病人；可这一两个礼拜咱们要多多相处，我想不如抓紧道一声‘你好’。这会儿不多搅你了。您太太好意请我明早把箱子搬过来，往后您看我会看够的，看腻为止。”
塞隆吞吞吐吐地望着她的脸，搜罗足以掩住这条消息在他心里搅起的厌恶的言词。城里来一个化债的已经够受了！化债的直接住进牧师宅！——他想到这里就咬牙。
艾丽斯把他的迟疑读对了。“索尔斯比姊妹本要去住客栈，”她赶紧插话，“洛伦·皮尔斯又追着请她住他家；是*我*斗胆对她说，我想咱们这儿能让她住得舒服些。”伴随这句话的，是一个那样大胆的鬼脸和点头，弄得她丈夫醒悟过来：这里面牵着一着议会政治的棋。
他挤出一个将信将疑的笑。“咱们俩都尽力。”他说。不容易，可他强使目光和腔调里的和气一分分加码。“索尔斯比弟兄也来了么？”他问。
化债的摇了摇头——又是那样利落、决断的一动，十足一个忙碌的实干家。“没有，”她答，“他这个礼拜在哈德逊河那边代职；不过主日早晨的爱筵，他会赶到。我总爱先来一步，看看地形。好了，晚安！明早您的头就好了。”
这保证究竟什么意思，塞隆懒得去猜。他受了她的道别，注意到她吻他太太时那副干练的样子，目送她出门进走廊，心里最主要的感想是：这是一个显然识得路数的人。尽管他预备着不喜欢她，尽管她那行当所代表的俗气手法叫他作呕，他不能不承认，她看上去是个非常能干的女人。
这一番心里的让步，并不妨碍艾丽斯回到屋里时，他迎她一望，一望里明明白白是不以为然。

===Original===

“Theron,” she broke forth, to anticipate his reproach, “I did it for the best. The Pierces would have got her if I hadn’t cut in. I thought it would help to have her on our side. And, besides, I like her. She’s the first sister I’ve seen since we’ve been in this hole that’s had a kind word for me﻿—or﻿—or sympathized with me! And﻿—and﻿—if you’re going to be offended﻿—I shall cry!”
There were real tears on her lashes, ready to make good the threat. “Oh, I guess I wouldn’t,” said Theron, with an approach to his old, half-playful manner. “If you like her, that’s the chief thing.”
Alice shook her teardrops away. “No,” she replied, with a wistful smile; “the chief thing is to have her like you. She’s as smart as a steel trap﻿—that woman is﻿—and if she took the notion, I believe she could help get us a better place.”

===Chinese===

“塞隆，”她抢在他责备之前开了口，“我是为咱们好。我要不插一手，她就归皮尔斯家了。我想她站在咱们一边会有好处。再说，我喜欢她。咱们掉进这个坑以来，她是头一位跟我说过一句好话——或者——或者体恤过我的姊妹！要是——要是你还要见怪——我就哭！”
她的睫毛上已挂着真的泪珠，随时兑现威胁。“哦，我看不至于。”塞隆说，找回了几分旧日半打趣的神情。“你喜欢她，这就是头一件要紧的。”
艾丽斯把泪珠甩掉了。“不，”她带着怅惘的笑答道，“头一件要紧的，是让她喜欢你。那女人精得像钢夹子——她要是起了这个念兴，我相信她能帮咱们谋个更好的地方。”

### XIV / 十四
===Original===

The ensuing week went by with a buzz and whirl, circling about Theron Ware’s dizzy consciousness like some huge, impalpable teetotum sent spinning under Sister Soulsby’s resolute hands. Whenever his vagrant memory recurred to it, in after months, he began by marvelling, and ended with a shudder of repulsion.
It was a week crowded with events, which seemed to him to shoot past so swiftly that in effect they came all of a heap. He never essayed the task, in retrospect, of arranging them in their order of sequence. They had, however, a definite and interdependent chronology which it is worth the while to trace.
Mrs. Soulsby brought her trunk round to the parsonage bright and early on Friday morning, and took up her lodgement in the best bedroom, and her headquarters in the house at large, with a cheerful and businesslike manner. She desired nothing so much, she said, as that people should not put themselves out on her account, or allow her to get in their way. She appeared to mean this, too, and to have very good ideas about securing its realization.
During both Friday and the following day, indeed, Theron saw her only at the family meals. There she displayed a hearty relish for all that was set before her which quite won Mrs. Ware’s heart, and though she talked rather more than Theron found himself expecting from a woman, he could not deny that her conversation was both seemly and entertaining. She had evidently been a great traveller, and referred to things she had seen in Savannah or Montreal or Los Angeles in as matter-of-fact fashion as he could have spoken of a visit to Tecumseh. Theron asked her many questions about these and other far-off cities, and her answers were all so pat and showed so keen and clear an eye that he began in spite of himself to think of her with a certain admiration.
She in turn plied him with inquiries about the principal pewholders and members of his congregation﻿—their means, their disposition, and the measure of their devotion. She put these queries with such intelligence, and seemed to assimilate his replies with such an alert understanding, that the young minister was spurred to put dashes of character in his descriptions, and set forth the idiosyncrasies and distinguishing earmarks of his flock with what he felt afterward might have been too free a tongue. But at the time her fine air of appreciation led him captive.

===Chinese===

随后的那个星期，就这样嗡嗡地、旋风般过去了，绕着塞隆·韦尔发晕的意识打转，像一只巨大无形的陀螺，在索尔斯比姐妹（Sister Soulsby）坚决的手底下旋转。此后数月，他那游移的记忆每次回到这一星期，总是开头惊奇，末了打一个厌恶的寒噤。
那是一个事件挤挤一堂的星期；在他看来它们掠过得那样快，效果上竟像一齐砸下来。事后回想，他也从没有试过把它们按先后排个次序。然而它们自有确定而相互衔接的年表，值得梳理一番。
星期五一清早，索尔斯比师母就把衣箱搬到了牧师住宅，在最好的卧室里落了脚，也在整座宅子里扎下了大营，一副爽快干练的做派。她说，她顶盼的就是大家不必为她费心，也不必让她碍事。这话她像是真心的，而且关于怎样落实它，主意极佳。
星期五和第二天整整两天，塞隆果真只在三餐时见到她。席间她对端上来的东西样样吃得香甜，完全赢得了韦尔太太的心；她的话虽比塞隆预想的女人要多些，可他不能不承认，谈吐既得体又有味。她显然走南闯北惯了，提起萨凡纳、蒙特利尔或洛杉矶的见闻，口气平淡得同他说起特库姆塞一样。塞隆就这些和别的远城问了她许多；她的回答句句妥帖，眼光那样锐利清明，他不由得对她生出几分佩服。
她反过来也一句句盘问他：会里主要的坐堂会友和教友——他们的家底、脾性、虔敬的分量。这些问话问得那样在行，他的答话她领会得那样敏捷，年轻的牧师受了激励，描述里便添上一笔笔性格的勾勒，把他羊群的种种怪癖、各自的记号摆了出来——事后想来，未免说得太多。可当时她那一副激赏的风仪，把他俘虏了。

===Original===

He gossiped about his parishioners as if he enjoyed it. He made a specially happy thumbnail sketch for her of one of his trustees, Erastus Winch, the loud-mouthed, ostentatiously jovial, and really cold-hearted cheese-buyer. She was particularly interested in hearing about this man. The personality of Winch seemed to have impressed her, and she brought the talk back to him more than once, and prompted Theron to the very threshold of indiscretion in his confidences on the subject.
Save at mealtimes, Sister Soulsby spent the two days out around among the Methodists of Octavius. She had little or nothing to say about what she thus saw and heard, but used it as the basis for still further inquiries. She told more than once, however, of how she had been pressed here or there to stay to dinner or supper, and how she had excused herself. “I’ve knocked about too much,” she would explain to the Wares, “not to fight shy of random country cooking. When I find such a born cook as you are﻿—well I know when I’m well off.” Alice flushed with pleased pride at this, and Theron himself felt that their visitor showed great good sense. By Saturday noon, the two women were calling each other by their first names. Theron learned with a certain interest that Sister Soulsby’s Christian name was Candace.
It was only natural that he should give even more thought to her than to her quaint and unfamiliar old Ethiopian name. She was undoubtedly a very smart woman. To his surprise she had never introduced in her talk any of the stock religious and devotional phrases which official Methodists so universally employed in mutual converse. She might have been an insurance agent, or a schoolteacher, visiting in a purely secular household, so little parade of cant was there about her.
He caught himself wondering how old she was. She seemed to have been pretty well over the whole American continent, and that must take years of time. Perhaps, however, the exertion of so much travel would tend to age one in appearance. Her eyes were still youthful﻿—decidedly wise eyes, but still juvenile. They had sparkled with almost girlish merriment at some of his jokes. She turned them about a good deal when she spoke, making their glances fit and illustrate the things she said. He had never met anyone whose eyes played so constant and prominent a part in their owner’s conversation.

===Chinese===

他数说自己的教民，仿佛乐在其中。他给一位理事画了一幅绝妙的速写：伊拉斯塔斯·温奇，大嗓门、摆热闹、其实心冷的那位奶酪买手。她听这个人的事听得特别起劲。温奇这个人似乎印进了她的脑子；她不止一次把话头引回他身上，逗着塞隆在这题目上的知心话，一直逗到失言的门边。
除开吃饭，索尔斯比姐妹这两天都散在奥克塔维乌斯的美以美会中间。她对这些见闻几乎不置一词，只拿来作进一步打探的底子。倒是讲过好几回：这里那里怎样硬留她吃正餐吃茶点，她怎样推辞了。「我东跑西颠惯了，」她对韦尔夫妇解释，「不能不提防乡下随机的厨艺。碰上您这样一位天生的厨子——我自然知道自己的福气。」艾丽斯喜得红着脸，满心得意；塞隆自己也觉来客见识高明。到星期六晌午，两个女人已彼此称呼起教名来。塞隆颇感兴趣地得知，索尔斯比姐妹的教名是坎迪斯（Candace）。
他对她本人的琢磨，自然比对她这个古怪而陌生的古衣索比亚名字更多。她无疑是位极精明的女人。叫他吃惊的是，她谈话里从没有夹带职业美以美会教友彼此交谈时人人在用的那套宗教口头禅。她满可以是个保险捾客，或个女教员，做客在一户纯世俗人家——她身上一点不摆教门的腔。
他发觉自己在捉摸她的年纪。她仿佛把整个美洲大陆都走遍了，那得花去许多年。不过，这样奔波的辛劳，也许容易把人催老。她的眼睛还年轻——那是一双断然聪明的眼睛，可仍旧年轻。听了他几句笑话，它们闪出过近乎少女的欢快。她说话时把眼睛转来转去，叫目光随处配合、随处例证她说的话。这样让眼睛在主人的谈话里始终唱主角的，他生平没有见过第二人。

===Original===

Theron had never seen a play; but he had encountered the portraits of famous queens of the drama several times in illustrated papers or shop windows, and it occurred to him that some of the more marked contortions of Sister Soulsby’s eyes﻿—notably a trick she had of rolling them swiftly round and plunging them, so to speak, into an intent, yearning, one might almost say devouring, gaze at the speaker﻿—were probably employed by eminent actresses like Ristori and Fanny Davenport.
The rest of Sister Soulsby was undoubtedly subordinated in interest to those eyes of hers. Sometimes her face seemed to be reviving temporarily a comeliness which had been constant in former days; then again it would look decidedly, organically, plain. It was the worn and loose-skinned face of a nervous, middle-aged woman, who had had more than her share of trouble, and drank too much tea. She wore the collar of her dress rather low; and Theron found himself wondering at this, because, though long and expansive, her neck certainly showed more cords and cavities than consorted with his vague ideal of statuesque beauty. Then he wondered at himself for thinking about it, and abruptly reined up his fancy, only to find that it was playing with speculations as to whether her yellowish complexion was due to that tea-drinking or came to her as a legacy of Southern blood.
He knew that she was born in the South because she said so. From the same source he learned that her father had been a wealthy planter, who was ruined by the war, and sank into a premature grave under the weight of his accumulated losses. The large dark rings around her eyes grew deeper still in their shadows when she told about this, and her ordinarily sharp voice took on a mellow cadence, with a soft, drawling accent, turning *u*’s into *o*’s, and having no *r*’s to speak of. Theron had imbibed somewhere in early days the conviction that the South was the land of romance, of cavaliers and gallants and black eyes flashing behind mantillas and outspread fans, and somehow when Sister Soulsby used this intonation she suggested all these things.
But almost all her talk was in another key﻿—a brisk, direct, idiomatic manner of speech, with an intonation hinting at no section in particular. It was merely that of the city-dweller as distinguished from the rustic. She was of about Alice’s height, perhaps a shade taller.

===Chinese===

塞隆没看过戏；可戏剧名后的画像他在画报上、橱窗里遇见过不止一回；他由此想到，索尔斯比姐妹眼睛里某些更夸张的操演——尤其她那手绝活：滴溜溜把眼睛转一圈，随即往下一沉，可以说是扎进一种专注的、渴慕的、几乎要吞噬说话人的凝视——多半正出自里斯托里、范妮·达文波特那班名伶的戏路。
索尔斯比姐妹的其余部分，在兴味上无疑都被那双眼睛压倒了。有时她的脸仿佛暂时复活了昔日的姿色；忽而又显得断然地、从里到外地平庸。那是一张紧张忙碌的中年女人的脸，皮肉松垮，烦恼受得比份内多，茶也喝得太多。她的领圈开得颇低；塞隆为此纳闷，因为那脖子虽长而宽，露出的筋络和凹坑，实在同他心中雕像之美的模糊理想不甚相称。随即他又为自己的琢磨纳闷，猛地把心思勒住；却发现它又在玩耍另一个猜测：她那微黄的脸色，是喝茶喝出来的，还是南方血统的遗产。
她生在南方，这一点他知道，因为她自己说的。同样来源，他又得知她父亲原是富有的种植园主，毁于战乱，在积亏的重压下过早进了坟墓。讲到这段时，她眼周的大黑圈阴影愈深，平日尖锐的嗓音也换上一种圆润的调子，带一点软软的拖腔：u 念成 o，r 则几乎不念。塞隆早年不知从哪里吸进了这个成见：南方是罗曼司之乡，有骑士和情郎，有面纱和团扇后闪亮的黑眼睛；索尔斯比姐妹一用这个腔调，不知怎么就把这一切都招来了。
可她的话几乎全用另一个调——干脆、直截、地道，不偏向任何一方的口音。那不过是城里人的话，有别于乡下人。她的个头同艾丽斯相仿，兴许还略高一线。

===Original===

It did not escape the attention of the Wares that she wore clothes of a more stylish cut and a livelier arrangement of hues than any Alice had ever dared own, even in lax-minded Tyre. The two talked of this in their room on Friday night; and Theron explained that congregations would tolerate things of this sort with a stranger which would be sharply resented in the case of local folk whom they controlled. It was on this occasion that Alice in turn told Theron she was sure Mrs. Soulsby had false teeth﻿—a confidence which she immediately regretted as an act of treachery to her sex.
On Saturday afternoon, toward evening, Brother Soulsby arrived, and was guided to the parsonage by his wife, who had gone to the depot to meet him. They must have talked over the situation pretty thoroughly on the way, for by the time the newcomer had washed his face and hands and put on a clean collar, Sister Soulsby was ready to announce her plan of campaign in detail.
Her husband was a man of small stature and, like herself, of uncertain age. He had a gentle, if rather dry, clean-shaven face, and wore his dust-colored hair long behind. His little figure was clad in black clothes of a distinctively clerical fashion, and he had a white neckcloth neatly tied under his collar. The Wares noted that he looked clean and amiable rather than intellectually or spiritually powerful, as he took the vacant seat between theirs, and joined them in concentrating attention upon Mrs. Soulsby.
This lady, holding herself erect and alert on the edge of the low, big easy-chair had the air of presiding over a meeting.
“My idea is,” she began, with an easy implication that no one else’s idea was needed, “that your Quarterly Conference, when it meets on Monday, must be adjourned to Tuesday. We will have the people all out tomorrow morning to love-feast, and announcement can be made there, and at the morning service afterward, that a series of revival meetings are to be begun that same evening. Mr. Soulsby and I can take charge in the evening, and we’ll see to it that *that* packs the house﻿—fills the church to overflowing Monday evening. Then we’ll quietly turn the meeting into a debt-raising convention, before they know where they are, and we’ll wipe off the best part of the load.

===Chinese===

韦尔夫妇都注意到，她衣裳的裁剪之入时、颜色搭配之活泛，都赛过艾丽斯敢有的任何一件，连在心思宽松的泰尔也没敢置过。星期五夜里，两人在自己屋里议论了这一点；塞隆解释说：这样的事，会众对一个外来的可以容忍，落在他们管得着的本地人身上，就要遭白眼。也是这一晚，艾丽斯反过来告诉塞隆，她断定索尔斯比太太装的是假牙——话一出口立刻后悔，觉得这是对同性的一次背叛。
星期六下午傍晚，索尔斯比弟兄到了，由去车站接他的太太领到牧师住宅。两人在路上必是把局面从头到尾商量透了；因为新来的人刚洗净手脸、换上干净领子，索尔斯比姐妹就已准备好把她的作战计划全盘摊开。
她丈夫身量矮小，同她一样年纪难猜。一张脸刮得干净，温和而略干；土色的头发脑后留得长。小小的身条裹着一身明显教士式的黑衣，领下端端正正系一条白领巾。他拣了韦尔夫妇中间那个空位坐下，两人注意到：他模样干净、和气，谈不上智力或灵性上的威势；于是一家三对的注意都聚到索尔斯比太太身上。
这位女士在低矮宽大的安乐椅沿上挺直而警觉地坐着，一副主持会议的气派。
「我的主意是，」她开腔了，轻巧地暗示着旁人的主意一概用不着，「你们那个季议会，星期一开了，必须延会到星期二。明天早上我们把人全数动员来赴爱筵，就在那儿、在随后的早礼拜上宣布：当天晚上起，连开奋兴布道会。晚上由索尔斯比先生和我掌盘；我们包管*那*一场满堂——星期一晚上把教堂挤到往外溢。随后，趁他们还没回过神，我们悄悄把大会转成募债大会，把担子卸掉一大截。

===Original===

Now, don’t you see,” she turned her eyes full upon Theron as she spoke, “you want to hold your Quarterly Conference *after* this money’s been raised, not before.”
“I see what you mean,” Mr. Ware responded gravely. “But﻿—”
“But what!” Sister Soulsby interjected, with vivacity.
“Well,” said Theron, picking his words, “in the first place, it rests with the Presiding Elder to say whether an adjournment can be made until Tuesday, not with me.”
“That’s all right. Leave that to me,” said the lady.
“In the second place,” Theron went on, still more hesitatingly, “there seems a certain﻿—what shall I say?﻿—indirection in﻿—in﻿—”
“In getting them together for a revival, and springing a debt-raising on them?” Sister Soulsby put in. “Why, man alive, that’s the best part of it. You ought to be getting some notion by this time what these Octavius folks of yours are like. I’ve only been here two days, but I’ve got their measure down to an allspice. Supposing you were to announce tomorrow that the debt was to be raised Monday. How many men with bank-accounts would turn up, do you think? You could put them all in your eye, sir﻿—all in your eye!”
“Very possibly you’re right,” faltered the young minister.
“Right? Why, of course I’m right,” she said, with placid confidence. “You’ve got to take folks as you find them; and you’ve got to find them the best way you can. One place can be worked, managed, in one way, and another needs quite a different way, and both ways would be dead frosts﻿—complete failures﻿—in a third.”
Brother Soulsby coughed softly here, and shuffled his feet for an instant on the carpet. His wife resumed her remarks with slightly abated animation, and at a slower pace.
“My experience,” she said, “has shown me that the Apostle was right. To properly serve the cause, one must be all things to all men. I have known very queer things indeed turn out to be means of grace. You simply *can’t* get along without some of the wisdom of the serpent. We are commanded to have it, for that matter. And now, speaking of that, do you know when the Presiding Elder arrives in town today, and where he is going to eat supper and sleep?”
Theron shook his head. “All I know is he isn’t likely to come here,” he said, and added sadly, “I’m afraid he’s not an admirer of mine.”
“Perhaps that’s not all his fault,” commented Sister Soulsby. “I’ll tell you something.

===Chinese===

您瞧，」说话时她把眼睛整个对准塞隆，「这笔钱募完*之后*再开您的季议会，别在先。」
「我明白您的意思，」韦尔先生郑重地应。「可是——」
「可是什么！」索尔斯比姐妹活泼地打断。
「唔，」塞隆字斟句酌，「第一，延会到星期二行不行，权在教区长老，不在我。」
「这就结了。那事交给我。」女士说。
「第二，」塞隆接着说，愈发迟疑，「这里面仿佛有一种——怎么说好呢——迂回，在——在——」
「先以奋兴的名义聚人，冷不防甩出募债？」索尔斯比姐妹接过去。「哎呀我的先生，妙就妙在这里。您奥克塔维乌斯的这班人是什么材料，您到这会儿总该有点数了。我只来了两天，已把他们的尺寸量到一分一厘。假如明天您宣布星期一募债，您想想，有银行账户的男人们会来几个？您把他们全搁进眼睛里也搁得下，先生——全搁进眼睛里！」
「很可能您是对的，」年轻的牧师嗫嚅。
「对？嗨，当然我对，」她说，平静而笃定。「人得照他碰到的样子拿；还得想法子碰得巧。一个地方用一种法子经营；另一个地方得用截然不同的法子；两样法子搁到第三个地方，都是死霜冻——一败涂地。」
索尔斯比弟兄在这里轻轻咳嗽，双脚在地毯上沙沙蹭了一下。太太接着讲，劲头略减，语速放慢。
「我的经验教我明白，使徒是对的。要好好侍奉这事业，必须向什么样的人就作什么样的人。我亲眼见过顶古怪的事情，末了竟是恩典的门径。蛇的智慧，你没有它，简直寸步难行。我们原是受命要有它的。说到这里——您可知道教区长老今天几点进城，晚饭、过夜又落在谁家？」
塞隆摇头。「我只知道他多半不会来这儿，」他说，又黯然添一句，「只怕他不赏识我。」
「这也许不全怪他，」索尔斯比姐妹评论道。「我告诉您一件事。

===Original===

He came in on the same train as my husband, and that old trustee Pierce of yours was waiting for him with his buggy, and I saw like a flash what was in the wind, and the minute the train stopped I caught the Presiding Elder, and invited him in your name to come right here and stay; told him you and Alice were just set on his coming﻿—wouldn’t take no for an answer. Of course he couldn’t come﻿—I knew well enough he had promised old Pierce﻿—but we got in our invitation anyway, and it won’t do you any harm. Now, that’s what I call having some gumption﻿—wisdom of the serpent, and so on.”
“I’m sure,” remarked Alice, “I should have been mortified to death if he *had* come. We lost the extension-leaf to our table in moving, and four is all it’ll seat decently.”
Sister Soulsby smiled winningly into the wife’s honest face. “Don’t you see, dear,” she explained patiently, “I only asked him because I knew he couldn’t come. A little butter spreads a long way, if it’s only intelligently warmed.”
“It was certainly very ingenious of you,” Theron began almost stiffly. Then he yielded to the humanities, and with a kindling smile added, “And it was as kind as kind could be. I’m afraid you’re wrong about it’s doing me any good, but I can see how well you meant it, and I’m grateful.”
“We *could* have sneaked in the kitchen table, perhaps, while he was out in the garden, and put on the extra long tablecloth,” interjected Alice, musingly.
Sister Soulsby smiled again at Sister Ware, but without any words this time; and Alice on the instant rose, with the remark that she must be going out to see about supper.
“I’m going to insist on coming out to help you,” Mrs. Soulsby declared, “as soon as I’ve talked over one little matter with your husband. Oh, yes, you must let me this time. I insist!”
As the kitchen door closed behind Mrs. Ware, a swift and apparently significant glance shot its way across from Sister Soulsby’s roving, eloquent eyes to the calmer and smaller gray orbs of her husband. He rose to his feet, made some little explanation about being a gardener himself, and desiring to inspect more closely some rhododendrons he had noticed in the garden, and forthwith moved decorously out by the other door into the front hall. They heard his footsteps on the gravel beneath the window before Mrs. Soulsby spoke again.

===Chinese===

他同我丈夫一趟车到的；您那位老理事皮尔斯赶着篷车在站上候他。我一眼看穿了风色；车一停，我就一把挽住教区长老，用您的名义请他立刻来这儿住；告诉他您同艾丽斯盼他来盼得什么似的——不答应不罢休。他自然来不成——他答应了老皮尔斯，我清楚得很——可我们的邀请到底递出去了，于您有百利而无一害。这才叫做有点门道——蛇的智慧，等等。」
「真的，」艾丽斯说，「他*真*来了，我可要窘死了。搬家把桌子的活络板丢了，这张桌子体体面面只坐得下四个人。」
索尔斯比姐妹朝着做妻子的那张诚实的脸盈盈一笑。「您瞧，亲爱的，」她耐心解释，「我请他，正因为我知道他来不成。一点黄油，只要会焐，能涂老远的路。」
「您这手实在巧妙，」塞隆说得几乎有点生硬。随后他到底让位于人情，眼色一亮，添道：「而且好心到了家。您说这事于我有益，我怕未必；可我看得出您用意多好，我感激。」
「他要是到园子里去了，咱们兴许还能把厨房那张桌子偷偷搬进来，铺上加长的桌布，」艾丽斯若有所思地插话。
索尔斯比姐妹又朝韦尔姐妹一笑，这回没有话；艾丽斯立刻起身，说该出去照管晚饭了。
「谈完一件小事，我马上也出来帮您，」索尔斯比太太宣布，「同您丈夫谈的。哦，不，这回您务必依我。我坚持！」
厨房门在韦尔太太身后一关，索尔斯比姐妹那双流转善辩的眼睛，迅疾而显然意味深长地扫向她丈夫那双较小而沉静的灰眼。他站起身，咕哝了一句什么自己也是个园艺家、想细看看园里那几株杜鹃，随即端端正正从另一扇门进了前厅。窗下砾石路上响起他的脚步之后，索尔斯比姐妹才重新开口。

===Original===

“You’re right about the Presiding Elder, and you’re wrong,” she said. “He isn’t what one might call precisely in love with you. Oh, I know the story﻿—how you got into debt at Tyre, and he stepped in and insisted on your being denied Tecumseh and sent here instead.”
“*He* was responsible for that, then, was he?” broke in Theron, with contracted brows.
“Why, don’t you make any effort to find out anything at *all*?” she asked pertly enough, but with such obvious good-nature that he could not but have pleasure in her speech. “Why, of course he did it! Who else did you suppose?”
“Well,” said the young minister, despondently, “if he’s as much against me as all that, I might as well hang up my fiddle and go home.”
Sister Soulsby gave a little involuntary groan of impatience. She bent forward, and, lifting her eyes, rolled them at him in a curve of downward motion which suggested to his fancy the image of two eagles in a concerted pounce upon a lamb.
“My friend,” she began, with a new note of impressiveness in her voice, “if you’ll pardon my saying it, you haven’t got the spunk of a mouse. If you’re going to lay down, and let everybody trample over you just as they please, you’re right! You *might* as well go home. But now here, this is what I wanted to say to you: Do you just keep your hands off these next few days, and leave this whole thing to me. I’ll pull it into shipshape for you. No﻿—wait a minute﻿—don’t interrupt now. I have taken a liking to you. You’ve got brains, and you’ve got human nature in you, and heart. What you lack is *sabe*﻿—common sense. You’ll get that, too, in time, and meanwhile I’m not going to stand by and see you cut up and fed to the dogs for want of it. I’ll get you through this scrape, and put you on your feet again, right-side-up-with care, because, as I said, I like you. I like your wife, too, mind. She’s a good, honest little soul, and she worships the very ground you tread on. Of course, as long as people *will* marry in their teens, the wrong people will get yoked up together. But that’s neither here nor there. She’s a kind sweet little body, and she’s devoted to you, and it isn’t every intellectual man that gets even that much. But now it’s a go, is it? You promise to keep quiet, do you, and leave the whole show absolutely to me? Shake hands on it.”

===Chinese===

「您对教区长老的看法，对一半，错一半，」她说。「他谈不上——怎么说呢——爱您。哦，内情我知道——您在泰尔怎样欠了债，他怎样插进来，硬不叫您去特库姆塞，把您打发到这儿。」
「原来*他*是这背后的主使，是么？」塞隆眉头一皱，打断道。
「哎呀，您就没打算费一根指头去打听点*什么*吗？」她问得够俏皮，却那样显然好意，他听了只有快活的份。「嗐，当然是他！不然您还猜是谁？」
「唉，」年轻的牧师沮丧地说，「他既这样跟我过不去，我不如挂起琴回家去。」
索尔斯比姐妹不由得轻轻哼出一声不耐烦。她俰身向前，抬起眼睛，朝他画了半个下坠的弧——在他想象里，那活像两只鹰串通好，合力扑向一头羔羊。
「我的朋友，」她开腔，嗓音里换上一种郑重的新调，「恕我直言：您连一只老鼠的胆气都没有。您要是打算躺倒，随人随意踩过来，那您说得对！真不如回家。可眼下，听我说，这正是我要同您讲的：这几天，您把手缩回来，整个事情全交给我。我替您把它收拾得妥妥帖帖。不——等一下——先别插嘴。我喜欢上您了。您有头脑，有人味儿，有心。您缺的是*sabe*（门道）——常识。这个您将来也会有；眼下嘛，我不能站在旁边，看您因为缺它被剁碎了喂狗。我领您趟过这趟浑水，把您重新扶上脚——小心轻放、正面朝上——因为我说了，我喜欢您。您太太我也喜欢，记住。她是个又良善又诚实的小灵魂，把您脚踩的土都当圣地供着。当然，人既然*非要*十几岁成亲，错配的姻缘自然难免。可那是另一码事。她是位和气的小可人儿，一心向着你；知识分子能捞到这一步，也不是人人有的福分。那么——说定了，好不好？您答应不出声，把整台戏全交给我？握个手。」

===Original===

Sister Soulsby had risen, and stood now holding out her hand in a frank, manly fashion. Theron looked at the hand, and made mental notes that there were a good many veins discernible on the small wrist, and that the forearm seemed to swell out more than would have been expected in a woman producing such a general effect of leanness. He caught the shine of a thin bracelet-band of gold under the sleeve. A delicate, significant odor just hinted its presence in the air about this outstretched arm﻿—something which was not a perfume, yet deserved as gracious a name.
He rose to his feet, and took the proffered hand with a deliberate gesture, as if he had been cautiously weighing all the possible arguments for and against this momentous compact.
“I promise,” he said gravely, and the two palms squeezed themselves together in an earnest clasp.
“Right you are,” exclaimed the lady, once more with cheery vivacity. “Mind, when it’s all over, I’m going to give you a good, serious, downright talking to﻿—a regular hoeing-over. I’m not sure I shan’t give you a sound shaking into the bargain. You need it. And now I’m going out to help Alice.”
The Reverend Mr. Ware remained standing after his new friend had left the room, and his meditative face wore an even unusual air of abstraction. He strolled aimlessly over, after a time, to the desk by the window, and stood there looking out at the slight figure of Brother Soulsby, who was bending over and attentively regarding some pink blossoms on a shrub through what seemed to be a pocket magnifying-glass.
What remained uppermost in his mind was not this interesting woman’s confident pledge of championship in his material difficulties. He found himself dwelling instead upon her remark about the incongruous results of early marriages. He wondered idly if the little man in the white tie, fussing out there over that rhododendron-bush, had figured in her thoughts as an example of these evils. Then he reflected that they had been mentioned in clear relation to talk about Alice.
Now that he faced this question, it was as if he had been consciously ignoring and putting it aside for a long time. How was it, he asked himself now, that Alice, who had once seemed so bright and keen-witted, who had in truth started out immeasurably his superior in swiftness of apprehension and readiness in humorous quips and conceits, should have grown so dull?

===Chinese===

索尔斯比姐妹已站起身，坦率地、近乎男子气地伸出手来。塞隆望着那只手，心里记下：细细的手腕上看得见好些青筋；那截小臂，比一个通身给人干瘦印象的女人所能预期的，似乎更饱满些。袖口底下闪出一线金镯的亮光。那条伸出的手臂四周的空气里，一缕精致的、意味深长的气味刚刚点到即止——不是香水，却当得起一样客气的名字。
他站起身，郑重其事地一伸手，握住那只手，仿佛方才已把赞成反对这桩重大盟约的一切理由，谨慎地掂量了一遍。
「我答应，」他庄重地说；两只手掌在恳切的相握里彼此收紧。
「这就对了，」女士嚷道，重新快活起来。「记着，事全办完，我要好好教训您一顿——正正经经、结结实实的一顿，锄一遍地。兴许还饶不了一顿好摇。您欠着呢。现在我出去帮艾丽斯。」
这位新朋友走后，韦尔牧师仍站着没动，沉思的脸上带着一层异乎寻常的出神。过了一会，他信步踱到窗边的书桌旁，站在那里望着外面索尔斯比弟兄瘦小的身影——那人正弓着腰，用一具好像是袖珍放大镜的东西，专心细看一丛灌木上的粉花。
盘踞他心头最上层的，并不是这位有趣的女子那番替他物质难关打抱不平的豪语。他发现自己反复咀嚼的，倒是她那句关于早婚之错乱的议论。他无聊地想：外头那个系白领巾的小个子，对着那丛杜鹃忙活，在她心里可曾被引作这等祸害的例证？随后他又想：那话分明是接着谈论艾丽斯说下来的。
如今直面这个问题，倒像他早已自觉不自觉地把它搁置了很久。他此刻问自己：艾丽斯，当初那样聪明伶俐——论悟性之快、谈吐的机锋雅谑，起步时实在远胜过他——怎么变得这样迟钝了？

===Original===

For she was undoubtedly slow to understand things nowadays. Her absurd lugging in of the extension-table problem, when the great strategic point of that invitation foisted upon the Presiding Elder came up, was only the latest sample of a score of these heavy-minded exhibitions that recalled themselves to him. And outsiders were apparently beginning to notice it. He knew by intuition what those phrases, “good, honest little soul” and “kind, sweet little body” signified, when another woman used them to a husband about his wife. The very employment of that word “little” was enough, considering that there was scarcely more than a hair’s difference between Mrs. Soulsby and Alice, and that they were both rather tall than otherwise, as the stature of women went.
What she had said about the chronic misfortunes of intellectual men in such matters gave added point to those meaning phrases. Nobody could deny that geniuses and men of conspicuous talent had as a rule, all through history, contracted unfortunate marriages. In almost every case where their wives were remembered at all, it was on account of their abnormal stupidity, or bad temper, or something of that sort. Take Xantippe, for example, and Shakespeare’s wife, and﻿—and﻿—well, there was Byron, and Bulwer-Lytton, and ever so many others.
Of course there was nothing to be done about it. These things happened, and one could only put the best possible face on them, and live one’s appointed life as patiently and contentedly as might be. And Alice undoubtedly merited all the praise which had been so generously bestowed upon her. She *was* good and honest and kindly, and there could be no doubt whatever as to her utter devotion to him. These were tangible, solid qualities, which must always secure respect for her. It was true that she no longer seemed to be very popular among people. He questioned whether men, for instance, like Father Forbes and Dr. Ledsmar would care much about her. Visions of the wifeless and academic calm in which these men spent their lives﻿—an existence consecrated to literature and knowledge and familiarity with all the loftiest and noblest thoughts of the past﻿—rose and enveloped him in a cloud of depression. No such lot would be his!

===Chinese===

因为她如今理解事情确实是慢。把活络餐桌的难题硬扯进来——在那强加给教区长老的邀请的战略要点正被议论时——只是浮上他心头的许多沉重心不在焉的表演里最新的一例。外人显然也开始觉出来了。另一个女人当着丈夫的面这样称他妻子：「又良善又诚实的小灵魂」「和气的小可人儿」——这些字眼意味着什么，他凭直觉全懂。单是那个「小」字就尽够了：想想索尔斯比太太同艾丽斯之间身量不过一发的相差，想想两人论女人的个头都还偏高。
她方才那番关于知识分子在这类事上的宿命倒运的话，又给这些有意味的字眼添了锋芒。谁也不能否认：天才和显赫的才子们，纵观史册，照例缔结的都是不幸的婚姻。他们的妻子但凡还被人记起，几乎无一例外，是因了反常的愚蠢、糟糕的脾气，或诸如此类。看克桑西佩，看莎士比亚的老婆，还有——还有——拜伦，布尔沃-利顿，还有好些别人。
当然，这事无可作为。这些事照例要发生；人只能给它撑一副顶好的脸面，把自己命定的日子过得尽量耐心、尽量知足。而艾丽斯无疑当得起方才那些慷慨加于她的赞语。她*确是*良善、诚实、和气；她对他的死心塌地，更毫无可疑。这些是摸得着的、实打实的品质，永远能保她受人敬重。不错，她如今在人前似乎不大得人心了。他怀疑，比方说，福布斯神父和莱兹马医生那一路的男人，会不会把她放在眼里。这两个人过的那种无妻室的、书斋的清静岁月——一种献身于文学与知识、与古往今来一切崇高思想相亲近的生活——的幻象升起来，把他裹进一片消沉的云。那样的命是不会轮到他的了！

===Original===

He must labor along among ignorant and spiteful narrow-minded people to the end of his days, pocketing their insults and fawning upon the harsh hands of jealous nonentities who happened to be his official masters, just to keep a roof over his head﻿—or rather Alice’s. He must sacrifice everything to this, his ambitions, his passionate desires to do real good in the world on a large scale, his mental freedom, yes, even his chance of having truly elevating, intellectual friendships. For it was plain enough that the men whose friendship would be of genuine and stimulating profit to him would not like her. Now that he thought of it, she seemed latterly to make no friends at all.
Suddenly, as he watched in a blank sort of way Brother Soulsby take out a penknife, and lop an offending twig from a rosebush against the fence, something occurred to him. There was a curious exception to that rule of Alice’s isolation. She had made at least one friend. Levi Gorringe seemed to like her extremely.
As if his mind had been a camera, Theron snapped a shutter down upon this odd, unbidden idea, and turned away from the window.
The sounds of an active, almost strenuous conversation in female voices came from the kitchen. Theron opened the door noiselessly, and put in his head, conscious of something furtive in his intention.
“You must dreen every drop of water off the spinach, mind, before you put it over, or else﻿—”
It was Sister Soulsby’s sharp and penetrating tones which came to him. Theron closed the door again, and surrendered himself once more to the circling whirl of his thoughts.

===Chinese===

他得在一群无知、怨毒、心胸狭窄的人中间苦捱到老，吞下他们的侮辱，向几个恰好做着他上司的、善妒的无名之辈的粗手陪笑脸，只为挣一个屋顶遮头——或者说，遮艾丽斯的头。他须得为此牺牲一切：他的抱负，他在这世上大规模行真正善事的火热心愿，他的精神自由，是的，甚至他获得真正起奋发之心、心智之交谊的机会。因为够明白的：那些友谊于他有真正滋补之益的男人，不会喜欢她。此刻想来，她近来似乎简直交不上一个朋友。
突然，他正恍惚望着索尔斯比弟兄掏出铅笔刀、把篱边玫瑰上一根碍眼的枝条削去，一个念头来了。艾丽斯那孤立的通例，有一个古怪的例外。她至少交下了一个朋友。利维·戈林奇像是极其喜欢她。
仿佛他的头脑是一架照相机，塞隆对着这个不请自来的怪念头咔哒落下一扇快门，转身离开了窗子。
厨房里传来女人嗓音的活跃、几乎是热烈的谈话。塞隆悄没声地推开门，探进头去，自觉这意图里有几分鬼祟。
「菠菜下锅之前，你务必把每一滴水漓干净，记着，不然——」
传到他耳朵里的是索尔斯比姐妹尖锐钻人的腔调。塞隆重新关上门，又把自己交给思绪的旋涡。

### XIX / 十九
===Original===

Theron Ware looked about him with frankly undisguised astonishment.
The room in which he found himself was so dark at first that it yielded little to the eye, and that little seemed altogether beyond his comprehension. His gaze helplessly followed Celia and her candle about as she busied herself in the work of illumination. When she had finished, and pinched out the taper, there were seven lights in the apartment﻿—lights beaming softly through half-opaque alternating rectangles of blue and yellow glass. They must be set in some sort of lanterns around against the wall, he thought, but the shape of these he could hardly make out.
Gradually his sight adapted itself to this subdued light, and he began to see other things. These queer lamps were placed, apparently, so as to shed a special radiance upon some statues which stood in the corners of the chamber, and upon some pictures which were embedded in the walls. Theron noted that the statues, the marble of which lost its aggressive whiteness under the tinted lights, were mostly of naked men and women; the pictures, four or five in number, were all variations of a single theme﻿—the Virgin Mary and the Child.
A less untutored vision than his would have caught more swiftly the scheme of color and line in which these works of art bore their share. The walls of the room were in part of flat upright wooden columns, terminating high above in simple capitals, and they were all painted in pale amber and straw and primrose hues, irregularly wavering here and there toward suggestions of white. Between these pilasters were broader panels of stamped leather, in gently varying shades of peacock blue. These contrasted colors vaguely interwove and mingled in what he could see of the shadowed ceiling far above. They were repeated in the draperies and huge cushions and pillows of the low, wide divan which ran about three sides of the room. Even the floor, where it revealed itself among the scattered rugs, was laid in a mosaic pattern of matched woods, which, like the rugs, gave back these same shifting blues and uncertain yellows.
The fourth side of the apartment was broken in outline at one end by the door through which they had entered, and at the other by a broad, square opening, hung with looped-back curtains of a thin silken stuff.

===Chinese===

塞隆·韦尔环顾四周，惊愕得全不加掩饰。
他置身其间的这间屋子，起初那样暗，眼睛几乎得不着什么；得着的那一点，又全然出于他的理解力之外。他的目光无可奈何地跟着塞莉娅和她的蜡烛走，看她忙着点灯的活计。点完了，掐灭了纸媒，屋里共有七盏灯——灯光透过蓝黄相间的半透明长方格，柔和地照映。它们想必装在某种壁灯笼里，靠墙一圈，他想；可灯笼的形状他辨不大清。
渐渐他的眼睛适应了这幽光，开始看见别的东西。这些古怪的灯，摆的位置显然是要把一片特异的光辉，洒在立在屋角的几座雕像上，洒在嵌在墙里的几幅画上。塞隆注意到，那些雕像——大理石在染色灯光下失掉了咄咄逼人的白——多半是裸体的男人和女人；那几幅画，四五幅，全是同一个题目的变奏——圣母马利亚和圣婴。
比他更有教养的眼睛，本可以更快地捉住这些艺术品共同分担的那套色彩与线条的布局。屋子的墙，一部分是扁平的立柱，高处在简单的柱头上收束，一律漆成淡琥珀、麦秆和樱草的色调，这里那里不规则地波动着，渐近于白。柱与柱之间是更宽的压花皮革壁板，孔雀蓝的调子轻柔变幻。这两种对比的颜色，在高处阴影里他望得见的那部分天花板上，隐约交织融会。它们又再现于矮而宽的长榻的帷幔和巨大的坐垫靠枕里——那长榻沿屋子三面环设。连地板，在散铺的小毯之间露出的地方，也嵌木拼花，同小毯一样，回报着同样变幻的蓝和游移的黄。
房间的第四面，一头被他们进来的那道门截断轮廓，另一头是一个宽方的开口，挂着挽起的薄绸帷幕。

===Original===

Between the two apertures rose against the wall what Theron took at first glance to be an altar. There were pyramidal rows of tall candles here on either side, each masked with a little silken hood; below, in the centre, a shelf-like projection supported what seemed a massive, carved casket, and in the beautiful intricacies of this, and the receding canopy of delicate ornamentation which depended above it, the dominant color was white, deepening away in its shadows, by tenderly minute gradations, to the tints which ruled the rest of the room.
Celia lighted some of the high, thick tapers in these candelabra, and opened the top of the casket. Theron saw with surprise that she had uncovered the keyboard of a piano. He viewed with much greater amazement her next proceeding﻿—which was to put a cigarette between her lips, and, bending over one of the candles with it for an instant, turn to him with a filmy, opalescent veil of smoke above her head.
“Make yourself comfortable anywhere,” she said, with a gesture which comprehended all the divans and pillows in the place. “Will you smoke?”
“I have never tried since I was a little boy,” said Theron, “but I think I could. If you don’t mind, I should like to see.”
Lounging at his ease on the oriental couch, Theron experimented cautiously upon the unaccustomed tobacco, and looked at Celia with what he felt to be the confident quiet of a man of the world. She had thrown aside her hat, and in doing so had half released some of the heavy strands of hair coiled at the back of her head. His glance instinctively rested upon this wonderful hair of hers. There was no mistaking the sudden fascination its disorder had for his eye.
She stood before him with the cigarette poised daintily between thumb and finger of a shapely hand, and smiled comprehendingly down on her guest.
“I suffered the horrors of the damned with this hair of mine when I was a child,” she said. “I daresay all children have a taste for persecuting redheads; but it’s a specialty with Irish children. They get hold somehow of an ancient national superstition, or legend, that red hair was brought into Ireland by the Danes. It’s been a term of reproach with us since Brian Boru’s time to call a child a Dane.

===Chinese===

两个开口之间，靠墙立着一件东西，塞隆一眼看去，当是一座祭坛。两侧各立着金字塔形的高烛，每支都罩着一个小小的绸帽；下方正中，一个架状凸出部承着一件貌似沉重的雕花箱子；在它精美的繁复里，在悬垂其上的精致装饰的退入式华盖上，主宰的色调是白，在阴影里经由温柔的毫厘渐变，深深隐入统辖全屋其余部分的色调。
塞莉娅点亮这些烛台上几支高高的粗蜡烛，掀开箱盖。塞隆惊讶地看见，她揭开的是一架钢琴的键盘。她随后的举动，更叫他大为惊异——她把一支香烟衔上唇间，就着一支蜡烛俯身点了一瞬，随即向他转过身来，头顶上罩着一层薄纱似的、乳光虹彩的烟。
“随便哪儿坐，怎么舒服怎么来。”她说，一个手势把满屋的长榻靠枕统统圈了进去。“抽烟么？”
“小时候试过，后来再没有，”塞隆说，“不过我想我行。你不介意的话，我倒想见识见识。”
塞隆在东方式长榻上惬意地半躺着，小心翼翼地试着这不惯的烟，用他自认的、老于世故者的从容静定望着塞莉娅。她已甩掉帽子，顺势半放开了脑后盘着的那几绺厚重的头发。他的目光不由自主地落在她这头奇发上。它那凌乱之态骤然施于他眼中的魅力，无可误会。
她立在他面前，一支烟纤巧地拈在一只秀气的手的拇指与食指之间，含着会心的笑，俯看她的客人。
“小时候，这头发叫我吃了被诅咒者才受的那份罪，”她说，“想必天下的孩子都爱迫害红头发；可这在爱尔兰孩子是一门专科。他们不知怎么抓着了一则古老的国家迷信，或者传说，说红头发是丹麦人带进爱尔兰的。从布赖恩·博鲁那时候起，骂一个孩子‘丹麦佬’，在我们那里就是一句现成的辱骂。

===Original===

I used to be pursued and baited with it every day of my life, until the one dream of my ambition was to get old enough to be a Sister of Charity, so that I might hide my hair under one of their big beastly white linen caps. I’ve got rather away from that ideal since, I’m afraid,” she added, with a droll downward curl of her lip.
“Your hair is very beautiful,” said Theron, in the calm tone of a connoisseur.
“I like it myself,” Celia admitted, and blew a little smoke-ring toward him. “I’ve made this whole room to match it. The colors, I mean,” she explained, in deference to his uplifted brows. “Between us, we make up what Whistler would call a symphony. That reminds me﻿—I was going to play for you. Let me finish the cigarette first.”
Theron felt grateful for her reticence about the fact that he had laid his own aside. “I have never seen a room at all like this,” he remarked. “You are right; it does fit you perfectly.”
She nodded her sense of his appreciation. “It is what I like,” she said. “It expresses *me*. I will not have anything about me﻿—or anybody either﻿—that I don’t like. I suppose if an old Greek could see it, it would make him sick, but it represents what *I* mean by being a Greek. It is as near as an Irishman can get to it.”
“I remember your puzzling me by saying that you were a Greek.”
Celia laughed, and tossed the cigarette-end away. “I’d puzzle you more, I’m afraid, if I tried to explain to you what I really meant by it. I divide people up into two classes, you know﻿—Greeks and Jews. Once you get hold of that principle, all other divisions and classifications, such as by race or language or nationality, seem pure foolishness. It is the only true division there is. It is just as true among negroes or wild Indians who never heard of Greece or Jerusalem, as it is among white folks. That is the beauty of it. It works everywhere, always.”
“Try it on me,” urged Theron, with a twinkling eye. “Which am I?”
“Both,” said the girl, with a merry nod of the head. “But now I’ll play. I told you you were to hear Chopin. I prescribe him for you. He is the Greekiest of the Greeks. *There* was a nation where all the people were artists, where everybody was an intellectual aristocrat, where the Philistine was as unknown, as extinct, as the dodo. Chopin might have written his music for them.”

===Chinese===

“我从前天天被人追着咬着拿这个取笑，一生里唯一的雄心梦，就是快快长到能当慈善会修女，好把这头发藏进他们那顶又大又可憎的白布帽底下。后来我算是离那个理想相当远了，恐怕。”她添一句，嘴唇朝下滑稽地一弯。
“你的头发美极了。”塞隆说，用鉴赏家的平静口吻。
“我自己也喜欢，”塞莉娅承认，朝他吹了个小烟圈。“这整间屋子就是照着它布置的。颜色，我是说，”她见他扬眉，补了一句。“咱俩合起来，正好凑成惠斯勒所说的那种交响曲。这倒提醒我——我答应弹琴给你听的。先让我把这支烟抽完。”
塞隆把自己那支搏在了一边，她竟不提；他对这份含蓄心怀感激。“我从没见过这样一间屋子，”他说，“你说得对；它跟你再配不过。”
她点头领受他的赏识。“这正是我喜欢的，”她说，“它表现*我*。我身边——身边任何人也是一样——凡我不喜欢的，一概不要。倘若一个古希腊人看见，怕要恶心；可它代表的，是*我*所说的做一个希腊人。一个爱尔兰人所能凑近的，也就到这一步了。”
“我记得你说过你是希腊人，把我弄得一头雾水。”
塞莉娅笑了，把烟头抛掉。“我要是当真解释起来，怕更要叫你糊涂。你知道，我把人分成两类——希腊人和犹太人。这条原则一旦抓住，其余一切分法，什么按种族、按语言、按国籍，都显得纯粹是愚蠢。这是唯一真确的分法。在从未听说过希腊或耶路撒冷的黑人和野印第安人中间，它同在白人中间一样真确。妙就妙在这里。它到处灵，永远灵。”
“拿我试试，”塞隆怂恿，眼里一闪。“我是哪一类？”
“两样都是。”姑娘说着，快活地点一点头。“不过现在我要弹琴了。我说过你要听肖邦。我拿他当方子开给你。他是希腊人里最希腊的。*那才*是一个国度，人人是艺术家，个个是智识的贵族，市侩在那里同渡渡鸟一样无人知晓、一样绝迹。肖邦的音乐，简直可以就是为他们写的。”

===Original===

“I am interested in Shopang,” put in Theron, suddenly recalling Sister Soulsby’s confidences as to the source of her tunes. “He lived with﻿—what’s his name﻿—George something. We were speaking about him only this afternoon.”
Celia looked down into her visitor’s face at first inquiringly, then with a latent grin about her lips. “Yes﻿—George something,” she said, in a tone which mystified him.
The Rev. Mr. Ware was sitting up, a minute afterward, in a ferment of awakened consciousness that he had never heard the piano played before. After a little, he noiselessly rearranged the cushions, and settled himself again in a recumbent posture. It was beyond his strength to follow that first impulse, and keep his mind abreast with what his ears took in. He sighed and lay back, and surrendered his senses to the mere unthinking charm of it all.
It was the Fourth Prelude that was singing in the air about him﻿—a simple, plaintive strain wandering at will over a surface of steady rhythmic movement underneath, always creeping upward through mysteries of sweetness, always sinking again in cadences of semitones. With only a moment’s pause, there came the Seventh Waltz﻿—a rich, bold confusion which yet was not confused. Theron’s ears dwelt with eager delight upon the chasing medley of swift, tinkling sounds, but it left his thoughts free.
From where he reclined, he turned his head to scrutinize, one by one, the statues in the corners. No doubt they were beautiful﻿—for this was a department in which he was all humility﻿—and one of them, the figure of a broad-browed, stately, though thick-waisted woman, bending slightly forward and with both arms broken off, was decently robed from the hips downward. The others were not robed at all. Theron stared at them with the erratic, rippling jangle of the waltz in his ears, and felt that he possessed a new and disturbing conception of what female emancipation meant in these later days. Roving along the wall, his glance rested again upon the largest of the Virgin pictures﻿—a full-length figure in sweeping draperies, its radiant, aureoled head upturned in rapt adoration, its feet resting on a crescent moon which shone forth in bluish silver through festooned clouds of cherubs. The incongruity between the unashamed statues and this serene incarnation of holy womanhood jarred upon him for the instant. Then his mind went to the piano.

===Chinese===

“我对‘肖庞’倒有兴趣，”塞隆插话，蓦地想起索尔斯比姊妹说过的她那些曲调的来源。“他同——那谁——乔治什么的一块儿住。就在今天下午，咱们还说起他来着。”
塞莉娅朝她客人的脸上先望了一眼，问讯似的；随后唇边含着一丝潜在的笑。“是——乔治什么。”她说，那腔调叫他摸不着头脑。
一分钟后，韦尔牧师直起身来，意识沸腾：他这是平生头一回听人弹钢琴。过了一阵，他又不出声地重摆了摆靠垫，重新躺下。要跟着第一股冲动走，让头脑同耳朵齐头并进，非他力所能及。他叹口气，向后一仰，把感官交给了这一切全不假思索的魅力。
在他四周的空气里歌唱着的，是第四前奏曲——一支简单、哀婉的曲调，随意游移在底下平稳的节奏面上，总是在甜美的奥秘里向上潜行，又总在半音的终止里重新沉落。只停了一瞬，来了第七圆舞曲——一片丰腴、大胆的纷乱，然而并不乱。塞隆的耳朵怀着热切的欢喜，流连在那追逐着的、叮咚作响的疾音的大杂烩上；可他的思绪倒是自由的。
他从躺处侧过头，把屋角的雕像一座一座细细端详。无疑它们是美的——在这一门上他满心谦卑——其中一座，一个阔额、端庄然而腰身粗厚的女子，微微前俯，双臂俱断，从胯以下倒是体面地遮着。其余的什么也没有遮。塞隆耳中响着圆舞曲那错落荡漾的铮鏦，瞪着它们，自觉获得了一种新的、扰人的概念：女性的解放在这些晚近的日子里究竟意味着什么。目光沿墙游去，又停在那幅最大的圣母像上——一个全身像，衣袂飘举，那颗罩着光轮的头仰起着，出神地敬拜，脚踩一弯新月，新月透过成串的小天使之云，泛着蓝银色的光。不知羞耻的雕像同这圣洁女性之宁静化身之间的不谐，一时擦痛了他。随后他的心思回到钢琴上。

===Original===

Without a break the waltz had slowed and expanded into a passage of what might be church music, an exquisitely modulated and gently solemn chant, through which a soft, lingering song roved capriciously, forcing the listener to wonder where it was coming out, even while it caressed and soothed to repose.
He looked from the Madonna to Celia. Beyond the carelessly drooping braids and coils of hair which blazed between the candles, he could see the outline of her brow and cheek, the noble contour of her lifted chin and full, modelled throat, all pink as the most delicate rose leaf is pink, against the cool lights of the altar-like wall. The sight convicted him in the court of his own soul as a prurient and mean-minded rustic. In the presence of such a face, of such music, there ceased to be any such thing as nudity, and statues no more needed clothes than did those slow, deep, magnificent chords which came now, gravely accumulating their spell upon him.
“It is all singing!” the player called out to him over her shoulder, in a minute of rest. “That is what Chopin does﻿—he sings!”
She began, with an effect of thinking of something else, the Sixth Nocturne, and Theron at first thought she was not playing anything in particular, so deliberately, haltingly, did the chain of charm unwind itself into sequence. Then it came closer to him than the others had done. The dreamy, wistful, meditative beauty of it all at once oppressed and inspired him. He saw Celia’s shoulders sway under the impulse of the rubato license﻿—the privilege to invest each measure with the stress of the whole, to loiter, to weep, to run and laugh at will﻿—and the music she made spoke to him as with a human voice. There was the wooing sense of roses and moonlight, of perfumes, white skins, alluring languorous eyes, and then﻿—
“You know this part, of course,” he heard her say.
On the instant they had stepped from the dark, scented, starlit garden, where the nightingale sang, into a great cathedral. A sombre and lofty anthem arose, and filled the place with the splendor of such dignified pomp of harmony and such suggestions of measureless choral power and authority that Theron sat abruptly up, then was drawn resistlessly to his feet. He stood motionless in the strange room, feeling most of all that one should kneel to hear such music.

===Chinese===

圆舞曲不曾间断，缓缓膨胀成一段堪称为教堂音乐的乐章——一支调制精微、温柔庄严的圣咏，其间一支柔缓流连的歌任性游走，逼得听的人一边受它抚慰入静，一边悬想它究竟要从哪里出来。
他从圣母像望向塞莉娅。越过烛台之间燃烧的几盘随意的发辫与发髻，他望得见她额头和面颊的轮廓，望得见她仰起的下颌和丰盈塑就的颈项的高贵线条——全都透着粉，像最娇嫩的玫瑰瓣那样的粉，衬着祭坛般的墙面那清冷的光。这景象在他自己灵魂的法庭上给他定了罪：一个淫猥而心地龌龊的乡巴佬。在这样的脸、这样的音乐面前，赤身露体这回事便不复存在；雕像之无需衣裳，正如那些此刻庄重地积攒着符咒、落在能身上的迟缓、深沉、壮丽的和弦之无需衣裳。
“全都是歌唱！”弹琴的人越过肩头向他喊道，在歇息的一分钟里。“肖邦的本事就在这里——他歌唱！”
她像想着别的事似的，起了第六夜曲；塞隆起初还当她不是在弹什么特别的曲子，那串魅力的链是这样从容、这样迟疑地舒展成序。随后它比先前几支更贴近他了。那梦境似的、怅惘的、沉思的美，一时压迫他、一时鼓舞他。他看见塞莉娅的双肩随自由节拍的放任而摇曳——那特权：把全曲的分量注进每一小节，可以流连，可以哭泣，可以奔跑，可以随时大笑——她造出的音乐对他说话，像用一副人的嗓音。其中有玫瑰和月光的引诱之感，有香气，有白皙的肌肤，有诱人而倦慵的眼睛，随后——
“这一段你当然知道。”他听见她说。
转瞬之间，他们已从那夜莺歌唱的幽暗、芬芳、星光的花圃，跨进了一座大教堂。一支阴郁而高峻的颂歌升起，以那样尊严的华彩、那样浩大无边的合唱之力与威的暗示，充满了这地方，塞隆霍地坐起，随即又被不可抗地引到脚边。他一动不动立在这间古怪的屋子里，最强烈地感到的是：听这样的音乐，应当下跪。

===Original===

“This you’ll know too﻿—the funeral march from the Second Sonata,” she was saying, before he realized that the end of the other had come. He sank upon the divan again, bending forward and clasping his hands tight around his knees. His heart beat furiously as he listened to the weird, medieval processional, with its wild, clashing chords held down in the bondage of an orderly sadness. There was a propelling motion in the thing﻿—a sense of being borne bodily along﻿—which affected him like dizziness. He breathed hard through the robust portions of stern, vigorous noise, and rocked himself to and fro when, as rosy morn breaks upon a storm-swept night, the drums are silenced for the sweet, comforting strain of solitary melody. The clanging minor harmonies into which the march relapses came to their abrupt end. Theron rose once more, and moved with a hesitating step to the piano.
“I want to rest a little,” he said, with his hand on her shoulder.
“Whew! so do I,” exclaimed Celia, letting her hands fall with an exaggerated gesture of weariness. “The sonatas take it out of one! They are hideously difficult, you know. They are rarely played.”
“I didn’t know,” remarked Theron. She seemed not to mind his hand upon her shoulder, and he kept it there. “I didn’t know anything about music at all. What I do know now is that﻿—that this evening is an event in my life.”
She looked up at him and smiled. He read unsuspected tendernesses and tolerances of friendship in the depths of her eyes, which emboldened him to stir the fingers of that audacious hand in a lingering, caressing trill upon her shoulder. The movement was of the faintest, but having ventured it, he drew his hand abruptly away.
“You are getting on,” she said to him. There was an enigmatic twinkle in the smile with which she continued to regard him. “We are Hellenizing you at a great rate.”
A sudden thought seemed to strike her. She shifted her eyes toward vacancy with a swift, abstracted glance, reflected for a moment, then let a sparkling half-wink and the dimpling beginnings of an almost roguish smile mark her assent to the conceit, whatever it might be.
“I will be with you in a moment,” he heard her say; and while the words were still in his ears she had risen and passed out of sight through the broad, open doorway to the right. The looped curtains fell together behind her.

===Chinese===

“这个你也会认得——第二奏鸣曲里的葬礼进行曲。”不等他察觉前一支已尽，她已经在说了。他重新落回长榻，前俯着，双手紧紧扣住膝头。他听着那中古的、野性的行列曲，听着那狂乱冲撞的和弦被拘在一种有秩序的悲悼底下，心跳得凶猛。这东西里有一股推进的动力——一种被整个载着走的感觉——像晕眩一样攫住了他。在那刚劲猛烈的轰响的段落里，他粗重地喘息；当鼓声沉寂、独奏的旋律浮出甜美慰藉的曲调，如玫瑰色的晨破开暴风雨扫荡过的夜，他又前后摇晃。进行曲重新跌回的那些铿锵的小调和声，蓦然收束。塞隆再度起身，迈着迟疑的脚步向钢琴走去。
“我想歇一小会儿。”他说，一只手搭上她的肩。
“呼！我也想，”塞莉娅喊道，夸张地做一个倦极的手势，任双手垂落。“奏鸣曲最耗人！难得吓人，你知道。很少人弹。”
“我不知道。”塞隆说。她似乎不介意他的手搭在肩上，他便留在那里。“我对音乐本来就一无所知。如今我知道的是——今天晚上，是我生平的一件大事。”
她抬眼望他，笑了。他读出她眼睛深处不曾料到的友情的温厚与宽容，胆子壮了，那只放肆的手的手指，在她肩上流连地、爱抚地颤了一颤。动作轻极了；可一旦做了，他又猛地把手抽回。
“你有长进了。”她对他说。她继续端详他的微笑里，带着一个谜样的闪动。“我们正在飞快地把你希腊化。”
一个念头似乎蓦地击中她。她把眼睛疾速而恍惚地一扫，投向虚空，沉吟片刻，随即让一只闪亮的半眨眼、一阵几乎称得上淘气的笑靥初晕，标示她对——无论那是什么——念头的应允。
“我一会儿就回来。”他听见她说；话音犹在耳际，她已起身，从右边那扇宽洞口出去，没了影。挽着的帷幕在她身后合拢。

===Original===

Presently a mellow light spread over their delicately translucent surface﻿—a creamy, undulating radiance which gave the effect of moving about among the myriad folds of the silk.
Theron gazed at these curtains for a little, then straightened his shoulders with a gesture of decision, and, turning on his heel, went over and examined the statues in the further corners minutely.
“If you would like some more, I will play you the Berceuse now.”
Her voice came to him with a delicious shock. He wheeled round and beheld her standing at the piano, with one hand resting, palm upward, on the keys. She was facing him. Her tall form was robed now in some shapeless, clinging drapery, lustrous and creamy and exquisitely soft, like the curtains. The wonderful hair hung free and luxuriant about her neck and shoulders, and glowed with an intensity of fiery color which made all the other hues of the room pale and vague. A fillet of faint, sky-like blue drew a gracious span through the flame of red above her temples, and from this there rose the gleam of jewels. Her head inclined gently, gravely, toward him﻿—with the posture of that armless woman in marble he had been studying﻿—and her brown eyes, regarding him from the shadows, emitted light.
“It is a lullaby﻿—the only one he wrote,” she said, as Theron, pale-faced and with tightened lips, approached her. “No﻿—you mustn’t stand there,” she added, sinking into the seat before the instrument; “go back and sit where you were.”
The most perfect of lullabies, with its swaying abandonment to cooing rhythm, ever and again rising in ripples to the point of insisting on something, one knows not what, and then rocking, melting away once more, passed, so to speak, over Theron’s head. He leaned back upon the cushions, and watched the white, rounded forearm which the falling folds of this strange, statue-like drapery made bare.
There was more that appealed to his mood in the Third Ballade. It seemed to him that there were words going along with it﻿—incoherent and impulsive yet very earnest words, appealing to him in strenuous argument and persuasion. Each time he almost knew what they said, and strained after their meaning with a passionate desire, and then there would come a kind of cuckoo call, and everything would swing dancing off again into a mockery of inconsequence.

===Chinese===

片刻后，一层醇光在那半透的绸面上漫开——乳白的、荡漾的光辉，像在绸的万千皱褶间移动的效果。
塞隆对着帷幕望了一阵，随即果断地一挺肩膀，转身走过去，把远处角落里的雕像一一细细察看。
“要是你还想听，我现在给你弹《摇篮曲》。”
她的声音带着一阵甜美的震骇传到他这里。他霍地回身，看见她立在琴边，一只手掌心朝上，搭在琴键上。她面向着他。她高挑的身量此刻裹在一袭无形无状、贴身的衣裾里，光润、乳白、柔软之极，同帷幕一样。那头奇发自由而丰盛地垂在颈项和肩头，燃着那样炽烈的火色，把屋里其余一切的色调都照得黯淡而朦胧。一道淡得近乎天色的蓝发带，在她鬓边的火焰里牵过一道雅致的跨度，带上闪着珠宝的光。她的头朝他微微地、庄重地一俯——同他方才端详的那尊断臂石女的姿势一样——她那双棕色的眼睛从阴影里望着他，放出光来。
“这是一支摇篮曲——他写的唯一一支。”她说；塞隆脸色发白、抿紧了唇，向她走近。“不——你别站在那儿，”她添一句，在琴前的座位里坐了下去；“回去，坐你原来的地方。”
一支至美至善的摇篮曲，那样摇荡地沉酣于咕哝似的节奏，时时又漾起涟漪，升到执意要求着什么——不知是什么——的顶点，随后又摇摆着、消融而去，可以说，从塞隆头顶上流过。他向后靠着软垫，望着那截白皙浑圆的小臂——这袭古怪的、雕像似的衣裾的下垂的褶，把它裸露出来。
第三叙事曲里有更多东西应和他的心境。他仿佛觉得其中有话语在同行——不成句、冲动、却极其恳切的话语，以热烈的争辩和劝说向他吁求。每回他几乎要听懂它们说什么了，怀着热烈的渴愿去够它们的意思，随后一声杜鹃似的啼响，一切又舞着荡开，化作对无关紧要的嘲弄。

===Original===

Upon the silence there fell the pure, liquid, mellifluous melody of a soft-throated woman singing to her lover.
“It is like Heine﻿—simply a love-poem,” said the girl, over her shoulder.
Theron followed now with all his senses, as she carried the Ninth Nocturne onward. The stormy passage, which she banged finely forth, was in truth a lover’s quarrel; and then the mild, placid flow of sweet harmonies into which the furore sank, dying languorously away upon a silence all alive with tender memories of sound﻿—was that not also a part of love?
They sat motionless through a minute﻿—the man on the divan, the girl at the piano﻿—and Theron listened for what he felt must be the audible thumping of his heart.
Then, throwing back her head, with upturned face, Celia began what she had withheld for the last﻿—the Sixteenth Mazurka. This strange foreign thing she played with her eyes closed, her head tilted obliquely so that Theron could see the rose-tinted, beautiful countenance, framed as if asleep in the billowing luxuriance of unloosed auburn hair. He fancied her beholding visions as she wrought the music﻿—visions full of barbaric color and romantic forms. As his mind swam along with the gliding, tricksy phantom of a tune, it seemed as if he too could see these visions﻿—as if he gazed at them through her eyes.
It could not be helped. He lifted himself noiselessly to his feet, and stole with caution toward her. He would hear the rest of this weird, voluptuous fantasy standing thus, so close behind her that he could look down upon her full, uplifted lace﻿—so close that, if she moved, that glowing nimbus of hair would touch him.
There had been some curious and awkward pauses in this last piece, which Theron, by some side cerebration, had put down to her not watching what her fingers did. There came another of these pauses now﻿—an odd, unaccountable halt in what seemed the middle of everything. He stared intently down upon her statuesque, dreaming face during the hush, and caught his breath as he waited. There fell at last a few faltering ascending notes, making a half-finished strain, and then again there was silence.
Celia opened her eyes, and poured a direct, deep gaze into the face above hers. Its pale lips were parted in suspense, and the color had faded from its cheeks.

===Chinese===

寂静之上，落下一段纯净、流丽、蜜也似的旋律——一个柔嗓的女人唱给她的情人。
“它像海涅——就是一首情诗。”姑娘越过肩头说。
她把第九夜曲往下带，塞隆此刻全副感官地追随。那阵她漂亮地砸出的狂暴的乐段，实在是一场情人的口角；随后怒气沉落进去的那道温和平顺的甜美和声的流，倦慵地死在一片被声音的温柔记忆激活了的寂静上——那难道不也是爱情的一部分？
两人一动不动坐了一分钟——男人在长榻上，姑娘在琴前——塞隆听着，听他断定必是自己心脏怦怦的可闻之声。
随后，塞莉娅仰起脸，把留到最后的那支——第十六玛祖卡——起了头。这一支异域的怪东西，她闭着眼弹，头斜侧着，好叫塞隆望见那张红润的、美丽的脸，像睡在一框汹涌丰盛的散开红发里。他想象她织造音乐时看见了幻象——满是蛮野色彩和浪漫形状的幻象。当他的心随那一滑一谑的曲调的幽灵浮游，他仿佛也看得见这些幻象——仿佛借她的眼睛在凝望。
没有办法。他无声地抬身而起，小心翼翼地向她潜近。这怪异、旖旎的狂想曲的余下部分，他要这样站着听——立得那样贴近她的背后，俯视得见她那张丰满仰起的脸——贴近得她一动，那圈发光的发晕便会触到他。
这最后一支曲子里有过几处古怪而尴尬的停顿，塞隆凭脑子一旁的分神，归因于她没有看手指的动向。此刻又来了这样一次停顿——在仿佛一切的半中间，一次古怪、无从解释的顿住。静默中他专注地俯望她那张雕像般入梦的脸，屏着息等待。末了落下几声踉跄上行的音，成半截子曲调，随后又是寂静。
塞莉娅睁开眼睛，把一道直接而深沉的注视，泼进她上方的那张脸。那张脸的灰白嘴唇悬着微张，颊上的血色褪尽了。

===Original===

“That is the end,” she said, and, with a turn of her lithe body, stood swiftly up, even while the echoes of the broken melody seemed panting in the air about her for completion.
Theron put his hands to his face, and pressed them tightly against eyes and brow for an instant. Then, throwing them aside with an expansive downward sweep of the arms, and holding them clenched, he returned Celia’s glance. It was as if he had never looked into a woman’s eyes before.
“It *can’t* be the end!” he heard himself saying, in a low voice charged with deep significance. He held her gaze in the grasp of his with implacable tenacity. There was a trouble about breathing, and the mosaic floor seemed to stir under his feet. He clung defiantly to the one idea of not releasing her eyes.
“How *could* it be the end?” he demanded, lifting an uncertain hand to his breast as he spoke, and spreading it there as if to control the tumultuous fluttering of his heart. “Things don’t end that way!”
A sharp, blinding spasm of giddiness closed upon and shook him, while the brave words were on his lips. He blinked and tottered under it, as it passed, and then backed humbly to his divan and sat down, gasping a little, and patting his hand on his heart. There was fright written all over his whitened face.
“We﻿—we forgot that I am a sick man,” he said feebly, answering Celia’s look of surprised inquiry with a forced, wan smile. “I was afraid my heart had gone wrong.”
She scrutinized him for a further moment, with growing reassurance in her air. Then, piling up the pillows and cushions behind him for support, for all the world like a big sister again, she stepped into the inner room, and returned with a flagon of quaint shape and a tiny glass. She poured this latter full to the brim of a thick yellowish, aromatic liquid, and gave it him to drink.
“This Benedictine is all I happen to have,” she said. “Swallow it down. It will do you good.”
Theron obeyed her. It brought tears to his eyes; but, upon reflection, it was grateful and warming. He did feel better almost immediately. A great wave of comfort seemed to enfold him as he settled himself back on the divan. For that one flashing instant he had thought that he was dying. He drew a long grateful breath of relief, and smiled his content.
Celia had seated herself beside him, a little away.

===Chinese===

“完了。”她说，身子一拧，利落地站起身——断掉的旋律的回声，仿佛还在她四周的空气里喘息着讨要下文。
塞隆双手掩面，把眼睛和额头紧紧按了一瞬。随后他把双手向下一挥，甩开，握紧着，回敬塞莉娅的凝望。这仿佛是他生平头一回望进一个女人的眼睛。
“*不可能*完！”他听见自己说，低低的嗓音里载着深意。他以不依不饶的执着，把她目光攥在他的目光里。呼吸乱了，脚下那拼花地板仿佛也在动。他倔强地死抱住一个念头：不放她的眼睛。
“*怎么会*完呢？”他质问，说话时把一只没把握的手举到胸前，摊开按在那里，仿佛要按住心脏狂乱的扑腾。“事情不会这样完法！”
一句尖利的、叫人眼花的晕眩，趁着豪言还在唇边，攫住他、摇撼他。他眨着眼、打着晃，挨过那一阵，随即低声下气地退回长榻坐下，微微喘着，用手拍着心口。煞白的脸上写满了惊惶。
“咱们——咱们忘了我是个病人。”他虚弱地说，用一个勉强的、惨淡的笑回答塞莉娅惊讶探问的一望。“我还当我的心脏出毛病了。”
她又审视了他一刻，神色里放心渐多。随后她把靠垫软枕在他背后堆起来撑住他——十足又成了一个大姐姐——跨进里间，捧出一只古雅的酒壶和一只小杯。她把小杯斟得齐边满，一种浓稠微黄、芳香的酒液，递给他喝。
“手边只有这种本笃甜酒，”她说，“咽下去。于你有好处。”
塞隆依了。酒呛出他两眼泪水；可回味起来，受用而暖和。差不多立时他真觉得好些了。他在长榻上重新安顿下来时，仿佛有一大浪的舒适裹住了他。方才那闪电般的一瞬，他还当自己要死了。他长长地、感激地舒出一口气，微笑出他的满足。
塞莉娅在他身旁坐下，隔开一点。

===Original===

She sat with her head against the wall, and one foot curled under her, and almost faced him.
“I dare say we forced the pace a little,” she remarked, after a pause, looking down at the floor, with the puckers of a ruminating amusement playing in the corners of her mouth. “It doesn’t do for a man to get to be a Greek all of a sudden. He must work along up to it gradually.”
He remembered the music. “Oh, if I only knew how to tell you,” he murmured ecstatically, “what a revelation your playing has been to me! I had never imagined anything like it. I shall think of it to my dying day.”
He began to remember as well the spirit that was in the air when the music ended. The details of what he had felt and said rose vaguely in his mind. Pondering them, his eye roved past Celia’s white-robed figure to the broad, open doorway beyond. The curtains behind which she had disappeared were again parted and fastened back. A dim light was burning within, out of sight, and its faint illumination disclosed a room filled with white marbles, white silks, white draperies of varying sorts, which shaped themselves, as he looked, into the canopy and trappings of an extravagantly oversized and sumptuous bed. He looked away again.
“I wish you would tell me what you really mean by that Greek idea of yours,” he said with the abruptness of confusion.
Celia did not display much enthusiasm in the tone of her answer. “Oh,” she said almost indifferently, “lots of things. Absolute freedom from moral bugbears, for one thing. The recognition that beauty is the only thing in life that is worth while. The courage to kick out of one’s life everything that isn’t worth while; and so on.”
“But,” said Theron, watching the mingled delicacy and power of the bared arm and the shapely grace of the hand which she had lifted to her face, “I am going to get you to teach it *all* to me.” The memories began crowding in upon him now, and the baffling note upon which the mazurka had stopped short chimed like a tuning-fork in his ears. “I want to be a Greek myself, if you’re one. I want to get as close to you﻿—to your ideal, that is, as I can. You open up to me a whole world that I had not even dreamed existed. We swore our friendship long ago, you know: and now, after tonight﻿—you and the music have decided me. I am going to put the things out of *my* life that are not worthwhile.

===Chinese===

她头靠着墙坐，一只脚蜷在身下，几乎同他面对面。
“想来咱们把步子逼得急了点，”停了停她说，眼睛望着地板，嘴角漾着反刍般逗趣的纹。“一个人一下子变成希腊人，可不行。得慢慢往上熬。”
他想起了那音乐。“哦，但愿我知道怎样告诉你，”他心醉神迷地低语，“你的琴于我是怎样一场启示！这样的事我连想象都没有想象过。到死那天我还会想着它。”
他也开始记起音乐收束时空气里的那股气氛。他方才的所感所言，细节纷纷依稀浮上心头。他一面想，一面把目光从塞莉娅白裳的身影旁掠过，投向更远处那扇宽大的洞口。她消失过的那道帷幕，如今又分开挽住了。里头看不见的地方亮着一盏暗灯，那微光透出来，照出一屋子的白色大理石、白绸、形形色色的白色帷幔；他望着望着，它们自己拼合起来，成为一张大得出奇、极尽奢华的床的华盖和铺陈。他把眼又挪开了。
“我但愿你告诉我，你那个希腊人的说法，到底是什么意思。”他带着慌乱的突兀说。
塞莉娅的答话没显出多少热忱。“哦，”她近乎淡漠地说，“意思多着呢。头一条，是绝对摆脱道德上的妖怪。是承认美是人生里唯一值得的东西。是有胆量把人生里一切不值得的东西统统踢出去；等等。”
“可是，”塞隆说，望着她抬到脸旁的那条裸臂的柔中带劲、那只手的秀雅，“我要请你把这一套*统统*教给我。”回忆此刻纷纷涌上他；玛祖卡戛然而止的那声叫人悬心的音，像音叉一样在他耳边嗡鸣。“你自己要是希腊人，我自己也要做希腊人。我要尽我所能地贴近你——贴近你的理想，我是说。你向我打开了一个我做梦也没想过它存在的整个世界。咱们的友谊早就立过誓了，你知道：如今，过了今晚——你和这音乐替我拿定了主意。我也要把不值得的东西，从*我的*人生里清出去。

===Original===

Only you must help me; you must tell me how to begin.”
He looked up as he spoke, to enforce the almost tender entreaty of his words. The spectacle of a yawn, only fractionally concealed behind those talented fingers, chilled his soft speech, and sent a flush over his face. He rose on the instant.
Celia was nothing abashed at his discovery. She laughed gayly in confession of her fault, and held her hand out to let him help her disentangle her foot from her draperies, and get off the divan. It seemed to be her meaning that he should continue holding her hand after she was also standing.
“You forgive me, don’t you?” she urged smilingly. “Chopin always first excites me, then sends me to sleep. You see how *you* sleep tonight!”
The brown, velvety eyes rested upon him, from under their heavy lids, with a languorous kindliness. Her warm, large palm clasped his in frank liking.
“I don’t want to sleep at all,” Mr. Ware was impelled to say. “I want to lie awake and think about﻿—about everything all over again.”
She smiled drowsily. “And you’re sure you feel strong enough to walk home?”
“Yes,” he replied, with a lingering dilatory note, which deepened upon reflection into a sigh. “Oh, yes.”
He followed her and her candle down the magnificent stairway again. She blew the light out in the hall, and, opening the front door, stood with him for a silent moment on the threshold. Then they shook hands once more, and with a whispered good night, parted.
Celia, returning to the blue and yellow room, lighted a cigarette and helped herself to some Benedictine in the glass which Theron had used. She looked meditatively at this little glass for a moment, turning it about in her fingers with a smile. The smile warmed itself suddenly into a joyous laugh. She tossed the glass aside, and, holding out her flowing skirts with both hands, executed a swinging pirouette in front of the gravely beautiful statue of the armless woman.

===Chinese===

“只是你必须帮我；你必须告诉我从哪儿起步。”
他说话时抬起眼，好加重这话里近乎温存的恳求。一幅景象——一个哈欠，在那双多才的指头后面只遮住一半——冷了他这一篇软语，叫他满脸通红。他立刻起身。
塞莉娅被他识破，毫不见窘。她快活地笑着，认下这过失，伸出手来，让他帮她把蜷着的脚从衣裾里解出来，扶下长榻。她的意思似乎是，她站起之后，他可以继续握着那只手。
“你原谅我，是不是？”她含笑催问。“肖邦总是先叫我兴奋，然后叫我瞌睡。瞧你今晚*怎么*睡吧！”
那双棕色、天鹅绒似的眼睛，从沉重的眼睑底下望着他，带着一种倦慵的好意。她温热宽大的手掌，坦然的喜欢，握着他的手。
“我根本不想睡。”韦尔先生不由得说。“我要醒着躺着想——把一切从头到尾再想一遍。”
她困倦地笑了。“你拿得准，你有力气走回家？”
“拿得准。”他答，话音里带着一记迁延的拖腔，回味之下又深成一叹。“哦，拿得准。”
他跟着她和她的蜡烛，再下一次那道堂皇的楼梯。她在门厅里吹灭烛火，开了前门，同他在门槛上静立了片刻。随后他们又握了一回手，低声道过晚安，分了手。
塞莉娅回到那间蓝黄的屋子，点上一支烟，用塞隆用过那只杯子，给自己斟了些本笃甜酒。她若有所思地对着这只小杯望了一会儿，手指间转着它，含着笑。那笑蓦地自己暖成了欢乐的一笑。她把杯子抛在一边，双手提起飘垂的裙子，在那尊断臂女子的庄穆秀美的雕像前，甩开一场荡漾的旋舞。

### XV / 十五
===Original===

A love-feast at nine in the morning opened the public services of a Sunday still memorable in the annals of Octavius Methodism.
This ceremony, which four times a year preceded the sessions of the Quarterly Conference, was not necessarily an event of importance. It was an occasion upon which the brethren and sisters who clung to the old-fashioned, primitive ways of the itinerant circuit-riders, let themselves go with emphasized independence, putting up more vehement prayers than usual, and adding a special fervor of noise to their “Amens!” and other interjections﻿—and that was all.
It was Theron’s first love-feast in Octavius, and as the big classroom in the church basement began to fill up, and he noted how the men with ultra radical views and the women clad in the most ostentatious drabs and grays were crowding into the front seats, he felt his spirits sinking. He had literally to force himself from sentence to sentence, when the time came for him to rise and open the proceedings with an exhortation. He had eagerly offered this function to the Presiding Elder, the Rev. Aziel P. Larrabee, who sat in severe silence on the little platform behind him, but had been informed that the dignitary would lead off in giving testimony later on. So Theron, feeling all the while the hostile eyes of the Elder burning holes in his back, dragged himself somehow through the task. He had never known any such difficulty of speech before. The relief was almost overwhelming when he came to the customary part where all are adjured to be as brief as possible in witnessing for the Lord, because the time belongs to all the people, and the Discipline forbids the feast to last more than ninety minutes. He delivered this injunction to brevity with marked earnestness, and then sat down abruptly.
There was some rather boisterous singing, during which the stewards, beginning with the platform, passed plates of bread cut in small cubes, and water in big plated pitchers and tumblers, about among the congregation, threading their way between the long wooden benches ordinarily occupied at this hour by the children of the Sunday-school, and helping each brother and sister in turn. They held by the old custom, here in Octavius, and all along the seats the sexes alternated, as they do at a polite dinner-table.
Theron impassively watched the familiar scene. The early nervousness had passed away.

===Chinese===

早晨九点钟的一场爱筵，为这个在奥克塔维乌斯美以美会史册上至今难忘的礼拜天拉开了公众礼拜的序幕。
这仪式每季度议会开会前举行四次，未必是什么大事。在这样的场合，那些抱守巡回骑师老派原始之风的弟兄姊妹，放任自己，格外地张扬独立：祷告比平常更激烈，“阿们”和各样的插叫也格外地响——如此而已。
这是塞隆在奥克塔维乌斯的头一场爱筵。教堂地下室那间大教室渐渐坐满，他注意到观点最极端的男人们和穿最扎眼的灰褐衣裳的女人们蜂拥着抢占前排座位，只觉心一个劲往下沉。轮到他起立、以一段劝勉宣布开会时，他是逐字逐句硬逼着自己说下来的。他曾热切地想把这项差事让给教区长老——阿齐尔·P·拉雷比牧师，此刻正板着肃穆的沉默坐在他身后的小讲台上——却被告知，这位尊长稍后在作见证时自会领起。于是塞隆一路感到长老敌意的目光在他背上烧着一个个窟窿，好歹把这差事拖完了。他从前从不知道说话还有这样的难。等到惯常的那一段——劝告众人为主作见证务求简短，因为时间属于全体民众，教规也不许爱筵超过九十分钟——他如释重负，重得几乎把他压倒。他把这条简短令交付得格外郑重，随即猛然坐下。
接下来是一阵相当喧闹的唱诗；唱诗中间，管事们从讲台起头，把切成小方块的面包装在盘子里、把镀银大壶和杯盏盛着的水，在会众中传递，在平日这个钟点归主日学孩子们坐的长木凳之间穿行，挨个服侍每位弟兄姊妹。在奥克塔维乌斯这里，他们守着老规矩，沿座位男女相间而坐，如同讲究的宴席。
塞隆木然望着这熟悉的场面。起初的紧张已经过去了。

===Original===

He felt now that he was not in the least afraid of these people, even with the Presiding Elder thrown in. Folks who sang with such unintelligence, and who threw themselves with such undignified fervor into this childish business of the bread and water, could not be formidable antagonists for a man of intellect. He had never realized before what a spectacle the Methodist love-feast probably presented to outsiders. What must they think of it!
He had noticed that the Soulsbys sat together, in the centre and toward the front. Next to Brother Soulsby sat Alice. He thought she looked pale and preoccupied, and set it down in passing to her innate distaste for the somber garments she was wearing, and for the company she perforce found herself in. Another head was in the way, and for a time Theron did not observe who sat beside Alice on the other side. When at last he saw that it was Levi Gorringe, his instinct was to wonder what the lawyer must be saying to himself about these noisy and shallow enthusiasts. A recurring emotion of loyalty to the simple people among whom, after all, he had lived his whole life, prompted him to feel that it wasn’t wholly nice of Gorringe to come and enjoy this revelation of their foolish side, as if it were a circus. There was some vague memory in his mind which associated Gorringe with other love-feasts, and with a cynical attitude toward them. Oh, yes! he had told how he went to one just for the sake of sitting beside the girl he admired﻿—and was pursuing.
The stewards had completed their round, and the loud, discordant singing came to an end. There ensued a little pause, during which Theron turned to the Presiding Elder with a gesture of invitation to take charge of the further proceedings. The Elder responded with another gesture, calling his attention to something going on in front.
Brother and Sister Soulsby, to the considerable surprise of everybody, had risen to their feet, and were standing in their places, quite motionless, and with an air of professional self-assurance dimly discernible under a large show of humility. They stood thus until complete silence had been secured. Then the woman, lifting her head, began to sing. The words were “Rock of Ages,” but no one present had heard the tune to which she wedded them. Her voice was full and very sweet, and had in it tender cadences which all her hearers found touching.

===Chinese===

他此刻觉得，他一点也不怕这些人，连教区长老一并算上也一样。唱起诗来这样没有头脑、投身于递饼递水这样孩子气的勾当又这样毫无体面的人，对一个有智识的人，成不了可怕的对手。他从前不曾意识到，美以美会的爱筵在外人眼里多半是一幅什么景象。他们看了会怎么想呢！
他注意到索尔斯比夫妇坐在当中靠前的位子。索尔斯比弟兄旁边坐着艾丽斯。他觉得她面色苍白、心事重重，顺手把这归因于她天生厌恶身上这套晦暗的衣裳、厌恶她被迫厕身其间的这伙人。另一个脑袋挡着，有一阵塞隆没看清艾丽斯另一边坐的是谁。待他终于看清那是利维·戈林奇，他的直觉是纳闷：这律师心里不知会怎么议论这些喧闹而浅薄的狂信者。一阵对淳朴人民的忠心——毕竟他一辈子生活在他们中间——又怂恿他觉得，戈林奇跑来把他们的愚蠢一面当马戏看，不见得体面。他心里有一点模糊的记忆，把戈林奇同别的爱筵、同一种对爱筵的冷嘲态度联在一起。哦，想起来了！他说过，他去参加过一回，就为了坐在他爱慕——并且正在追求——的姑娘旁边。
管事们传完了圈，高亢而走调的唱诗结束了。略停了停，塞隆朝教区长老一让手势，请他接管接下来的程序。长老回了一个手势，叫他注意前面正发生的事。
索尔斯比弟兄和姊妹——大大出于众人的意外——站了起来，立在各自的位置上，一动不动，谦卑铺陈得满满的底下，隐约透出一股职业的自持。他们这样立着，直到争得彻底的安静。随后那妇人抬起头，唱了起来。词是《万古磐石》，可曲调在场没有一个人听过。她的嗓音饱满而极甜，里面有一种柔婉的抑扬，听得人无不心动。

===Original===

She knew how to sing, and she put forth the words so that each was distinctly intelligible. There came a part where Brother Soulsby, lifting his head in turn, took up a tuneful second to her air. Although the two did not, as one could hear by listening closely, sing the same words at the same time, they produced none the less most moving and delightful harmonies of sound.
The experience was so novel and charming that listeners ran ahead in their minds to fix the number of verses there were in the hymn, and to hope that none would be left out.
