他得了急痨。怕是捱不到下雪了。」
「哦，听了这个我*难过*极了！」塞隆说，声音发颤、无比诚恳。在他感觉里，迈克尔仿佛同他有了一层亲。
「他们全家都苦，」神父接着说。「迈克尔这个人，甜纯圣洁到人能想到的极限。他是他父亲的掌上珠。爷儿俩从没分开过。您认得那位父亲、马登先生么？」
塞隆摇头。「见过一面，我想，」他说。「小个子，灰络腮胡。」
「一个农夫，」福布斯神父说，「却有一颗金子做的心。可怜的人！他的钱财，他自己享着的少极了。唉，西海岸的人，我在这一边见过的他们中间的悲剧可太多了！他们的肺叶构造是初级的，像青蛙的，正配他们生息的那温和湿润柔软的空气。这边的冽气，把他们像霜天的苍蝇一样扑杀。整家整家地没。老杰里迈亚的孩子，墓园里躺着怕有一打。迈克尔若能捱过二十八岁，就还有指望，至少捱到三十五。肺病这种东西，说来以七年为期。」
「这我倒不知道，」塞隆说。「真古怪——也真惨。」他吃了一惊的心思，忽然全数扑到对塞莉娅年纪的猜测上。
「那位妹妹——马登小姐——看着极强健，」他试探着说。
「塞莉娅或许逃得过这一族的劫数，」神父说。客人注意到，他说这话时，那只好看的白手在桌上攥成了拳；那温和的、精心调理过的嗓音里，也起了一层沙砾似的硬。「*那个*，想起来就太可怕了，」他添一句。
塞隆默默打个寒噤，竭力把心向这念头关上。
「她把迈克尔的病搁在心里搁得太深，」神父接着说，「又那样不知疲倦地侍奉他，我都有点为她担心。我一直劝她出门换换空气、换换环境，哪怕几天也好。她睡不好；那向来不是好事。」
「我记得她仿佛对我说过一回，她有时会整夜睡不着，」塞隆说。「她说那时节她就不论什么钟点砸钢琴，或者像野人一样把软垫一间屋一间屋拖来拖去。一位非常有意思的年轻小姐——您不觉得么？」
福布斯神父唇边浮起一丝惨然的微笑。

===Original===

“What, our Celia?” he said. “Interesting! Why, Mr. Ware, there is no one like her in the world. She is as unique as﻿—what shall I say?﻿—as the Irish are among races. Her father and mother were both born in mud-cabins, and she﻿—she might be the daughter of a hundred kings, except that they seem mostly rather under-witted than otherwise. She always impresses me as a sort of atavistic idealization of the old Celt at his finest and best. There in Ireland you got a strange mixture of elementary early peoples, walled off from the outer world by the four seas, and free to work out their own racial amalgam on their own lines. They brought with them at the outset a great inheritance of Eastern mysticism. Others lost it, but the Irish, all alone on their island, kept it alive and brooded on it, and rooted their whole spiritual side in it. Their religion is full of it; their blood is full of it; our Celia is fuller of it than anybody else. The Ireland of two thousand years ago is incarnated in her. They are the merriest people and the saddest, the most turbulent and the most docile, the most talented and the most unproductive, the most practical and the most visionary, the most devout and the most pagan. These impossible contradictions war ceaselessly in their blood. When I look at Celia, I seem to see in my mind’s eye the fair young-ancestral mother of them all.”
Theron gazed at the speaker with open admiration. “I love to hear you talk,” he said simply.
An unbidden memory flitted upward in his mind. Those were the very words that Alice had so often on her lips in their old courtship days. How curious it was! He looked at the priest, and had a quaint sensation of feeling as a romantic woman must feel in the presence of a specially impressive masculine personality. It was indeed strange that this soft-voiced, portly creature in a gown, with his white, fat hands and his feline suavity of manner, should produce such a commanding and unique effect of virility. No doubt this was a part of the great sex mystery which historically surrounded the figure of the celibate priest as with an atmosphere. Women had always been prostrating themselves before it. Theron, watching his companion’s full, pallid face in the lamplight, tried to fancy himself in the priest’s place, looking down upon these worshipping female forms. He wondered what the celibate’s attitude really was.

===Chinese===

「怎么，我们的塞莉娅？」他说。「有意思！哎呀韦尔先生，世上再没有第二个她。她的独一无二，就像——怎么说好呢——就像爱尔兰人在诸民族中间那样独一无二。她的父亲母亲都生在泥屋里；她呢——她尽可以做一百个国王的女儿，只可惜国王们多半脑子不大灵。她给我的印象，永远像一种返祖的理想化：老凯尔特人最精粹、最美好的一面的返祖。爱尔兰那地方，你们得到的是几种原始初民的古怪混合，四面海水一锁，同外间隔绝，尽可以循自己的路线炮制自家的种族合剂。他们起初就带来一大宗东方神秘主义的遗产。别人丢掉了，爱尔兰人独守孤岛，把它养着、孵着，把整个灵性的一面扎在它上头。他们的宗教里满是它；他们的血里满是它；我们的塞莉娅比谁都满。两千年前的爱尔兰，在她身上成了肉身。他们是最快乐的民族，又是最悲哀的；最躁动，又最驯顺；最有才，又最无成；最实际，又最爱空想；最虔敬，又最异教。这些不可能的矛盾，在他们血里厮杀不休。我望着塞莉娅，心眼里仿佛望见他们全体那位年轻秀美的始祖母。」
塞隆望着说话的人，倾慕毫不掩饰。「我爱听您讲话，」他简单地说。
一个不请自来的记忆从他心里浮起。这几个字，正是当年求婚的日子里艾丽斯常挂在嘴边的。多巧啊！他望着神父，忽有一种古怪的体会：像一个浪漫的女人面对一个特别慑人的男性人格时必有的体会。说来也怪：这样一位穿袍子的、嗓音柔和、身量富态的造物，凭他白胖的手和猫一般的圆滑举止，竟产生出这样一种威严而独步的丈夫气。无疑，这是那桩大的性神秘的一部分——历史上，它像一层气氛，笼着独身神父的身影。女人们从来就拜倒在这身影前。塞隆望着灯下同伴那张丰润苍白的脸，试着把自己放到神父的位置上，俯视这些崇拜的女性身影。他想知道，独身者的态度究竟是怎样的。

===Original===

The enigma fascinated him.
Father Forbes, after his rhetorical outburst, had been eating. He pushed aside his cheese-plate. “I grow enthusiastic on the subject of my race sometimes,” he remarked, with the suggestion of an apology. “But I make up for it other times﻿—most of the time﻿—by scolding them. If it were not such a noble thing to be an Irishman, it would be ridiculous.”
“Ah,” said Theron, deprecatingly, “who would not be enthusiastic in talking of Miss Madden? What you said about her was perfect. As you spoke, I was thinking how proud and thankful we ought to be for the privilege of knowing her﻿—we who do know her well﻿—although of course your friendship with her is vastly more intimate than mine﻿—than mine could ever hope to be.”
The priest offered no comment, and Theron went on: “I hardly know how to describe the remarkable impression she makes upon me. I can’t imagine to myself any other young woman so brilliant or broad in her views, or so courageous. Of course, her being so rich makes it easier for her to do just what she wants to do, but her bravery is astonishing all the same. We had a long and very sympathetic talk in the woods, that day of the picnic, after we left you. I don’t know whether she spoke to you about it?”
Father Forbes made a movement of the head and eyes which seemed to negative the suggestion.
“Her talk,” continued Theron, “gave me quite new ideas of the range and capacity of the female mind. I wonder that everybody in Octavius isn’t full of praise and admiration for her talents and exceptional character. In such a small town as this, you would think she would be the centre of attention﻿—the pride of the place.”
“I think she has as much praise as is good for her,” remarked the priest, quietly.
“And here’s a thing that puzzles me,” pursued Mr. Ware. “I was immensely surprised to find that Dr. Ledsmar doesn’t even think she is smart﻿—or at least he professes the utmost intellectual contempt for her, and says he dislikes her into the bargain. But of course she dislikes him, too, so that’s only natural. But I can’t understand his denying her great ability.”
The priest smiled in a dubious way. “Don’t borrow unnecessary alarm about that, Mr. Ware,” he said, with studied smoothness of modulated tones.

这个谜迷住了他。

「唉，」塞隆附和着，「谈起马登小姐，谁能不热呢？您说她的那番话，说到十分了。您说的时候我在想：我们这些深知她的人，得着认识她的特权，该怎样骄傲、怎样感谢——当然，您同她的交谊，比我的亲密得多——比我所敢望的亲密得多。」
神父不予置评；塞隆接着说：「我几乎不知道怎样形容她给我的特殊印象。我想象不出还有哪个年轻女子这样才华焕发、见识这样宽、这样有胆量。当然，她这样有钱，想做什么就更容易做；可她的胆气依旧惊人。野餐那天，离开您之后，我们在林子里作了一场很长、很投机的谈话。不知她同您提起过没有？」
福布斯神父把头和眼睛动了一动，像是否定了这个揣测。
「她那一席话，」塞隆继续说，「使我对于女性心智的幅面和容量，得了一套全新的观念。我奇怪奥克塔维乌斯怎么没有一个人不为她的才华和出群的品格赞叹。这样一个小城，您会以为她该是众目所归——一地的骄傲。」
「我想，捧她的话，她得到的已够多了，」神父平静地说。
「还有一桩叫我不解的事，」韦尔先生说。「我大吃一惊地发现，莱兹马医生竟不认为她聪明——至少他口口声声对她报以极度的智力上的蔑视，还说厌恶她。当然她也厌恶他，那倒自然。可他否认她的大才，我不懂。」
神父露出将信将疑的微笑。「这上头您不必多虑，韦尔先生，」他说，调门里带着刻意抚平的圆滑。

===Original===

“These two good friends of mine have much enjoyment out of the idea that they are fighting for the mastery over my poor unstable character. It has grown to be a habit with them, and a hobby as well, and they pursue it with tireless zest. There are not many intellectual diversions open to us here, and they make the most of this one. It amuses them, and it is not without its charms for me, in my capacity as an interested observer. It is a part of the game that they should pretend to themselves that they detest each other. In reality I fancy that they like each other very much. At any rate, there is nothing to be disturbed about.”
His mellifluous tones had somehow the effect of suggesting to Theron that he was an outsider and would better mind his own business. Ah, if this purring pussycat of a priest only knew how little of an outsider he really was! The thought gave him an easy self-control.
“Of course,” he said, “our warm mutual friendship makes the observation of these little individual vagaries merely a part of a delightful whole. I should not dream of discussing Miss Madden’s confidences to me, or the doctor’s either, outside our own little group.”
Father Forbes reached behind him and took from a chair his black three-cornered cap with the tassel. “Unfortunately I have a sick call waiting me,” he said, gathering up his gown and slowly rising.
“Yes, I saw the man sitting in the hall,” remarked Theron, getting to his feet.
“I would ask you to go upstairs and wait,” the priest went on, “but my return, unhappily, is quite uncertain. Another evening I may be more fortunate. I am leaving town tomorrow for some days, but when I get back﻿—”
The polite sentence did not complete itself. Father Forbes had come out into the hall, giving a cool nod to the workingman, who rose from the bench as they passed, and shook hands with his guest on the doorstep.
When the door had closed upon Mr. Ware, the priest turned to the man. “You have come about those frames,” he said. “If you will come upstairs, I will show you the prints, and you can give me a notion of what can be done with them. I rather fancy the idea of a triptych in carved old English, if you can manage it.”
After the workman had gone away, Father Forbes put on slippers and an old loose soutane, lighted a cigar, and, pushing an easy-chair over to the reading lamp, sat down with a book.

===Chinese===

「我这两位好朋友，从争着驾驭我这可怜的、不坚定的性格里得到不少乐趣。这于他们已成了习惯，也成了癖好，孜孜不倦。我们这儿能开展的智性消遣不多，他们把这一样用足了。这逗他们乐，于我这个感兴趣的旁观者，也不是没有趣味。按游戏的规矩，他们各自都要当真以为自己恨对方。其实呢，我看他们彼此喜欢得很。总之，没有什么可不安的。」
他那套甜津津的调子，不知怎么给了塞隆一个感觉：他是外人，顶好少管闲事。哼，这只打呼噜的猫儿一般的神父，若是知道他其实算得多少外人！这念头给了他从容的自制。
「当然，」他说，「我们彼此热诚的友谊，使观察这些小小的个人怪癖，不过是一幅可喜的全景的一部分。马登小姐对我说的体己话，或医生对我说的，我做梦也不会拿到我们这个小圈子外头去讲。」
福布斯神父伸手从身后椅子上取过他那顶带穗的三楞黑帽。「不巧有一个探病的召唤等着我，」他说，拢起袍子缓缓起身。
「是，我看见那人坐在门厅里，」塞隆说着也站起来。
「本想请您上楼坐等，」神父接着说，「可我几时回来，不幸全无一定。别的晚上，我或许运气好些。明天我出城几天；等回来——」
这句客气话没有说完。福布斯神父已出到门厅，朝那工人冷淡地点了点头——两人经过时他从长凳上站起——又在门阶上同客人握了手。
韦尔先生的身影在门后消失，神父转向那人。「你是为那几副框子来的，」他说。「上楼来，我把画片给你看；你给我说说可以拿它们怎么办。我倒有点属意一座雕花的古英文体三联框，你若办得来。」
工人走后，福布斯神父换上拖鞋和一件宽大的旧长袍，点一支雪茄，把安乐椅拖到读书灯前，坐下来读书。

### XXVII / 二十七
===Original===

That night brought the first frost of the season worth counting. In the morning, when Theron came downstairs, his casual glance through the window caught a desolate picture of blackened dahlia stalks and shrivelled blooms. The gayety and color of the garden were gone, and in their place was shabby and dishevelled ruin. He flung the sash up and leaned out. The nipping autumn air was good to breathe. He looked about him, surveying the havoc the frost had wrought among the flowers, and smiled.
At breakfast he smiled again﻿—a mirthless and calculated smile. “I see that Brother Gorringe’s flowers have come to grief over night,” he remarked.
Alice looked at him before she spoke, and saw on his face a confirmation of the hostile hint in his voice. She nodded in a constrained way, and said nothing.
“Or rather, I should say,” Theron went on, with deliberate words, “the late Brother Gorringe’s flowers.”
“How do you mean﻿—*late*?” asked his wife, swiftly.
“Oh, calm yourself!” replied the husband. “He is not dead. He has only intimated to me his desire to sever his connection. I may add that he did so in a highly offensive manner.”
“I am very sorry,” said Alice, in a low tone, and with her eyes on her plate.
“I took it for granted you would be grieved at his backsliding,” remarked Theron, making his phrases as pointed as he could. “He was such a promising probationer, and you took such a keen interest in his spiritual awakening. But the frost has nipped his zeal﻿—along with the hundred or more dollars’ worth of flowers by which he testified his faith. I find something interesting in their having been blasted simultaneously.”
Alice dropped all pretence of interest in her breakfast. With a flushed face and lips tightly compressed, she made a movement as if to rise from her chair. Then, changing her mind, she sat bolt upright and faced her husband.
“I think we had better have this out right now,” she said, in a voice which Theron hardly recognized. “You have been hinting round the subject long enough﻿—too long. There are *some* things nobody is obliged to put up with, and this is one of them. You will oblige me by saying out in so many words what it is you are driving at.”
The outburst astounded Theron. He laid down his knife and fork, and gazed at his wife in frank surprise.

===Chinese===

这一夜，落了本季第一场合得上数的大霜。早晨塞隆下楼，朝窗外随手一瞥，撞上一幅凄凉的画面：大丽花的秸秆焦黑，花朵菴萎。园中的明艳和颜色没有了，顶替它们的是寒碜而披靡的一片颓败。他推开窗扇，探身出去。碰骨的秋气，吸着正好。他四下张望，检阅霜在花丛间造下的这场浩劫，微笑了。
早餐时他又微笑了——一个不含笑意、经过计算的笑。“我看，戈林奇弟兄的花，一夜之间遭了殃。”他说。
艾丽斯先望了望他，看见了他脸上对那句含敌意的话的印证。她勉强点了点头，什么也没说。
“或者说——我该说——”塞隆一字一顿地接着说，“已故的戈林奇弟兄的花。”
“你什么意思——*已故*？”妻子急急地问。
“哦，你宽心！”丈夫答道，“他没有死。他只是向我示意，愿意断绝他的关系。我可以补一句：他示意的方式恶劣得很。”
“我很遗憾。”艾丽斯低声说，眼睛望着盘子。
“我自当你会为他的后退痛心，”塞隆说，把话说得尽量带刺，“他是这样一位有前途的见习教友，你对他的灵性苏醒又寄予这样深切的关怀。可惜霜把他的热心同那百十来块钱的、见证过他信心的花一并冻杀了。两样东西同时毙命，我觉出一点兴味。”
艾丽斯把对早餐的兴趣装也装不下去了。她红着脸，双唇紧拚，做了一个像要从椅子里站起的动作。随即改了主意，笔直地坐定，面对她的丈夫。
“我看，咱们最好现在就把这件事说透。”她说，用的嗓音塞隆几乎认不得。“你围着这个题目旁敲侧击得够久了——太久了。有些事是谁也不必忍的，这就是一件。请你干脆把你的意思用明明白白的话说出来，就算是给我的面子。”
这爆发惊得塞隆目瞪口呆。他放下刀叉，坦率而惊讶地凝视妻子。

===Original===

She had so accustomed him, of late, to a demeanor almost abject in its depressed docility that he had quite forgotten the Alice of the old days, when she had spirit and courage enough for two, and a notable tongue of her own. The flash in her eyes and the lines of resolution about her mouth and chin for a moment daunted him. Then he observed by a flutter of the frill at her wrist that she was trembling.
“I am sure I have nothing to ‘say out in so many words,’ as you put it,” he replied, forcing his voice into cool, impassive tones. “I merely commented upon a coincidence, that was all. If, for any reason under the sun, the subject chances to be unpleasant to you, I have no earthly desire to pursue it.”
“But I insist upon having it pursued!” returned Alice. “I’ve had just all I can stand of your insinuations and innuendoes, and it’s high time we had some plain talk. Ever since the revival, you have been dropping sly, underhand hints about Mr. Gorringe and﻿—and me. Now I ask you what you mean by it.”
Yes, there was a shake in her voice, and he could see how her bosom heaved in a tremor of nervousness. It was easy for him to be very calm.
“It is you who introduce these astonishing suggestions, not I,” he replied coldly. “It is you who couple your name with his﻿—somewhat to my surprise, I admit﻿—but let me suggest that we drop the subject. You are excited just now, and you might say things that you would prefer to leave unsaid. It would surely be better for all concerned to say no more about it.”
Alice, staring across the table at him with knitted brows, emitted a sharp little snort of indignation. “Well, I never! Theron, I wouldn’t have thought it of you!”
“There are so many things you wouldn’t have thought, on such a variety of subjects,” he observed, with a show of resuming his breakfast. “But why continue? We are only angering each other.”
“Never mind that,” she replied, with more control over her speech. “I guess things have come to a pass where a little anger won’t do any harm. I have a right to insist on knowing what you mean by your insinuations.”
Theron sighed. “Why will you keep harping on the thing?” he asked wearily. “I have displayed no curiosity. I don’t ask for any explanations. I think I mentioned that the man had behaved insultingly to me﻿—but that doesn’t matter. I don’t bring it up as a grievance. I am very well able to take care of myself.

===Chinese===

近来她叫他习惯了那几乎卑屈的、消沉的恭顺，他早把旧日的艾丽斯忘了——那个胆气够两个人使、自己有一条出名利舌的艾丽斯。她眼里的一闪，她嘴角和下颈那几道决断的纹，一时竟镇住了他。随后他凭她腕边花边的一颤看出，她在发抖。
“我可以肯定，我没有什么要‘用明明白白的话说出’的东西，照你的说法。”他答，强把嗓音压进冷淡、平板的调门，“我不过评论了一番巧合，如此而已。倘若由于天底下的什么缘故，这个题目碰巧叫你不快，我毫无追究的意思。”
“可我偏要追究到底！”艾丽斯回敬，“你那些影射和暗讽，我受够了；咱们正该来一场明白的谈话。打从奋兴会起，你就一直朝戈林奇先生和——和我滴溜溜地放冷箭。现在我请问，你是什么意思。”
是，她的嗓音在抖，他也看得出她的胸口怎样因紧张的震颤而起伏。他要非常冷静，是容易的。
“是你在引进这些惊人的暗示，不是我。”他冷冷地答，“是你把你的名字同他的联在一起——我承认，颇出我的意外——不过我建议，咱们还是丢开这个题目。你这会儿激动，说不定说出些你本不愿说的话。事关各方，就此打住，岂不更好。”
艾丽斯蹙着眉隔桌瞪着他，鼻子里发出一声愤懑的短哼。“嗐，真是！塞隆，我竟想不到你会这样！”
“你想不到的事多着呢，题目也五花八门。”他说，摆出继续用早饭的样子。“何必再谈？咱们不过是彼此惹恼。”
“那个不用管，”她答，对言语多了几分把持，“我看事到如今，一点恼怒伤不着谁。我有权利弄清，你那些影射到底是什么意思。”
塞隆叹了口气。“你为什么偏要揪住这件事不放？”他厌倦地问，“我没有表现好奇。我不要求任何解释。我记得我说过那人对我无礼——可那不要紧。我没有把它当怨情来提。我照顾得了自己。

===Original===

I have no wish to recur to the incident in any way. So far as I am concerned, the topic is dismissed.”
“Listen to me!” broke in Alice, with eager gravity. She hesitated, as he looked up with a nod of attention, and reflected as well as she was able among her thoughts for a minute or two. “This is what I want to say to you. Ever since we came to this hateful Octavius, you and I have been drifting apart﻿—or no, that doesn’t express it﻿—simply rushing away from each other. It only began last spring, and now the space between us is so wide that we are worse than complete strangers. For strangers at least don’t hate each other, and I’ve had a good many occasions lately to see that you positively do hate me﻿—”
“What grotesque absurdity,” interposed Theron, impatiently.
“No, it isn’t absurdity; it’s gospel truth,” retorted Alice. “And﻿—don’t interrupt me﻿—there have been times, too, when I have had to ask myself if I wasn’t getting almost to hate you in return. I tell you this frankly.”
“Yes, you are undoubtedly frank,” commented the husband, toying with his teaspoon. “A hypercritical person might consider, almost too frank.”
Alice scanned his face closely while he spoke, and held her breath as if in expectant suspense. Her countenance clouded once more. “You don’t realize, Theron,” she said gravely; “your voice when you speak to me, your look, your manner, they have all changed. You are like another man﻿—some man who never loved me, and doesn’t even know me, much less like me. I want to know what the end of it is to be. Up to the time of your sickness last summer, until after the Soulsbys went away, I didn’t let myself get downright discouraged. It seemed too monstrous for belief that you should go away out of my life like that. It didn’t seem possible that God could allow such a thing. It came to me that I had been lax in my Christian life, especially in my position as a minister’s wife, and that this was my punishment. I went to the altar, to intercede with Him, and to try to loose my burden at His feet. But nothing has come of it. I got no help from you.”
“Really, Alice,” broke in Theron, “I explained over and over again to you how preoccupied I was﻿—with the book﻿—and affairs generally.”
“I got no assistance from Heaven either,” she went on, declining the diversion he offered.

===Chinese===

“我不愿以任何方式重提这件事。就我而论，题目作罢。”
“听我说！”艾丽斯打断，急切而郑重。他抬头注意地点了点头；她迟疑了一下，在纷乱的思绪里尽力想了想，这才开口。“我要对你说的是这个。打从咱们来到这可恨的奥克塔维乌斯，你我就一直漂离——不，这话不够——简直是彼此狂奔而去。开头不过是去年春天，如今咱们之间的鸿沟已宽到连陌生的路人都不如。陌生人至少不彼此恨；而近来我有的是机会看出，你是实实在在地恨我——”
“多么荒唐的怪话，”塞隆不耐烦地插嘴。
“不，不是怪话；是千真万确的实话，”艾丽斯回敬，“还有——别打岔——也有那样的时刻，我不得不问自己，我是不是也快恨起你来了。我坦白告诉你。”
“是，你无疑很坦白，”丈夫评论道，把玩着茶匙，“一个苛求的人或许认为，坦白得过了头。”
艾丽斯趁他说话细细察看他的脸，屏着气，像在悬望。她的脸色又阴下来。“你没有意识到，塞隆，”她郑重地说，“你同我说话的嗓音、你的神色、你的举止，全变了。你像另外一个人——一个从没爱过我的人，连认都不认得我，更谈不上喜欢。我要知道，这一切的结局是什么。直到去年夏天你生病、直到索尔斯比夫妇走后，我还不许自己彻底灰心。你会那样从我生命里走掉，这念头荒唐得叫人无法相信。上帝竟容这样的事，似乎不可能。我想到，是我的基督徒生活懈怠了，尤其是我身居牧师妻子的位置；这是对我的惩罚。我走上圣坛，向他代求，想把我的重担卸在他脚前。可是什么也没换来。从你那里我没有得到帮助。”
“真的，艾丽斯，”塞隆插进来，“我一而再再而三向你解释过，我的心事怎样被占着——为那本书——还有种种事务。”
“从上天那里我也没有得到帮助，”她接着说，不接受他递来的岔头。

“I don’t want to talk impiously, but if there is a God, he has forgotten me, his poor heartbroken handmaiden.”
“You *are* talking impiously, Alice,” observed her husband. “And you are doing me cruel injustice, into the bargain.”
“I only wish I were!” she replied; “I only wish to God I were!”
“Well, then, accept my complete assurance that you *are*﻿—that your whole conception of me, and of what you are pleased to describe as my change toward you, is an entire and utter mistake. Of course, the married state is no more exempt from the universal law of growth, development, alteration, than any other human institution. On its spiritual side, of course, viewed either as a sacrament, or as﻿—”
“Don’t let us go into that,” interposed Alice, abruptly. “In fact, there is no good in talking any more at all. It is as if we didn’t speak the same language. You don’t understand what I say; it makes no impression upon your mind.”
“Quite to the contrary,” he assured her; “I have been deeply interested and concerned in all you have said. I think you are laboring under a great delusion, and I have tried my best to convince you of it; but I have never heard you speak more intelligibly or, I might say, effectively.”
A little gleam of softness stole over Alice’s face. “If you only gave me a little more credit for intelligence,” she said, “you would find that I am not such a blockhead as you think I am.”
“Come, come!” he said, with a smiling show of impatience. “You really mustn’t impute things to me wholesale, like that.”
She was glad to answer the smile in kind. “No; but truly,” she pleaded, “you don’t realize it, but you have grown into a way of treating me as if I had absolutely no mind at all.”
“You have a very admirable mind,” he responded, and took up his teaspoon again. She reached for his cup, and poured out hot coffee for him. An almost cheerful spirit had suddenly descended upon the breakfast table.
“And now let me say the thing I have been aching to say for months,” she began in less burdened voice.
He lifted his brows. “Haven’t things been discussed pretty fully already?” he asked.
The doubtful, harassed expression clouded upon her face at his words, and she paused. “No,” she said resolutely, after an instant’s reflection; “it is my duty to discuss this, too. It is a misunderstanding all round. You remember that I told you Mr.

===Chinese===

“我不想说不敬的话；可倘若真有上帝，他也忘了我这个心碎的使女了。”
“你*这是*说不敬的话，艾丽斯，”她丈夫评论道，“而且顺便说，你还残忍地冤枉了我。”
“我倒巴不得是冤枉！”她答，“我巴不得向上帝是冤枉！”
“那么，请接受我十足的保证：你*就是*冤枉——你对我和对你所谓的我对你的转变的全部概念，是一个彻头彻尾的错误。当然，婚姻这制度同任何别的人类制度一样，逃脱不了生长、发育、变异的普遍法则。就其灵性一面而论，无论看作一件圣事，还是看作——”
“咱们别扯那个，”艾丽斯蒦地拦住，“其实，再谈也没有好处。咱们就像说的不是同一种语言。你不懂我的话；它在你心里留不下印象。”
“恰恰相反，”他向她保证，“你说的一切我都深切地关切。我认为你正困在一场大错觉之下，我已尽力使你信服这一点；可我从没有听你说话说得这样明白——或者我可以说——这样有力。”
一缕柔和的光偷偷爬上艾丽斯的脸。“你只要多承认我一点头脑，”她说，“就会发现我并不是你想的那个木头人。”
“好了好了！”他带笑摆出不耐烦的样子，“你可真不许这样成批成批地往我头上栽东西。”
她乐于以同样的笑回应这笑。“不；可是说真的，”她恳求道，“你自己不知道，你已经养成了把我当作全然没有头脑的习惯。”
“你有一副极可敬的头脑，”他应道，重新拿起茶匙。她伸手取过他的杯子，给他斟上热咖啡。一种近乎轻快的气氛，蒦地降临在早餐桌上。
“现在让我说那句我憋了几个月、急于要说的话吧，”她开口，嗓音松快了些。
他扬起眉。“不是都谈得相当充分了么？”他问。
他这话一出，那迟疑、苦恼的神色又罩上她的脸；她顿了顿。“不，”略一沉吟，她决然说，“这一件也是我的本分，要谈。这是一场全面的误会。你记得我告诉过你，戈林奇先生给过我一些花木，是从什么园子里弄来的？

===Original===

Gorringe had given me some plants, which he got from some garden or other?”
“If you really wish to go on with the subject﻿—yes I have a recollection of that particular falsehood of his.”
“He did it with the kindest and friendliest motives in the world!” protested Alice. “He saw how down-in-the-mouth and moping I was here, among these strangers﻿—and I really was getting quite peaked and rundown﻿—and he said I stayed indoors too much and it would do me all sorts of good to work in the garden, and he would send me some plants. The next I knew, here they were, with a book about mixing soils and planting, and so on. When I saw him next, and thanked him, I suppose I showed some apprehension about his having laid out money on them, and he, just to ease my mind, invented the story about his getting them for nothing. When I found out the truth﻿—I got it out of that boy, Harvey Semple﻿—he admitted it quite frankly﻿—said he was wrong to deceive me.”
“This was in the fine first fervor of his term of probation, I suppose,” put in Theron. He made no effort to dissemble the sneer in his voice.
“Well,” answered Alice, with a touch of acerbity, “I have told you now, and it is off my mind. There never would have been the slightest concealment about it, if you hadn’t begun by keeping me at arm’s length, and making it next door to impossible to speak to you at all, and if﻿—”
“And if he hadn’t lied.” Theron, as he finished her sentence for her, rose from the table. Dallying for a brief moment by his chair, there seemed the magnetic premonition in the air of some further and kindlier word. Then he turned and walked sedately into the next room, and closed the door behind him. The talk was finished; and Alice, left alone, passed the knuckle of her thumb over one swimming eye and then the other, and bit her lips and swallowed down the sob that rose in her throat.

===Chinese===

“你当真愿意继续这个题目——是，我记得他那句特定的谎言。”
“他那样做，怀着天底下最善良、最友好的动机！”艾丽斯抗辩，“他看我在这生人堆里垂头丧气、闷闷不乐——我也确实一天天憔悴下去——他说我闷在屋里太多，做做园子活于我有百利，他要送我一些花木。随后没多大工夫，花就到了，还附着一本讲配土和栽植的书。后来我见到他道谢，想必是露出了怕他破费的神色，他就为宽我的心，编了一套他是白捡来的话。等我查明真相——是从那孩子哈维·森普尔嘴里採出来的——他倒爽爽快快认了——说骗我是他的不对。”
“想必是在他见习期之初那番美好的初热里，”塞隆插话。他不加掩饰，话里的讥诮全挂在嗓音上。
“好了，”艾丽斯带着一点尖刻答道，“我如今告诉了你，心里也卸下了。这件事本来不会有丝毫隐瞒，倘若不是你先把我拒于一臂之外，弄得同你说一句话都几乎不可能；倘若不是——”
“倘若不是他撒了谎。”塞隆替她把句子续完，从桌边站起。他在椅边流连了短短一刻，空气里仿佛有某种磁性的预感，还有什么更温存的话要来。随后他转过身，安步踱进隔壁房间，随手带上了门。谈话完结了；艾丽斯独自留下，用大拇指的指节抹过一只含泪的眼，又抹过另一只，咬住嘴唇，把涌上喉头的那声呜咽咽了下去。

### XXVIII / 二十八
===Original===

It was early afternoon when Theron walked out of his yard, bestowing no glance upon the withered and tarnished show of the garden, and started with a definite step down the street. The tendency to ruminative loitering, which those who saw him abroad always associated with his tall, spare figure, was not suggested today. He moved forward like a man with a purpose.
All the forenoon in the seclusion of the sitting-room, with a book opened before him, he had been thinking hard. It was not the talk with Alice that occupied his thoughts. That rose in his mind from time to time, only as a disagreeable blur, and he refused to dwell upon it. It was nothing to him, he said to himself, what Gorringe’s motives in lying had been. As for Alice, he hardened his heart against her. Just now it was her mood to try and make up to him. But it had been something different yesterday, and who could say what it would be tomorrow? He really had passed the limit of patience with her shifting emotional vagaries, now lurching in this direction, now in that. She had had her chance to maintain a hold upon his interest and imagination, and had let it slip. These were the accidents of life, the inevitable harsh happenings in the great tragedy of Nature. They could not be helped, and there was nothing more to be said.
He had bestowed much more attention upon what the priest had said the previous evening. He passed in review all the glowing tributes Father Forbes had paid to Celia. They warmed his senses as he recalled them, but they also, in a curious, indefinite way, caused him uneasiness. There had been a personal fervor about them which was something more than priestly. He remembered how the priest had turned pale and faltered when the question whether Celia would escape the general doom of her family came up. It was not a merely pastoral agitation that, he felt sure.
A hundred obscure hints, doubts, stray little suspicions, crowded upward together in his thoughts. It became apparent to him now that from the outset he had been conscious of something queer﻿—yes, from that very first day when he saw the priest and Celia together, and noted their glance of recognition inside the house of death.

===Chinese===

午后不久，塞隆走出自家院子，对园子里那凋残失色的陈设一瞥也不给，迈着确定的步子上了街。惯于见他出门的人，总把沉思的闲步同他那瘦长的身影连在一起；今天却不见这影子。他像个怀有目的的人那样朝前走。
整个上午，他闷坐在起居室里，面前摊开一本书，脑子却想得很苦。占住他思想的，不是同艾丽斯那场谈话。那件事只像一块不快的污渍，时时在他心里浮起，他不肯多想。戈林奇撒谎的动机是什么，同他不相干，他对自己说。至于艾丽斯，他对她硬起了心。她眼下的性子是设法同他修好。昨天可另是一副，明天谁知道又是什么一副？她这情绪的阴晴忽东忽西，他实在忍到了头。要抓住他的兴味和想象，她的机会有过，她自己放掉了。这些是人生的意外，大自然大悲剧里免不了的酷事。无可如何，也没有什么可说的了。
神父前一晚说的话，他倒是掂量得多。他把福布斯神父加给塞莉娅的那一篇篇灼热的颂赞，从头过了一遍。回想起来，它们烤热他的感官；可同时也以一种古怪的、说不清的方式叫他不安。那里面有一种个人的热度，超出了神父的分寸。他记得，塞莉娅能否逃过她家族的劫数这问题一提起，神父怎样白了脸、结了巴。那决不只是牧者的激动，他拿得稳。
上百个晦暗的暗示、疑心、零零星星的小猜，一齐朝他思绪的头上挤。此刻他明白了：打一开始他就觉出一点古怪——是的，就从最头一天，他看见神父同塞莉娅在一起、看见他们在那死亡之屋里交换相识的一瞥起。

===Original===

He realized now, upon reflection, that the tone of other people, his own parishioners and his casual acquaintances in Octavius alike, had always had a certain note of reservation in it when it touched upon Miss Madden. Her running in and out of the pastorate at all hours, the way the priest patted her on the shoulder before others, the obvious dislike the priest’s ugly old housekeeper bore her, the astonishing freedom of their talk with each other﻿—these dark memories loomed forth out of a mass of sinister conjecture.
He could bear the uncertainty no longer. Was it indeed not entirely his own fault that it had existed thus long? No man with the spirit of a mouse would have shilly-shallied in this preposterous fashion, week after week, with the fever of a beautiful woman’s kiss in his blood, and the woman herself living only round the corner. The whole world had been as good as offered to him﻿—a bewildering world of wealth and beauty and spiritual exaltation and love﻿—and he, like a weak fool, had waited for it to be brought to him on a salver, as it were, and actually forced upon his acceptance! “That is my failing,” he reflected; “these miserable ecclesiastical bandages of mine have dwarfed my manly side. The meanest of Thurston’s clerks would have shown a more adventurous spirit and a bolder nerve. If I do not act at once, with courage and resolution, everything will be lost. Already she must think me unworthy of the honor it was in her sweet will to bestow.” Then he remembered that she was now always at home. “Not another hour of foolish indecision!” he whispered to himself. “I will put my destiny to the test. I will see her today!”
A middle-aged, plain-faced servant answered his ring at the doorbell of the Madden mansion. She was palpably Irish, and looked at him with a saddened preoccupation in her gray eyes, holding the door only a little ajar.
Theron had got out one of his cards. “I wish to make inquiry about young Mr. Madden﻿—Mr. Michael Madden,” he said, holding the card forth tentatively. “I have only just heard of his illness, and it has been a great grief to me.”
“He is no better,” answered the woman, briefly.
“I am the Rev. Mr. Ware,” he went on, “and you may say that, if he is well enough, I should be glad to see him.”
The servant peered out at him with a suddenly altered expression, then shook her head.

===Chinese===

如今回想，他明白了：旁人的口气——他自己的教友同他在奥克塔维乌斯的泛泛之交都一样——一碰到马登小姐，总带着一分保留。她无时无刻出入牧馆；神父当着人拍她的肩；神父那丑老管家对她显然的厌恶；他们彼此交谈那份惊人的放肆——这些阴暗的记忆，从一大团险恶的猜测里耸了出来。
这悬心他再也担不住了。它拖到今天，难道不全是他自己的错？稍有一点血性的人，血里烧着一个美丽女人的一吻，那女人又只住在转角上，谁会像他这样，一个星期又一个星期地磨蹭？整个世界等于是捧到他面前了——一个叫人晕眩的、财富、美、精神的飞扬和爱的世界——而他像个软弱的傻瓜，坐等世界用托盘端到他面前，硬塞进他怀里！「这是我的毛病，」他反省；「我这层层可怜的教会的裹布，把我男人的那一面裹小了。瑟斯顿铺子里最低微的伙计，也比我更有闯劲、更有胆。再不立刻行动、拿出勇气和决断，一切就完了。此刻她必已认为，我不配领受她美意赐予的那份光荣。」随后他想起，她如今总在家。「再不许有一个钟头的愚蠢的迟疑！」他对自己低语。「我要把我的命运押上考场。今天我就去见她！」
马登巨宅的门铃响过，来开门的是个中年的、相貌平常的女仆。她一望即知是爱尔兰人，一双灰眼睛带着悲哀的心不在焉望他，门只开了一线。
塞隆掏出一张名片。「我想探问一下小马登先生——迈克尔·马登先生的病，」他把名片试探着递过去。「我刚听说他病了，心里非常难过。」
「不见好，」女人简单地答。
「我是韦尔牧师，」他接着说，「请转告：他若撑得住，我很愿意见他。」
女仆眯眼看他，神色陡然一变，随即摇头。
「我想他不会愿意见*您*，」她答。口吻听得出，她疑心来客的用意。
塞隆不由得笑了。「我不是以牧师的身份来的，」他解释，「是以家属朋友的身份。您去通报马登小姐说我在，也一样。是，咱们不去搅扰他。劳驾把名片递给他妹妹。」

===Original===

“I don’t think he would be wishing to see *you*,” she replied. It was evident from her tone that she suspected the visitor’s intentions.
Theron smiled in spite of himself. “I have not come as a clergyman,” he explained, “but as a friend of the family. If you will tell Miss Madden that I am here, it will do just as well. Yes, we won’t bother him. If you will kindly hand my card to his sister.”
When the domestic turned at this and went in, Theron felt like throwing his hat in the air, there where he stood. The woman’s churlish sectarian prejudices had played ideally into his hands. In no other imaginable way could he have asked for Celia so naturally. He wondered a little that a servant at such a grand house as this should leave callers standing on the doorstep. Still more he wondered what he should say to the lady of his dream when he came into her presence.
“Will you please to walk this way?” The woman had returned. She closed the door noiselessly behind him, and led the way, not up the sumptuous staircase, as Theron had expected, but along through the broad hall, past several large doors, to a small curtained archway at the end. She pushed aside this curtain, and Theron found himself in a sort of conservatory, full of the hot, vague light of sunshine falling through ground-glass. The air was moist and close, and heavy with the smell of verdure and wet earth. A tall bank of palms, with ferns sprawling at their base, reared itself directly in front of him. The floor was of mosaic, and he saw now that there were rugs upon it, and that there were chairs and sofas, and other signs of habitation. It was, indeed, only half a greenhouse, for the lower part of it was in rosewood panels, with floral paintings on them, like a room.
Moving to one side of the barrier of palms, he discovered, to his great surprise, the figure of Michael, sitting propped up with pillows in a huge easy-chair. The sick man was looking at him with big, gravely intent eyes. His face did not show as much change as Theron had in fancy pictured. It had seemed almost as bony and cadaverous on the day of the picnic. The hands spread out on the chair-arms were very white and thin, though, and the gaze in the blue eyes had a spectral quality which disturbed him.
Michael raised his right hand, and Theron, stepping forward, took it limply in his for an instant. Then he laid it down again.

===Chinese===

女仆闻言转身进去了；塞隆站在原地，直想把帽子抛上天去。这女人粗鲁的教派偏见，理想地成全了他。再没有别的想得出的路数，能叫他这样自然地求见塞莉娅。他略感奇怪：这样华贵的一宅，仆人怎么让来客立在门阶上等。他更奇怪的是：见了梦中情人，他该说什么。
「请这边走。」女仆回来了。她在他身后轻轻掩上门，引路——出乎塞隆所料，不是上那堂皇的楼梯，而是沿着宽阔的门厅，经过几扇大门，到尽头一道垂帘的小拱门。她掀开帘子，塞隆发现自己进了一间花房似的小天地，满是被磨砂玻璃滤过的、灼热而朦胧的日光。空气潮湿窒闷，饱含着青枝和湿土的气味。一排高高的棕榈，脚下披散着羊齿，正立在他面前。地面是马赛克，此刻他才看见上面铺着毯，摆着椅子、长榻和别的住家的迹象。这实在只是半间花房：下半截是花梨木的板壁，板上绘着花卉，同一间屋子一样。
他绕到棕榈屏障的一侧，大为意外地发现了迈克尔的身影：倚着枕头坐在一把巨大的安乐椅里。病人正用一双大大的、郑重专注的眼睛望着他。那张脸并没有塞隆幻想中描画的那样大的变化。野餐那天，它看起来就差不多这样多骨、这样死灰。只是摊在椅臂上的两手白得、瘦得扎眼；蓝眼睛里的凝望带着一种幽灵似的性质，扰得他不安。
迈克尔抬起右手；塞隆趋前，软软地握了一瞬，又替他放下。

===Original===

The touch of people about to die had always been repugnant to him. He could feel on his own warm palm the very damp of the grave.
“I only heard from Father Forbes last evening of your﻿—your ill-health,” he said, somewhat hesitatingly. He seated himself on a bench beneath the palms, facing the invalid, but still holding his hat. “I hope very sincerely that you will soon be all right again.”
“My sister is lying down in her room,” answered Michael. He had not once taken his sombre and embarrassing gaze from the other’s face. The voice in which he uttered this uncalled-for remark was thin in fibre, cold and impassive. It fell upon Theron’s ears with a suggestion of hidden meaning. He looked uneasily into Michael’s eyes, and then away again. They seemed to be looking straight through him, and there was no shirking the sensation that they saw and comprehended things with an unnatural prescience.
“I hope she is feeling better,” Theron found himself saying. “Father Forbes mentioned that she was a little under the weather. I dined with him last night.”
“I am glad that you came,” said Michael, after a little pause. His earnest, unblinking eyes seemed to supplement his tongue with speech of their own. “I do be thinking a great deal about you. I have matters to speak of to you, now that you are here.”
Theron bowed his head gently, in token of grateful attention. He tried the experiment of looking away from Michael, but his glance went back again irresistibly, and fastened itself upon the sick man’s gaze, and clung there.
“I am next door to a dead man,” he went on, paying no heed to the other’s deprecatory gesture. “It is not years or months with me, but weeks. Then I go away to stand up for judgment on my sins, and if it is His merciful will, I shall see God. So I say my goodbyes now, and so you will let me speak plainly, and not think ill of what I say. You are much changed, Mr. Ware, since you came to Octavius, and it is not a change for the good.”
Theron lifted his brows in unaffected surprise, and put inquiry into his glance.
“I don’t know if Protestants will be saved, in God’s good time, or not,” continued Michael. “I find there are different opinions among the clergy about that, and of course it is not for me, only a plain mechanic, to be sure where learned and pious scholars are in doubt. But I am sure about one thing.

===Chinese===

临死的人的触碰，一向叫他厌恶。他自己温热的掌心上，仿佛沾到了坟墓的湿气。
「昨晚才从福布斯神父那里听说您——您的贵恙，」他有些嗫嚅地说。他在棕榈树下一张长凳上坐下，面对病人，帽子仍捏在手里。「我诚心诚意盼您早日复元。」
「我妹妹在她屋里躺着，」迈克尔答。他那一双阴沉、逼人的眼睛，一刻也不曾从对方脸上挪开。他说出这句没头没脑的话，嗓音纤细、冰冷、无动于衷，落在塞隆耳朵里，含有藏而不露的意味。他不安地望进迈克尔的眼睛，又移开。那双眼睛像是直直望穿了他；它们以一种不自然的先知先觉，看见并懂得一些事情——这感觉无从闪避。
「愿她身子好些，」塞隆听见自己说。「福布斯神父提过，她有点不适。昨晚我同他一起用的饭。」
「您来了，我很高兴，」迈克尔略停一停说。那双专注不眨的眼睛，仿佛在替舌头补充另一套言语。「我常常想到您。您既来了，我有些话要对您讲。」
塞隆轻轻颜首，表示感激的恭听。他试着把目光挪开，可它不由分说地又转回去，钉住病人的凝望，粘在那里。
「我已经是一只脚在坟里的人，」他不顾对方劝阻的手势，接着说。「论的不是几年、几个月，是几个星期。随后我就去了，站上去领我罪孽的审判；若蒙祂慈悲的旨意，我就得见上帝。所以我现在就道别；我这些直话，您要容我讲，也别往坏处想。您变了，韦尔先生，自从来到奥克塔维乌斯；而且不是往好里变。」
塞隆毫不做作地扬起眉，眼里放出探询。
「新教徒到上帝定的日子到底能不能得救，我说不准，」迈克尔接着说。「我发现教士们中间也有好几种说法；博学虔诚的学者尚且疑惑，我不过一个粗人，自然更拿不准。可有一样我拿得准。

===Original===

Those Protestants, and others too, mind you, who profess and preach good deeds, and themselves do bad deeds﻿—they will never be saved. They will have no chance at all to escape hellfire.”
“I think we are all agreed upon that, Mr. Madden,” said Theron, with surface suavity.
“Then I say to you, Mr. Ware, you are yourself in a bad path. Take the warning of a dying man, sir, and turn from it!”
The impulse to smile tugged at Theron’s facial muscles. This was really too droll. He looked up at the ceiling, the while he forced his countenance into a polite composure, then turned again to Michael, with some conciliatory commonplace ready for utterance. But he said nothing, and all suggestion of levity left his mind, under the searching inspection bent upon him by the young man’s hollow eyes. What did Michael suspect? What did he know? What was he hinting at, in this strange talk of his?
“I saw you often on the street when first you came here,” continued Michael. “I knew the man who was here before you﻿—that is, by sight﻿—and he was not a good man. But your face, when you came, pleased me. I liked to look at you. I was tormented just then, do you see, that so many decent, kindly people, old schoolmates and friends and neighbors of mine﻿—and, for that matter, others all over the country must lose their souls because they were Protestants. At my boyhood and young manhood, that thought took the joy out of me. Sometimes I usen’t to sleep a whole night long, for thinking that some lad I had been playing with, perhaps in his own house, that very day, would be taken when he died, and his mother too, when she died, and thrown into the flames of hell for all eternity. It made me so unhappy that finally I wouldn’t go to any Protestant boy’s house, and have his mother be nice to me, and give me cake and apples﻿—and me thinking all the while that they were bound to be damned, no matter how good they were to me.”
The primitive humanity of this touched Theron, and he nodded approbation with a tender smile in his eyes, forgetting for the moment that a personal application of the monologue had been hinted at.
“But then later, as I grew up,” the sick man went on, “I learned that it was not altogether certain.

===Chinese===

那些新教徒——还有别的人，您听着——口头宣讲善行、自己却行恶事的，永不能得救。地狱的火，他们一丝逃遁的机会也没有。」
「这一点我想我们大家是一致的，马登先生，」塞隆表面圆滑地说。
「那么我告诉您，韦尔先生，您自己正走在坏路上。听一个垂死的人一句劝告，先生，回头吧！」
微笑的冲动拽着塞隆的脸部肌肉。这实在滑稽透了。他抬眼望天花板，强把脸摆成客气的平静，再转向迈克尔，一句圆场的常备话已到嘴边。可他一个字没说出来；在年轻人那双凹陷的眼睛的搜刺的审视下，一切轻浮的念头都离开了他。迈克尔疑心什么？他知道什么？他这一套怪谈，句句点的是什么？
「您初来时，我在街上常见到您，」迈克尔接着说。「您以前的那一位我认得——只是认得脸——他不是好人。可您来时的那张脸，我喜欢。我爱看您。您不知道，我那时正为一件事受苦：那么多正派、厚道的人，我的老同学、朋友、街坊——再说全国各地的又何尝不是——只因是新教徒，就要丧失灵魂。少年和青年时代，这个念头夺去了我一切的快乐。有时我整夜睡不着，想着白天也许刚在他自己家里同我玩过的哪个孩子，死时会被收去，他母亲死时也会被收去，抛进地狱的火焰里，直到永永远远。这叫我痛苦到后来，我索性不上新教徒孩子的家去了，不叫他母亲待我好、给我饼和苹果——我心里却一直想：他们对我再好，也注定要沉沦。」
这番原始的人情触动了塞隆；他点头称许，眼里带着温柔的笑，一时忘了这一篇独白里已暗示了对他的应用。
「可后来，长大了些，」病人接着说，「我了解到这事并非全然一定。有些权威，我发现，坚持说此事存疑；有些则明说：无辜而不识真相的好人，必有得救的可能。于是我今天有指望，明天没指望；做工时想它，晚上同父亲讨论它——什么也没讨论定。当然，我们怎么可能定呢？」

===Original===

Some of the authorities, I found, maintained that it was doubtful, and some said openly that there must be salvation possible for good people who lived in ignorance of the truth through no fault of their own. Then I had hope one day, and no hope the next, and as I did my work I thought it over, and in the evenings my father and I talked it over, and we settled nothing of it at all. Of course, how could we?”
“Did you ever discuss the question with your sister?” it occurred suddenly to Theron to interpose. He was conscious of some daring in doing so, and he fancied that Michael’s drawn face clouded a little at his words.
“My sister is no theologian,” he answered briefly. “Women have no call to meddle with such matters. But I was saying﻿—it was in the middle of these doubtings of mine that you came here to Octavius, and I noticed you on the streets, and once in the evening﻿—I made no secret of it to my people﻿—I sat in the back of your church and heard you preach. As I say, I liked you. It was your face, and what I thought it showed of the man underneath it, that helped settle my mind more than anything else. I said to myself: ‘Here is a young man, only about my own age, and he has education and talents, and he does not seek to make money for himself, or a great name, but he is content to live humbly on the salary of a bookkeeper, and devote all his time to prayer and the meditation of his religion, and preaching, and visiting the sick and the poor, and comforting them. His very face is a pleasure and a help for those in suffering and trouble to look at. The very sight of it makes one believe in pure thoughts and merciful deeds. I will not credit it that God intends damning such a man as that, or any like him!’ ”
Theron bowed, with a slow, hesitating gravity of manner, and deep, not wholly complacent, attention on his face. Evidently all this was by way of preparation for something unpleasant.
“That was only last spring,” said Michael. His tired voice sank for a sentence or two into a meditative half-whisper. “And it was *my* last spring of all. I shall not be growing weak any more, or drawing hard breaths, when the first warm weather comes. It will be one season to me hereafter, always the same.” He lifted his voice with perceptible effort. “I am talking too much. The rest I can say in a word.

===Chinese===

「这问题你同你妹妹讨论过么？」塞隆忽然想起插这一句。他自觉这话问得大胆；又仿佛觉得，迈克尔那张瘦削的脸闻言暗了一暗。
「我妹妹不是神学家，」他简单地答。「女人不该沾手这些事。可我方才说——正是在我这重重疑心的当口，您来到了奥克塔维乌斯。我在街上注意到您；有一晚——我对家里人也不曾隐瞒——我坐在您教堂后头，听您讲道。我说过，我喜欢您。正是您这张脸，同我从脸上看出的人品，最叫我的心安顿下来。我对自己说：『这是个年轻人，同我差不多年纪；他有学问，有才分，却不想给自己挣钱、挣大名，甘心靠一份记账员的薪水清寒度日，把全部光阴用在祷告、默想他的宗教、讲道、探望病人和穷人、安慰他们上。他这张脸本身，就叫受苦受难的人望着得安慰、得帮助。见了他，人便信世上有纯洁的心思、慈悲的行事。我不能信，上帝存心叫这样一个人沉沦——或叫任何一个同他一样的人沉沦！』」
塞隆一躬到地，缓慢、迟疑而郑重，脸上是深沉的、并不全然受用的专注。这一切，显见得是给什么不愉快的事作的铺垫。
「那不过是今年春天，」迈克尔说。他倦乏的嗓音，有一两句沉成了沉思的半语。「而那是*我*此生最后一个春天。往后第一个暖天来时，我不会再越发虚弱、不再艰难地喘了。它于我从此只是一季，永远一样。」他明显费力地提起嗓音。「我话说得太多了。其余的一句话就说尽。

===Original===

Only half a year has gone by, and you have another face on you entirely. I had noticed the small changes before, one by one. I saw the great change, all of a sudden, the day of the picnic. I see it a hundred times more now, as you sit there. If it seemed to me like the face of a saint before, it is more like the face of a barkeeper now!”
This was quite too much. Theron rose, flushed to the temples, and scowled down at the helpless man in the chair. He swallowed the sharp words which came uppermost, and bit and moistened his lips as he forced himself to remember that this was a dying man, and Celia’s brother, to whom she was devoted, and whom he himself felt he wanted to be very fond of. He got the shadow of a smile on to his countenance.
“I fear you *have* tired yourself unduly,” he said, in as non-contentious a tone as he could manage. He even contrived a little deprecatory laugh. “I am afraid your real quarrel is with the air of Octavius. It agrees with me so wonderfully﻿—I am getting as fat as a seal. But I do hope I am not paying for it by such a wholesale deterioration inside. If my own opinion could be of any value, I should assure you that I feel myself an infinitely better and broader and stronger man than I was when I came here.”
Michael shook his head dogmatically. “That is the greatest pity of all,” he said, with renewed earnestness. “You are entirely deceived about yourself. You do not at all realize how you have altered your direction, or where you are going. It was a great misfortune for you, sir, that you did not keep among your own people. That poor half-brother of mine, though the drink was in him when he said that same to you, never spoke a truer word. Keep among your own people, Mr. Ware! When you go among others﻿—you know what I mean﻿—you have no proper understanding of what their sayings and doings really mean. You do not realize that they are held up by the power of the true Church, as a little child learning to walk is held up with a belt by its nurse. They can say and do things, and no harm at all come to them, which would mean destruction to you, because they have help, and you are walking alone. And so be said by me, Mr. Ware! Go back to the way you were brought up in, and leave alone the people whose ways are different from yours. You are a married man, and you are the preacher of a religion, such as it is.

===Chinese===

才过去半年，您却全然换上另一张脸。小的变化，我先前一处一处看在眼里。大的变化，是野餐那天，猛然一下看见的。此刻您坐在那儿，我看见它又深刻了一百倍。从前我看着像圣徒的一张脸，如今倒像酒吧掌柜的了！」
这实在欺人太甚。塞隆站起身，红到太阳穴，朝椅子里那无助的人皱眉俯视。冲到嘴边的狠话，他咽了回去，咬着、润着嘴唇，强使自己记起：这是个垂死的人，是塞莉娅一心疼爱的哥哥，而他自己也觉得该疼他。他给脸上勉强挣出一丝笑影。
「怕是您当真累过了头，」他尽量用不较劲的口吻说，甚至还挤出一点告罪的轻笑。「我看您真正过不去的是奥克塔维乌斯的空气。它于我可太相宜了——我都胖得像头海豹。但愿我没有为此付出内脏全面败坏的代价。倘若我自己的话作得数，我可以向您担保：比起初来时，我自觉是个好得多、宽得多、强得多的人。」
迈克尔武断地摇头。「这才是顶可惜的一点，」他带着更新的恳切说。「您完全看错了自己。您根本不明白自己已转了向，也不知道正走向哪里。先生，您没有守在自家人中间，这是您的大不幸。我那可怜的半弟弟，虽然对我说那话时酒在身上，说的却是再真不过的理。守在自家人中间吧，韦尔先生！您走到外人中间——您懂我的意思——就全然摸不清他们的言行究竟是什么意思。您不明白：他们是靠真教会的力量托着的，好比学步的娃娃，叫奶妈用一条带子吊着。他们说得、做得，一点祸也没有；您说得、做得，就是毁灭——因为他们有扶持，您是独自行走。所以，听我一句，韦尔先生！回到您受教养的那条路上去，别再沾同您路数不同的人。您是有妇之夫，又是一门宗教的传道人——不管那宗教怎样。

===Original===

There can be nothing better for you than to go and strive to be a good husband, and to set a good example to the people of your Church, who look up to you﻿—and mix yourself up no more with outside people and outside notions that only do you mischief. And that is what I wanted to say to you.”
Theron took up his hat. “I take in all kindness what you have felt it your duty to say to me, Mr. Madden,” he said. “I am not sure that I have altogether followed you, but I am very sure you mean it well.”
“I mean well by you,” replied Michael, wearily moving his head on the pillow, and speaking in an undertone of languor and pain, “and I mean well by others, that are nearer to me, and that I have a right to care more about. When a man lies by the site of his open grave, he does not be meaning ill to any human soul.”
“Yes﻿—thanks﻿—quite so!” faltered Theron. He dallied for an instant with the temptation to seek some further explanation, but the sight of Michael’s half-closed eyes and worn-out expression decided him against it. It did not seem to be expected, either, that he should shake hands, and with a few perfunctory words of hope for the invalid’s recovery, which fell with a jarring note of falsehood upon his own ears, he turned and left the room. As he did so, Michael touched a bell on the table beside him.
Theron drew a long breath in the hall, as the curtain fell behind him. It was an immense relief to escape from the oppressive humidity and heat of the flower-room, and from that ridiculous bore of a Michael as well.
The middle-aged, grave-faced servant, warned by the bell, stood waiting to conduct him to the door.
“I am sorry to have missed Miss Madden,” he said to her. “She must be quite worn out. Perhaps later in the day﻿—”
“She will not be seeing anybody today,” returned the woman. “She is going to New York this evening, and she is taking some rest against the journey.”
“Will she be away long?” he asked mechanically. The servant’s answer, “I have no idea,” hardly penetrated his consciousness at all.
He moved down the steps, and along the gravel to the street, in a maze of mental confusion. When he reached the sidewalk, under the familiar elms, he paused, and made a definite effort to pull his thoughts together, and take stock of what had happened, of what was going to happen; but the thing baffled him. It was as if some drug had stupefied his faculties.

===Chinese===

再没有比这于您更好的了：回去努力做个好丈夫，给您教会仰望您的会众立个好榜样——别再同外人外道搅在一起，那些只叫您吃亏。这，就是我要对您说的话。」
塞隆拿起帽子。「您觉得有责任对我说的这些话，我全当好意收下，马登先生，」他说。「我不敢说全跟上了您的话；可我万分相信您是出于好意。」
「我是为您好，」迈克尔答，头在枕上倦倦地动了动，声音低沉、倦怠而带痛，「也为旁人好——于我更亲的旁人，那些我更有权利牵挂的。一个人躺在自己敞开的坟墓边上，对哪一个世人的灵魂，也不会存恶意。」
「是——谢谢——正是！」塞隆嗫嚅。他一时痒痒地想再讨一点解说；可看见迈克尔半阖的眼和力竭的神情，便打消了。看来也不指望他握手；他随口说了几句祝病人康复的门面话——话落进他自己的耳朵，带着刺耳的假音——转身出了屋。他这样走时，迈克尔按了按手边桌上的铃。
帘子在身后落下，塞隆在门厅里长长吸了一口气。逃出花房那压人的湿热，也逃开迈克尔那个可笑的厌物，真是莫大的宽解。
那中年的、面色凝重的女仆，得了铃声知会，正候着送他到门口。
「没见着马登小姐，很遗憾，」他对她说。「她必是累坏了。也许晚些时候——」
「她今天谁也不见，」女人答。「她今晚去纽约，正歇着养神，好上路。」
「要去很久么？」他机械地问。女仆那一句「我说不上」，几乎没有透进他的知觉。
他步下台阶，沿砾石道走到街上，脑中一团乱麻。走到熟识的榆树底下的行人道，他站住，打定主意把思绪收拢，盘一盘已经发生、将要发生的事；可是这团东西解不开。仿佛有什么药物麻了他的官能。

===Original===

He began to walk, and gradually saw that what he was thinking about was the fact of Celia’s departure for New York that evening. He stared at this fact, at first in its nakedness, then clothed with reassuring suggestions that this was no doubt a trip she very often made. There was a blind sense of comfort in this idea, and he rested himself upon it. Yes, of course, she travelled a great deal. New York must be as familiar to her as Octavius was to him. Her going there now was quite a matter of course﻿—the most natural thing in the world.
Then there burst suddenly uppermost in his mind the other fact﻿—that Father Forbes was also going to New York that evening. The two things spindled upward, side by side, yet separately, in his mental vision; then they twisted and twined themselves together. He followed their convolutions miserably, walking as if his eyes were shut.
In slow fashion matters defined and arranged themselves before him. The process of tracing their sequence was all torture, but there was no possibility, no notion, of shirking any detail of the pain. The priest had spoken of his efforts to persuade Celia to go away for a few days, for rest and change of air and scene. He must have known only too well that she was going, but of that he had been careful to drop no hint. The possibility of accident was too slight to be worth considering. People on such intimate terms as Celia and the priest﻿—people with such facilities for seeing each other whenever they desired﻿—did not find themselves on the same train of cars, with the same long journey in view, by mere chance.
Theron walked until dusk began to close in upon the autumn day. It grew colder, as he turned his face homeward. He wondered if it would freeze again overnight, and then remembered the shrivelled flowers in his wife’s garden. For a moment they shaped themselves in a picture before his mind’s eye; he saw their blackened foliage, their sicklied, drooping stalks, and wilted blooms, and as he looked, they restored themselves to the vigor and grace and richness of color of summertime, as vividly as if they had been painted on a canvas. Or no, the picture he stared at was not on canvas, but on the glossy, varnished panel of a luxurious sleeping-car.

===Chinese===

他开始走；渐渐看出，他正想着的，是塞莉娅今晚动身去纽约这事实。他直瞪着这个事实——起初是它赤裸裸的本身，随后给它披上宽慰的揣测：这想必是她常来常往的一趟。这想法里有一种盲目的舒服，他便靠着它歇脚。是，当然，她走动得多。纽约于她，必像奥克塔维乌斯于他一样熟。她此刻去那里，完全顺理成章——世上顶自然不过的事。
随即另一个事实猛地蹿上他心头——福布斯神父今晚也去纽约。两桩事在他的心眼里并排竖起，各自分开；随即绞缠到了一起。他凄凄惶惶跟着它们的盘绕走，眼睛像闭着一样。
诸事缓缓地在他面前定义、排队。理出先后的过程全是酷刑；可这苦，一点也躲不掉，一星也想不到躲。神父讲过，他怎样劝塞莉娅出门几天、换换空气和环境。他必再清楚不过地知道她要走；这一点，他仔细得不露一丝口风。偶然同行的可能，小得不值一提。像塞莉娅同神父这样亲昵的两个人——要见随时得见的两个人——断不会碰巧落进同一班火车、奔同一条长路。
塞隆一直走到暮色开始合拢这秋日。转身回家时，天更冷了。他捉摸夜里会不会又上冻；随即想起他太太园里那些蔫了的花。有一瞬，它们在他心眼前拼成一幅画：他看见它们焦黑的叶、病恹恹垂倒的梗、萎了的花；而当他望着，它们又还原成夏日那精神的丰姿和浓色，鲜活得像画在画布上。不——他盯着的这幅画不在画布上，而在一节豪华卧车那上光打蜡的车壁板上。

===Original===

He shook his head angrily and blinked his eyes again and again, to prevent their seeing, seated together in the open window above this panel, the two people he knew were there, gloved and habited for the night’s journey, waiting for the train to start.
---
“Very much to my surprise,” he found himself saying to Alice, watching her nervously as she laid the supper-table, “I find I must go to Albany tonight. That is, it isn’t absolutely necessary, for that matter, but I think it may easily turn out to be greatly to my advantage to go. Something has arisen﻿—I can’t speak about it as yet﻿—but the sooner I see the Bishop about it the better. Things like that occur in a man’s life, where boldly striking out a line of action, and following it up without an instant’s delay, may make all the difference in the world to him. Tomorrow it might be too late; and, besides, I can be home the sooner again.”
Alice’s face showed surprise, but no trace of suspicion. She spoke with studied amiability during the meal, and deferred with such unexpected tact to his implied desire not to be questioned as to the mysterious motives of the journey, that his mood instinctively softened and warmed toward her, as they finished supper.
He smiled a little. “I do hope I shan’t have to go on tomorrow to New York; but these Bishops of ours are such gadabouts one never knows where to catch them. As like as not Sanderson may be down in New York, on Book-Concern business or something; and if he is, I shall have to chase him up. But, after all, perhaps the trip will do me good﻿—the change of air and scene, you know.”
“I’m sure I hope so,” said Alice, honestly enough. “If you do go on to New York, I suppose you’ll go by the river-boat. Everybody talks so much of that beautiful sail down the Hudson.”
“That’s an idea!” exclaimed Theron, welcoming it with enthusiasm. “It hadn’t occurred to me. If I do have to go, and it is as lovely as they make out, the next time I promise I won’t go without you, my girl. I *have* been rather out of sorts lately,” he continued. “When I come back, I daresay I shall be feeling better, more like my old self. Then I’m going to try, Alice, to be nicer to you than I have been of late. I’m afraid there was only too much truth in what you said this morning.”
“Never mind what I said this morning﻿—or any other time,” broke in Alice, softly.

===Chinese===

他愤怒地摇头，一次又一次眨眼，免得自己的眼睛看见那壁板上方敞开的车窗里并坐的两个人——他知道他们在那儿，戴着手套、作好了夜行的装束，等开车。
---
「大大出乎我意料，」他听见自己对艾丽斯说——她正摆晚饭，他紧张地望着她，「我发现今晚我必须去一趟奥尔巴尼。就是说，倒也不是非去不可；可我想，去了很可能大有益处。出了一件事——眼下还不能讲——但越早见到主教越好。人一生里总有这样的时候：果断地定一条行动方针，一刻也不耽搁地跟上去，可能就决定了他的全部前程。到明天也许就迟了；再说，我也能早去早回。」
艾丽斯脸上是惊讶，却没有一丝疑心。整顿饭她说话客气周到，又那样出人意料地有分寸，不探问此行神秘的原因——塞隆不由得心软了、暖了，饭吃完时。
他微微一笑。「我实在指望明天不必再往前去纽约；可我们这些主教都是满天飞的人，谁知道在哪儿逮得着他。多半桑德森就在纽约，为圣书公会什么的办事；真是那样，我就只好追去。不过说到底，这趟说不定于我有益——换换空气和环境，您知道的。」
「我诚心盼望如此，」艾丽斯说得十足诚恳。「您若真去纽约，我想您会坐河轮吧。人人都那样夸哈德逊河上那趟美丽的航行。」
「好主意！」塞隆热烈地欢迎。「我倒没有想到。若真要去，又真像他们说的那样美，我保证下回不带你去就不去，我的姑娘。我近来确实有点没精神，」他接着说。「回来时，我多半会觉得好些，更像从前的我。那时我要想法待你更好些，艾丽斯，胜过近来。只怕你今天早上说的，实在没有冤枉我。」
「别把我今天早上说的放在心上——任何时候说的都别，」艾丽斯轻声打断。

===Original===

“Don’t ever remember it again, Theron, if only﻿—only﻿—”
He rose as she spoke, moved round the table to where she sat, and, bending over her, stopped the faltering sentence with a kiss. When was it, he wondered, that he had last kissed her? It seemed years, ages, ago.
An hour later, with hat and overcoat on, and his valise in his hand, he stood on the doorstep of the parsonage, and kissed her once more before he turned and descended into the darkness. He felt like whistling as his feet sounded firmly on the plank sidewalk beyond the gate. It seemed as if he had never been in such capital good spirits before in his life.

===Chinese===

「永远别再记得它，塞隆，只要——只要——」
她说话时他站起身，绕过桌子到她坐的地方，俰下身去，用一个吻堵住了那句嗫嚅的话。他上一回吻她，是什么时候来着？仿佛隔了几年、几个世代。
一个钟头后，他戴好帽子、穿好大衣，提着旅行包，站在牧师住宅的门阶上，转身没入黑暗之前，又吻了她一回。出了院门、双脚笃笃踏上木板人行道时，他直想吹口哨。生平仿佛从不曾这样神清气爽过。

### XXX / 三十
===Original===

At ten o’clock Theron, loitering near the bookstall in the corridor, saw Father Forbes come downstairs, pass out through the big front doors, get into a carriage, and drive away.
This relieved him of a certain sense of responsibility, and he retired to a corner sofa and sat down. The detective side of him being off duty, so to speak, there was leisure at last for reflection upon the other aspects of his mission. Yes; it was high time for him to consider what he should do next.
It was easier to recognize this fact, however, than to act upon it. His mind was full of tricksy devices for eluding this task of serious thought which he sought to impose upon it. It seemed so much pleasanter not to think at all﻿—but just to drift. He found himself watching with envy the men who, as they came out from their breakfast, walked over to the bookstall, and bought cigars from the row of boxes nestling there among the newspaper piles. They had such evident delight in the work of selection; they took off the ends of the cigars so carefully, and lighted them with such meditative attention﻿—he could see that he was woefully handicapped by not knowing how to smoke. He had had the most wonderful breakfast of his life, but even in the consciousness of comfortable repletion which pervaded his being, there was an obstinate sense of something lacking. No doubt a good cigar was the thing needed to round out the perfection of such a breakfast. He half rose once, fired by a sudden resolution to go over and get one. But of course that was nonsense; it would only make him sick. He sat down, and determinedly set himself to thinking.
The effort finally brought fruit﻿—and of a kind which gave him a very unhappy quarter of an hour. The lover part of him was uppermost now, insistently exposing all its raw surfaces to the stings and scalds of jealousy. Up to this moment, his brain had always evaded the direct question of how he and the priest relatively stood in Celia’s estimation. It forced itself remorselessly upon him now; and his thoughts, so far from shirking the subject, seemed to rise up to meet it. It was extremely unpleasant, all this.
But then a calmer view asserted itself. Why go out of his way to invent anguish for himself? The relations between Celia and the priest, whatever they might be, were certainly of old standing. They had begun before his time.

===Chinese===

十点钟，塞隆在走廊的书报亭近旁闲荡，望见福布斯神父下了楼，从大前门出去，登上一辆马车，驶走了。
这卸下了他某一分责任感；他退到角落一张沙发上坐下。可以说，他身上那侦探的一面下了班，终于有闲暇反省此行使命的另外几面了。是；该考虑下一步怎么办，正是时候了。
然而，认清这一事实容易，照着办却难。他的头脑里满是习钻的花招，想逃避他强加给它的这桩严肃思考。什么都不想、只管漂流，似乎殹意得多。他发觉自己羡慕起那些人来：他们用完早餐出来，践到书报亭前，从报纸堆间挨挤着的一排盒子里买雪茄。他们挑选的工夫里有那样明白的乐趣；他们那样细心地掐去烟头，那样凝神地点燃——他看得出，不会抽烟这件事叫他吃了大亏。他刚度过了平生最精彩的一顿早餐；可纵然一身漫着饱餐的舒泰，也还有一股执拗的缺憾之感。无疑，一支好雪茄正是一顿这样的早餐补足圆满所需的东西。有一回他半站起来，一时奋发，想去买一支。可这自然是无稽之举；那只会叫他难受。他坐下，横下心来思索。
这番努力终于结了果——一种叫他很不痛快地过了一刻钟的果。他身上恋人的一部分此刻占了上风，执拗地把它一切生嫩的创面，袒给嫉妒的螫刺和烫泼。直到此刻，他的头脑始终回避那个直截的问题：他和神父在塞莉娅的估量里各占什么位置。此刻它毫不留情地逼上来了；他的念头非但不避，反倒迎了上去。这一切都极不愉快。
可随后一个较冷静的看法立了起来。何苦绕道给自己发明苦恼？塞莉娅同神父的关系，无论是什么，都必定由来已久。它们起在他来之前。

===Original===

His own romance was a more recent affair, and must take its place, of course, subject to existing conditions.
It was all right for him to come to New York, and satisfy his legitimate curiosity as to the exact character and scope of these conditions. But it was foolish to pretend to be amazed or dismayed at the discovery of their existence. They were a part of the situation which he, with his eyes wide open, had accepted. It was his function to triumph over them, to supplant them, to rear the edifice of his own victorious passion upon their ruins. It was to this that Celia’s kiss had invited him. It was for this that he had come to New York. To let his purpose be hampered or thwarted now by childish doubts and jealousies would be ridiculous.
He rose, and holding himself very erect, walked with measured deliberation across the corridor and up the broad staircase. There was an elevator near at hand, he had noticed, but he preferred the stairs. One or two of the colored boys clustered about the foot of the stairs looked at him, and he had a moment of dreadful apprehension lest they should stop his progress. Nothing was said, and he went on. The numbers on the first floor were not what he wanted, and after some wandering about he ascended to the next, and then to the third. Every now and then he encountered attendants, but intuitively he bore himself with an air of knowing what he was about which protected him from inquiry.
Finally he came upon the hallway he sought. Passing along, he found the doors bearing the numbers he had memorized so well. They were quite close together, and there was nothing to help him guess which belonged to the parlor. He hesitated, gazing wistfully from one to the other. In the instant of indecision, even while his alert ear caught the sound of feet coming along toward the passage in which he stood, a thought came to quicken his resolve. It became apparent to him that his discovery gave him a certain new measure of freedom with Celia, a sort of right to take things more for granted than heretofore. He chose a door at random, and rapped distinctly on the panel.
“Come!”
The voice he knew for Celia’s. The single word, however, recalled the usage of Father Forbes, which he had noted more than once at the pastorate, when Maggie had knocked.
He straightened his shoulders, took his hat off, and pushed open the door.

===Chinese===

他自己这段罗曼史是更晚近的事，当然必须就位在既成条件之下。
他来纽约，满足一下关于这些条件的性质和范围的好奇，这原无不可。可发现它们存在便假装惊讶或惊惶，就是愚蠢了。它们是他睁着眼接受下来的局面的一部分。他的职能是战胜它们，取代它们，在他自己得胜的热情的废墟上，筑起他自己的大厦。塞莉娅的那一吻邀请的正是这个。他为的正是这个才来纽约。此刻让幼稚的疑惧和醋意碍住、折了他的大志，未免可笑。
他站起身，把身子挺得笔直，以有分寸的从容穿过走廊，登上宽阔的楼梯。近旁就有一部电梯，他注意到了，可他情愿走楼梯。聚在楼梯脚的一两个有色男孩望了望他；他有一阵可怕的担心，怕他们拦他的路。没有人开口，他继续走。二楼的门牌不是他要的；盘桓了一阵，他上到再上一层，又上到第三层。他不时撞见侍者，可他凭直觉摆出一副知道自己来做什么的气派，保他不受盘问。
末了他撞见了寻访的那条走廊。走过去，找到了那两个他背得烂熟的门牌号。两扇门挨得很近，没有什么能帮他猜哪一扇通客厅。他迟疑着，怅惘地这边望望、那边望望。在举棋不定的一瞬——就在他警觉的耳朵捕捉到有脚步朝他站立的这条走廊走来时——一个念头来给他的决断加了速。他明白了：他的发现给了他同一份新的自由，一种比先前更可以把事情视作当然的权利。他随便挑了一扇门，在门板上清清楚楚地叩了叩。
“进来！”
这嗓音他一听便知是塞莉娅的。可这一个字，也叫他想起福布斯神父的惯用语——在牧师寓所，玛吉来叩门时，他不只一回注意到了。
他挺一挺肩，摘下帽子，推门进去。

===Original===

It *was* the parlor﻿—a room of sofas, pianos, big easy-chairs, and luxurious bric-a-brac. A tall woman was walking up and down in it, with bowed head. Her back was at the moment toward him; and he looked at her, saying to himself that this was the lady of his dreams, the enchantress of the kiss, the woman who loved him﻿—but somehow it did not seem to his senses to be Celia.
She turned, and moved a step or two in his direction before she mechanically lifted her eyes and saw who was standing in her doorway. She stopped short, and regarded him. Her face was in the shadow, and he could make out nothing of its expression, save that there was a general effect of gravity about it.
“I cannot receive you,” she said. “You must go away. You have no business to come like this without sending up your card.”
Theron smiled at her. The notion of taking in earnest her inhospitable words did not at all occur to him. He could see now that her face had vexed and saddened lines upon it, and the sharpness of her tone remained in his ears. But he smiled again gently, to reassure her.
“I ought to have sent up my name, I know,” he said, “but I couldn’t bear to wait. I just saw your name on the register and﻿—you *will* forgive me, won’t you?﻿—I ran to you at once. I know you won’t have the heart to send me away!”
She stood where she had halted, her arms behind her, looking him fixedly in the face. He had made a movement to advance, and offer his hand in greeting, but her posture checked the impulse. His courage began to falter under her inspection.
“Must I really go down again?” he pleaded. “It’s a crushing penalty to suffer for such little indiscretion. I was so excited to find you were here﻿—I never stopped to think. Don’t send me away; please don’t!”
Celia raised her head. “Well, shut the door, then,” she said, “since you are so anxious to stay. You would have done much better, though, very much better indeed, to have taken the hint and gone away.”
“Will you shake hands with me, Celia?” he asked softly, as he came near her.
“Sit there, please!” she made answer, indicating a chair in the middle of the room. He obeyed her, but to his surprise, instead of seating herself as well, she began walking up and down the length of the floor again. After a turn or two she stopped in front of him, and looked him full in the eye.

===Chinese===

正是客厅——一间满是沙发、钢琴、大安乐椅和奢华古玩的屋子。一个高挑的女人正在屋里踱步，低着头。这一刻背影朝着他；他望着她，对自己说：这就是他梦中的女人，那一吻的妖姬，爱他的女人——可不知怎么，他的感官不觉得这是塞莉娅。
她转过身，朝他这边挪了一两步，才机械地抬起眼，看见立在门口的是谁。她蒦地停住，端详他。她的脸在阴影里，他辨不出它的神情，只觉出一片笼统的凝重。
“我不能接待你，”她说，“你必须走。不递名片就这样闯上来，是没有道理的。”
塞隆朝她微笑。把这不客气的言语当真，这念头根本不曾进他脑子。他此刻看得出，她脸上有恼怒和悲伤的纹，她话音的尖利也留在他耳中。可他又温柔地笑了笑，好让她安心。
“我本该先把名字送上来，我知道，”他说，“可我等不及。我在登记簿上一看见你的名字就——你*会*原谅我的，是不是？——我立刻就奔上来了。我知道你狠不下心赶我走！”
她立在她停步的地方，双臂背在身后，定定地端详他的脸。他本已动身要上前握手问好，被她这姿势拦下了那冲动。在她的审视之下，他的勇气开始动摇。
“我真得再下去一趟么？”他恳求，“为这样一点小小的不检点受这样毁灭性的刑罚。发现你在这里，我那样兴奋——根本没停下来想。别赶我走；求你别赶！”
塞莉娅昂起头。“那么，把门关上，”她说，“既然你这样急着留下。不过，你本该好得多——实在好得多——识得那点暗示就走开的。”
“同我握握手好么，塞莉娅？”他挨近她，轻声问。
“请坐那儿！”她回答，指了指屋子当中的一把椅子。他依了；可叫他意外的是，她并没有也落座，反而又开始沿屋子的长度踱起步来。转了一两圈，她在他面前停住，直视他的眼睛。

===Original===

The light from the windows was on her countenance now, and its revelations vaguely troubled him. It was a Celia he had never seen before who confronted him.
“I am much occupied by other matters,” she said, speaking with cold impassivity, “but still I find myself curious to know just what limits you set to your dishonesty.”
Theron stared up at her. His lips quivered, but no speech came to them. If this was all merely fond playfulness, it was being carried to a heart-aching point.
“I saw you hiding about in the depot at home last evening,” she went on. “You come up here, pretending to have discovered me by accident, but I saw you following me from the Grand Central this morning.”
“Yes, I did both these things,” said Theron, boldly. A fine bravery tingled in his veins all at once. He looked into her face and found the spirit to disregard its frowning aspect. “Yes, I did them,” he repeated defiantly. “That is not the hundredth part, or the thousandth part, of what I would do for your sake. I have got way beyond caring for any consequences. Position, reputation, the good opinion of fools﻿—what are they? Life itself﻿—what does it amount to? Nothing at all﻿—with you in the balance!”
“Yes﻿—but I am not in the balance,” observed Celia, quietly. “That is where you have made your mistake.”
Theron laid aside his hat. Women were curious creatures, he reflected. Some were susceptible to one line of treatment, some to another. His own reading of Celia had always been that she liked opposition, of a smart, rattling, almost cheeky, sort. One got on best with her by saying bright things. He searched his brain now for some clever quip that would strike sparks from the adamantine mood which for the moment it was her whim to assume. To cover the process, he smiled a little. Then her beauty, as she stood before him, her queenly form clad in a more stiffly fashionable dress than he had seen her wearing before, appealed afresh and overwhelmingly to him. He rose to his feet.
“Have you forgotten our talk in the woods?” he murmured with a wooing note. “Have you forgotten the kiss?”
She shook her head calmly. “I have forgotten nothing.”
“Then why play with me so cruelly now?” he went on, in a voice of tender deprecation. “I know you don’t mean it, but all the same it bruises my heart a little.

===Chinese===

窗上的光此刻照在她脸上；它透露的东西隐约叫他不安。面对他的，是一个他从没有见过的塞莉娅。
“我另有许多事缠身，”她说，说得冷淡平板，“可我仍旧不免好奇，想知道你给自己的不诚实划的界线，究竟在哪里。”
塞隆仰头瞪着她。他的嘴唇颤了，却说不出话来。倘若这一切不过是有情的玩笑，它已经开到了令人心碎的地步。
“昨天傍晚，我看见你在家那边的车站里藏着，”她接着说，“你上这里来，装作偶然发现了我；可今天早晨，从中央车站起，我就看见你跟着我。”
“是，这两件事我都做了，”塞隆说，放胆地。一阵漂亮的豪勇蒦地流进他的血管。他望着她的脸，竟有胆量无视它的阴沉。“是，我做了，”他挑衅地重复，“为你，这还抵不上我会做的事的百分之一、千分之一。什么后果，我早已全不在乎。地位、名声、蠢人的好评——算得了什么？性命本身——又算什么？一钱不值——同你放在天平上的分量一比！”
“是——可我不在天平上，”塞莉娅静静地指出，“你的错就错在这里。”
塞隆把帽子掠到一边。女人是奇怪的造物，他想。有的吃这一套，有的吃那一套。他一向对塞莉娅的解读是：她喜欢对抗——机灵的、连珠炮似的、近乎放肆的对抗。同她打交道，妙语最占便宜。他此刻在脑子里搜寻一句俏皮话，好从她一时兴起摆出的这副金刚不坏上磕出火花来。为掩住这搜寻，他微微一笑。随后，她立在面前的那份美——她那王后的身量，裹着一身比他从前所见更挺括入时的裙裾——重新以压倒之势打动了他。他站了起来。
“你忘了咱们在林中的谈话了么？”他带着求爱的声调低语，“你忘了那一吻么？”
她平静地摇头。“我什么也没有忘。”
“那你此刻为什么这样残忍地耍弄我？”他以温柔的哀告接着说，“我知道你不是有心的，可它仍旧把我的心碰伤了一点。

===Original===

I build myself so wholly upon you, I have made existence itself depend so completely upon your smile, upon a soft glance in your eyes, that when they are not there, why, I suffer, I don’t know how to live at all. So be kinder to me, Celia!”
“I was kinder, as you call it, when you came in,” she replied. “I told you to go away. That was pure kindness﻿—more kindness than you deserved.”
Theron looked at his hat, where it stood on the carpet by his feet. He felt tears coming into his eyes. “You tell me that you remember,” he said, in depressed tones, “and yet you treat me like this! Perhaps I am wrong. No doubt it is my own fault. I suppose I ought not to have come down here at all.”
Celia nodded her head in assent to this view.
“But I swear that I was helpless in the matter,” he burst forth. “I *had* to come! It would have been literally impossible for me to have stayed at home, knowing that you were here, and knowing also that﻿—that﻿—”
“Go on!” said Celia, thrusting forth her underlip a trifle, and hardening still further the gleam in her eye, as he stumbled over his sentence and left it unfinished. “What was the other thing that you were ‘knowing’?”
“Knowing﻿—” he took up the word hesitatingly﻿—“knowing that life would be insupportable to me if I could not be near you.”
She curled her lip at him. “You skated over the thin spot very well,” she commented. “It was on the tip of your tongue to mention the fact that Father Forbes came with me. Oh, I can read you through and through, Mr. Ware.”
In a misty way Theron felt things slipping from his grasp. The rising moisture blurred his eyes as their gaze clung to Celia.
“Then if you do read me,” he protested, “you must know how utterly my heart and brain are filled with you. No other man in all the world can yield himself so absolutely to the woman he worships as I can. You have taken possession of me so wholly, I am not in the least master of myself any more. I don’t know what I say or what I do. I am not worthy of you, I know. No man alive could be that. But no one else will idolize and reverence you as I do. Believe me when I say that, Celia! And how can you blame me, in your heart, for following you? ‘Whither thou goest, I will go, and where thou lodgest I will lodge; thy people shall be my people, and thy God my God; where thou diest, will I die, and there will I be buried.

===Chinese===

“我把自己的全部都搭在你身上，我把生存本身全押在你的微笑上、押在你眼睛里一点温柔的一瞥上；它们不在的时候，嗐，我就受苦，我就全然不知怎样活。所以待我仁慈些，塞莉娅！”
“你进来的时候，我已经仁慈过了——照你的说法，”她答，“我叫你走。那是纯粹的仁慈——比你配得的仁慈多。”
塞隆望着他那顶立在脚边地毯上的帽子。他觉得眼泪正涌上眼睛。“你说你记得，”他垂头丧气地说，“却这样待我！也许是我错了。无疑是我自己的错。我原就不该到这儿来。”
塞莉娅点头，赞成这个看法。
“可我发誓，这件事上我无能为力，”他发作了，“我*不得不*来！知道你在这里，又知道——知道——却还留在家里，对我说来简直不可能。”
“说下去！”塞莉娅说，把下唇微微一撅，眼里的寒光又硬了一层，因为他绊在半截话上收了尾。“你‘知道’的另一件事是什么？”
“知道——”他迟疑地接过这个词，“知道如果不能在你身边，人生于我将无法忍受。”
她朝他一撇嘴。“薄冰上那一段，你滑得很好，”她评论道，“话都到了舌尖：福布斯神父是同我一起来的。哦，我把你从头到脚读得透透的，韦尔先生。”
塞隆恍惚觉得，一切正从他手里溜走。涌上来的湿气模糊了他的眼，而他的目光还粘在塞莉娅身上。
“那么你既然读得透我，”他抗议，“就必然知道我的心和脑被你填得怎样满。普天之下，没有别的男人能像我这样，把自己全然交给他崇拜的女人。你已经这样整个地占据了我，我再也全然做不得自己的主。我不知道我说什么、做什么。我配不上你，我知道。活着的男人谁也配不上。可再没有别人会像我这样崇拜你、敬奉你。我说这话时请相信我，塞莉娅！你在心里，又怎能怪我跟着你？‘你往哪里去，我也往那里去；你在哪里住宿，我也在那里住宿；你的国就是我的国，你的神就是我的神；你在哪里死，我也在那里死，也葬在那里。除非死能使你我相离，不然，愿耶和华重重地降罚与我！’”

===Original===

The Lord do so to me, and more also, if aught but death part thee and me!’ ”
Celia shrugged her shoulders, and moved a few steps away from him. Something like despair seized upon him.
“Surely,” he urged with passion, “surely I have a right to remind you of the kiss!”
She turned. “The kiss,” she said meditatively. “Yes, you have a right to remind me of it. Oh, yes, an undoubted right. You have another right too﻿—the right to have the kiss explained to you. It was of the goodbye order. It signified that we weren’t to meet again, and that just for one little moment I permitted myself to be sorry for you. That was all.”
He held himself erect under the incredible words, and gazed blankly at her. The magnitude of what he confronted bewildered him; his mind was incapable of taking it in. “You mean﻿—” he started to say, and then stopped, helplessly staring into her face, with a dropped jaw. It was too much to try to think what she meant.
A little side-thought sprouted in the confusion of his brain. It grew until it spread a bitter smile over his pale face. “I know so little about kisses,” he said; “I am such a greenhorn at that sort of thing. You should have had pity on my inexperience, and told me just what brand of kiss it was I was getting. Probably I ought to have been able to distinguish, but you see I was brought up in the country﻿—on a farm. They don’t have kisses in assorted varieties there.”
She bowed her head slightly. “Yes, you are entitled to say that,” she assented. “I was to blame, and it is quite fair that you should tell me so. You spoke of your inexperience, your innocence. That was why I kissed you in saying goodbye. It was in memory of that innocence of yours, to which you yourself had been busy saying goodbye ever since I first saw you. The idea seemed to me to mean something at the moment. I see now that it was too subtle. I do not usually err on that side.”
Theron kept his hold upon her gaze, as if it afforded him bodily support. He felt that he ought to stoop and take up his hat, but he dared not look away from her. “Do you not err now, on the side of cruelty?” he asked her piteously.
It seemed for the instant as if she were wavering, and he swiftly thrust forth other pleas. “I admit that I did wrong to follow you to New York. I see that now. But it was an offence committed in entire good faith. Think of it, Celia!

===Chinese===

塞莉娅耸了耸肩，从他身边挪开几步。一阵近乎绝望的东西攫住了他。
“当然，”他热烈地敦促，“我总有权利提醒你那一吻吧！”
她转过身。“那一吻，”她若有所思地说，“是，你有权利向我提它。哦，是，无可置疑的权利。你还有另一个权利——让那一吻向你解释清楚的权利。它是道别的那一种。它的意思是：咱们不要再见面了；只不过有小小的一刻，我容许自己为你惋惜。如此而已。”
他在这些难以置信的话底下挺直了身子，茫然瞪着她。他所面对之事的庞大把他弄糊涂了；他的头脑吞不下它。“你的意思是——”他起头要说，随即停住，无助地瞪着她的脸，下巴垂着。要想她是什么意思，太多了。
一个旁逸的小念头在他纷乱的脑子里发了芽。它长啊长，直到在他苍白的脸上铺开一个苦笑。“我对吻懂得这样少，”他说，“这一类事上，我是这样的生手。你本该怜悯我的无知，告诉我接到的究竟是哪一个牌子的吻。按说我该分辨得出；可你知道，我是在乡下长大的——在农场上。那儿没有分门别类的吻。”
她微微颜首。“是，你有资格这样说，”她认可，“错在我；你来告诉我，也完全公平。你说到你的无知，你的天真。我道别时吻你，正是为这个。是为了纪念你那份天真——从我初见你起，你自己就一直忙着同它道别的那份天真。这主意当时于我仿佛还有点意思。我如今看出，它太微妙了。我平常不在这一边上出错。”
塞隆始终攥着她的目光，仿佛它给他身体的支撑。他觉得自己该弯腰拾起帽子，却不敢把眼从她身上挪开。“你此刻不是正在另一边上出错么——在残忍这一边？”他可怜兮兮地问。
有一瞬她仿佛动摇了；他赶紧又递上别的恳求。“我承认，跟你到纽约来是我错了。我如今看明白了。可那是一桩全然出于诚心的过犯。想想吧，塞莉娅！

===Original===

I have never seen you since that day﻿—that day in the woods. I have waited﻿—and waited﻿—with no sign from you, no chance of seeing you at all. Think what that meant to me! Everything in the world had been altered for me, torn up by the roots. I was a new being, plunged into a new existence. The kiss had done that. But until I saw you again, I could not tell whether this vast change in me and my life was for good or for bad﻿—whether the kiss had come to me as a blessing or a curse. The suspense was killing me, Celia! That is why, when I learned that you were coming here, I threw everything to the winds and followed you. You blame me for it, and I bow my head and accept the blame. But are you justified in punishing me so terribly﻿—in going on after I have confessed my error, and cutting my heart into little strips, putting me to death by torture?”
“Sit down,” said Celia, with a softened weariness in her voice. She seated herself in front of him as he sank into his chair again. “I don’t want to give you unnecessary pain, but you have insisted on forcing yourself into a position where there isn’t anything else but pain. I warned you to go away, but you wouldn’t. No matter how gently I may try to explain things to you, you are bound to get nothing but suffering out of the explanation. Now shall I still go on?”
He inclined his head in token of assent, and did not lift it again, but raised toward her a disconsolate gaze from a pallid, drooping face.
“It is all in a single word, Mr. Ware,” she proceeded, in low tones. “I speak for others as well as myself, mind you﻿—we find that you are a bore.”
Theron’s stiffened countenance remained immovable. He continued to stare unblinkingly up into her eyes.
“We were disposed to like you very much when we first knew you,” Celia went on. “You impressed us as an innocent, simple, genuine young character, full of mother’s milk. It was like the smell of early spring in the country to come in contact with you. Your honesty of nature, your sincerity in that absurd religion of yours, your general naivete of mental and spiritual getup, all pleased us a great deal. We thought you were going to be a real acquisition.”
“Just a moment﻿—whom do you mean by ‘we’?” He asked the question calmly enough, but in a voice with an effect of distance in it.
“It may not be necessary to enter into that,” she replied. “Let me go on.

===Chinese===

“自那一天——林子里的那一天以后，我再没有见过你。我等着——等着——没有你的一点信号，全无见你的机会。想想这于我意味着什么！世上的一切都为我变了，连根拔起。我是一个新的人，跌进了一种新的存在。那一吻做下的就是这桩事。可在再见你之前，我无从知道我和我人生的这场巨变是福是祸——那一吻临到我，是祝福还是诅咒。这悬悬的等待在杀我，塞莉娅！所以，一听说你要来这儿，我就把一切都抛给风，跟着你来了。你为此责备我，我低头领受这责备。可你惩罚我惩罚得这样狠——在我认了错之后还要往下——把我的心切成一条条，用酷刑处死我——你担待得起么？”
“坐下，”塞莉娅说，嗓音里带着软化的倦意。他重新陷进椅子，她在他对面落座。“我不想给你不必要的痛苦；可你执意把自己塞进一个除了痛苦别无他物的境地。我警告过你走，你不肯。如今不论我把话说得怎样温存，你从这番解释里能得到的也只可能是苦。我还要说下去么？”
他俯首示意应允，没有再抬起来，只从一张苍白下垂的脸上，朝她仰起一蓬颓丧的凝望。
“全在一句话里，韦尔先生，”她接着说，声音很低，“请注意，我是为旁人也为自己说话——我们发觉，你是个惹人厌。”
塞隆那张僵住的脸纹丝不动。他继续一眨不眨地仰望着她的眼睛。
“我们初识你时，本是很愿意喜欢你的，”塞莉娅往下说，“你给我们的印象，是一个天真、单纯、实在的年轻性格，满身是母亲的乳汁。同你接触，像乡间早春的气息。你天性的诚实，你在你那套荒唐宗教里的虔诚，你精神头脑装束的整体的天真，都叫我们大大地喜欢。我们当你真是一桩难得的收获。”
“且慢——你说的‘我们’指谁？”这问题问得够平静，嗓音里却有一种辽远的效果。
“这一点也许不必细说，”她答，“让我说下去。

===Original===

But then it became apparent, little by little, that we had misjudged you. We liked you, as I have said, because you were unsophisticated and delightfully fresh and natural. Somehow we took it for granted you would stay so. But that is just what you didn’t do﻿—just what you hadn’t the sense to try to do. Instead, we found you inflating yourself with all sorts of egotisms and vanities. We found you presuming upon the friendships which had been mistakenly extended to you. Do you want instances? You went to Dr. Ledsmar’s house that very day after I had been with you to get a piano at Thurston’s, and tried to inveigle him into talking scandal about me. You came to me with tales about him. You went to Father Forbes, and sought to get him to gossip about us both. Neither of those men will ever ask you inside his house again. But that is only one part of it. Your whole mind became an unpleasant thing to contemplate. You thought it would amuse and impress us to hear you ridiculing and reviling the people of your church, whose money supports you, and making a mock of the things they believe in, and which you for your life wouldn’t dare let them know you didn’t believe in. You talked to us slightingly about your wife. What were you thinking of, not to comprehend that that would disgust us? You showed me once﻿—do you remember?﻿—a life of George Sand that you had just bought﻿—bought because you had just discovered that she had an unclean side to her life. You chuckled as you spoke to me about it, and you were for all the world like a little nasty boy, giggling over something dirty that older people had learned not to notice. These are merely random incidents. They are just samples, picked haphazard, of the things in you which have been opening our eyes, little by little, to our mistake. I can understand that all the while you really fancied that you were expanding, growing, in all directions. What you took to be improvement was degeneration. When you thought that you were impressing us most by your smart sayings and doings, you were reminding us most of the fable about the donkey trying to play lapdog. And it wasn’t even an honest, straightforward donkey at that!”
She uttered these last words sorrowfully, her hands clasped in her lap, and her eyes sinking to the floor. A silence ensued.

===Chinese===

“可随后渐渐地显明：我们看错了你。我们喜欢你，如我所说，因为你不通世故，新鲜可喜，出于自然。不知怎么，我们想当然地以为你会一直这样。可偏偏你没有——偏偏你连试着这样做的见识都没有。我们看见的，是你给自己充各种各样的自负和虚荣。我们看见你僭用那些错付给你的友谊。要例子么？我陪你到瑟斯顿买琴的当天，你就去了莱兹马医生家，想诱他说我的坏话。你又拿到我面前去说他。你去福布斯神父那里，想引他说咱们两人的闲话。那两位先生，谁也不会再请你进他的家门了。可这还只是一面。你那副头脑，整个成了叫人不堪注目的东西。你当我们听着你讥笑辱骂你教会的人——用他们的钱养活你的人——听着你嘲弄他们信的东西——而那些东西你自己是不信的，又打死不敢让他们知道你不信——会觉着有趣、觉着体面；你打的是这个主意。你同我们谈起你太太，口气轻蔑。你是怎么想的，竟不懂那会叫我们作呕？你有一回拿给我看——记得么？——一本你刚买的乔治·桑的传——买它，就因为你刚发现她生活里有不干净的一面。你同我谈起它时咯咯地笑，活脱一个小龌龊男孩，对着大人们早已学会不去理会的一件脏东西傻笑。这些不过是随手拈来的零星事例。都是些样品，随手捡的，取自你身上那些一点一点开着我们眼睛、叫我们认识错误的东西。我明白，这整个时间你当真自以为在扩张、在向四面八方生长。你当作长进的，是堕落。当你以为自己的妙语妙行给我们印象最深的时候，你叫我们最想起那则驴子学哈巴狗的寓言。而且那甚至还不是一头老实的、直来直去的驴子！”
末了这几句她说得很哀伤，双手交叠在膝上，眼睛沉向地板。随之是沉默。

===Original===
